\section*{IMPORTANT:}
This book is a compilation of all the problems published by College Board in AP Physics B and AP Physics C that were appropriate for the AP B level as well as problems from AAPT's Physics Bowl and U.S. Physics Team Qualifying Exams organized by topic.

\section*{DISCLAIMER}
The Multiple Choice Questions in this workbook have been compiled and modified from previous AP Physics B and C examinations and Physics Bowl exams. They are not meant to be representative of the new AP Physics courses.

The Free-Response Questions have not been edited and might not represent the topics covered nor the style of questions in the new exams.

\section*{PLEASE RESPECT YOUR FELLOW TEACHERS AND ABIDE BY}
 COPYRIGHT LAW BY NOT POSTING THIS WORKBOOK AND/OR SECTIONS OF THIS WORKBOOK ONLINEThe answers as presented are not the only method to solving many of these problems and physics teachers may present slightly different methods and/or different symbols and variables in each topic, but the underlying physics concepts are the same and we ask you read the solutions with an open mind and use these differences to expand your problem solving skills.

Finally, we are fallible and if you find any typographical errors, formatting errors or anything that strikes you as unclear or unreadable, please let us know so we can make the necessary announcements and corrections.

\begin{enumerate}
  \setcounter{enumi}{51}
  \item Two initially uncharged conductors, 1 and 2 , are mounted on insulating stands and are in contact, as shown above. A negatively charged rod is brought near but does not touch them. With the rod held in place, conductor 2 is moved to the right by pushing its stand, so that the conductors are separated. Which of the following is now true of conductor 2?\\
(A) It is uncharged.\\
(B) It is positively charged.\\
(C) It is negatively charged.\\
(D) It is charged, but its sign cannot be predicted.
\end{enumerate}

Questions 53-54\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-017(6)}\\
53. As shown above, two particles, each of charge $+Q$, are fixed at opposite corners of a square that lies in the plane of the page. A positive test charge $+q$ is placed at a third corner. What is the direction of the force on the test charge due to the two other charges?\\
(A)\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-017}\\
(B)\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-017(1)}\\
(C)\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-017(3)}\\
(D)\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-017(5)}\\
54. If F is the magnitude of the force on the test charge due to only one of the other charges, what is the magnitude of the net force acting on the test charge due to both of these charges?\\
(A) Zero\\
(B) $\frac{F}{\sqrt{2}}$\\
(D) $\sqrt{2} F$\\
(E) 2

Questions 55-56\\
Two charges are located on the line shown in the figure below, in which the charge at point I is +3 q and the charge at point III is +2 q. Point II is halfway between points I and III.\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-017(4)}\\
55. Other than at infinity, the electric field strength is zero at a point on the line in which of the following ranges?\\
(A) To the left of I\\
(B) Between I and II\\
(C) Between II and III\\
(D) To the right of III\\
56. The electric potential is negative at some points on the line in which of the following ranges?\\
(A) To the left of I\\
(B) Between I and II\\
(C) Between II and III\\
(D) None; this potential is never negative.\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-017(2)}\\
57. The graph above shows the electric potential V in a region of space as a function of position along the x -axis. At which point would a charged particle experience the force of greatest magnitude?\\
(A) A\\
(B) B\\
(C) D\\
(D) E\\
58. If the only force acting on an electron is due to a uniform electric field, the electron moves with constant\\
(A) acceleration in a direction opposite to that of the field\\
(B) acceleration in the direction of the field\\
(C) speed in a direction opposite to that of the field\\
(D) speed in the direction of the field\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-018(6)}\\
59. Two charged particles, each with a charge of +q , are located along the x -axis at $\mathrm{x}=2$ and $\mathrm{x}=4$, as shown above. Which of the following shows the graph of the magnitude of the electric field along the x -axis from the origin to $\mathrm{x}=6$ ?\\
(A)\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-018}\\
(B)\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-018(2)}\\
(C)\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-018(1)}\\
(D)\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-018(5)}\\
60. A positive electric charge is moved at a constant speed between two locations in an electric field, with no work done by or against the field at any time during the motion. This situation can occur only if the\\
(A) charge is moved in the direction of the field\\
(B) charge is moved opposite to the direction of the field\\
(C) charge is moved perpendicular to an equipotential line\\
(D) charge is moved along an equipotential line\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-018(3)}\\
61. The nonconducting hollow sphere of radius R shown above carries a large charge +Q , which is uniformly distributed on its surface. There is a small hole in the sphere. A small charge $+q$ is initially located at point $P$. a distance r from the center of the sphere. If $\mathrm{k}=1 / 4 \pi \varepsilon_{0}$, what is the work that must be done by an external agent in moving the charge +q from P through the hole to the center O of the sphere?\\
(A) $\mathrm{kqQ} / \mathrm{r}$\\
(B) $\mathrm{kqQ} / \mathrm{R}$\\
(C) $k q(Q-q) / r$\\
(D) $\mathrm{kqQ}(1 / \mathrm{R}-1 / \mathrm{r})$

Questions 62-63\\
A capacitor is constructed of two identical conducting plates parallel to each other and separated by a distance d. The capacitor is charged to a potential difference of $V_{0}$ by a battery, which is then disconnected.\\
62. If any edge effects are negligible, what is the magnitude of the electric field between the plates?\\
(A) $\mathrm{V}_{0} \mathrm{~d}$\\
(B) $\mathrm{V}_{0} / \mathrm{d}$\\
(C) $V_{0} / d^{2}$\\
(D) $\mathrm{V}_{0}{ }^{2} / \mathrm{d}$\\
63. A sheet of insulating plastic material is inserted between the plates without otherwise disturbing the system. What effect does this have on the capacitance?\\
(A) It causes the capacitance to increase.\\
(B) It causes the capacitance to decrease.\\
(C) None; the capacitance does not change.\\
(E) Nothing can be said about the effect without knowing the thickness of the sheet.\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-018(4)}\\
64. A point charge $+Q$ is inside an uncharged conducting spherical shell that in turn is near several isolated point charges, as shown above. The electric field at point P inside the shell depends on the magnitude of\\
(A) Q only\\
(B) the charge distribution on the sphere only\\
(C) Q and the charge distribution on the sphere\\
(D) all of the point charges\\
65. A potential difference $V$ is maintained between two large, parallel conducting plates. An electron starts from rest on the surface of one plate and accelerates toward the other. Its speed as it reaches the second plate is proportional to\\
(A) $\frac{1}{\sqrt{V}}$\\
(B) $\sqrt{V}$\\
(C) $V$\\
(D) $V^{2}$

Questions 66-67\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-019(3)}

Particles of charge Q and -4 Q are located on the x -axis as shown in the figure above. Assume the particles are isolated from all other charges.\\
66. Which of the following describes the direction of the electric field at point P ?\\
(A) +y\\
(B) -y\\
(C) Components in both the $-x$ and $+y$ directions\\
(D) Components in both the $+x$ and $-y$ directions\\
67. At which of the labeled points on the x -axis is the electric field zero?\\
(A) A\\
(B) B\\
(C) C\\
(D) E\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-019(5)}\\
68. A solid metallic sphere of radius $R$ has charge $Q$ uniformly distributed on its outer surface. A graph of electric potential $V$ as a function of position $r$ is shown above. Which of the following graphs best represents the magnitude of the electric field $E$ as a function of position $r$ for this sphere?\\
(A)\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-019}\\
(B)\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-019(4)}\\
(C)\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-019(2)}\\
(D)\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-019(1)}\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-020(2)}

As shown in the figure above, six particles, each with charge $+Q$, are held fixed and ate equally spaced around the circumference of a circle of radius $R$.\\
69. What is the magnitude of the resultant electric field at the center of the circle?\\
(A) 0\\
(B) $\frac{\sqrt{6}}{4 \pi \varepsilon_{0}} \frac{Q}{R^{2}}$\\
(C) $\frac{3 \sqrt{2}}{4 \pi \varepsilon_{0}} \frac{Q}{R^{2}}$\\
(D) $\frac{3}{2 \pi \varepsilon_{0}} \frac{Q}{R^{2}}$\\
70. With the six particles held fixed, how much work would be required to bring a seventh particle of charge $+Q$ from very far away and place it at the center of the circle?\\
(A) 0\\
(B) $\frac{\sqrt{6}}{4 \pi \varepsilon_{0}} \frac{Q}{R}$\\
(C) $\frac{3}{2 \pi \varepsilon_{0}} \frac{Q^{2}}{R^{2}}$\\
(D) $\frac{3}{2 \pi \varepsilon_{0}} \frac{Q^{2}}{R}$

Questions 71-73\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-020(4)}

The diagram above shows equipotential lines produced by an unknown charge distribution. A, B, C, D, and E are points in the plane.\\
71. Which vector below best describes the direction of the electric field at point A ?\\
(A)\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-020(3)}\\
(B)\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-020(5)}\\

(D)\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-020(1)}\\
72. At which point does the electric field have the greatest magnitude?\\
(A) A\\
(B) B\\
(C) C\\
(D) E\\
73. How much net work must be done by an external force to move $\mathrm{a}-1 \mu \mathrm{C}$ point charge from rest at point C to rest at point E ?\\
(A) $-20 \mu \mathrm{~J}$\\
(B) $-10 \mu \mathrm{~J}$\\
(C) $10 \mu \mathrm{~J}$\\
(D) $20 \mu \mathrm{~J}$\\
74. A physics problem starts: "A solid sphere has charge distributed uniformly throughout. . ." It may be correctly concluded that the\\
(A) electric field is zero everywhere inside the sphere\\
(B) electric potential on the surface of the sphere is not constant\\
(C) electric potential in the center of the sphere is zero\\
(D) sphere is not made of metal\\
75. A uniform spherical charge distribution has radius $R$.. Which of the following is true of the electric field strength due to this charge distribution at a distance r from the center of the charge?\\
(A) It is greatest when $r=0$.\\
(B) It is directly proportional to $r$ when $r>R$.\\
(C) It is directly proportional to $r$ when $r<R$.\\
(D) It is directly proportional to $r^{2}$.\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-021(2)}

I\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-021}

II\\
76. When a negatively charged rod is brought near, but does not touch, the initially uncharged electroscope shown above, the leaves spring apart (I). When the electroscope is then touched with a finger, the leaves collapse (II). When next the finger and finally the rod are removed, the leaves spring apart a second time (III). The charge on the leaves is\\
(A) positive in both I and III\\
(B) negative in both I and III\\
(C) positive in I, negative in III\\
(D) negative in I, positive in III\\
77. A positively charged conductor attracts a second object. Which of the following statements could be true?\\
I. The second object is a conductor with negative net charge.\\
II. The second object is a conductor with zero net charge.\\
III. The second object is an insulator with zero net charge..\\
(A) I only\\
(B) II only\\
(C) III only\\
(D) I, II \& III

Questions 78-79\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-021(3)}

A point charge of $+4.0 \mu \mathrm{C}$ is placed on the negative x -axis 0.20 m to the left of the origin, as shown in the accompanying figure. A second point charge $q$ is placed on the positive x -axis 0.30 m to the right of the origin.\\
78. If the net electric field at the origin is zero. What is $q$ ?\\
(A) $+9.0 \mu \mathrm{C}$\\
(B) $+6.0 \mu \mathrm{C}$\\
(C) $-6.0 \mu \mathrm{C}$\\
(D) $-9.0 \mu \mathrm{C}$\\
79. If the net electric potential at the origin is zero, what is $q$ ?\\
(A) $+9.0 \mu \mathrm{C}$\\
(B) $+6.0 \mu \mathrm{C}$\\
(C) $-6.0 \mu \mathrm{C}$\\
(D) $-9.0 \mu \mathrm{C}$\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-021(1)}\\
80. A small object with charge q and weight mg is attached to one end of a string of length L . The other end is attached to a stationary support. The system is placed in a uniform horizontal electric field E , as shown in the accompanying figure. In the presence of the field, the string makes a constant angle q with the vertical. What is the sign and magnitude of q ?\\
(A) positive with magnitude $\frac{m g}{E}$\\
(B) negative with magnitude $\frac{m g}{E}$\\
(C) positive with magnitude $\frac{m g}{E} \tan \theta$\\
(D) negative with magnitude $\frac{m g}{E} \tan \theta$\\
81. Two large parallel plates a distance $d$ apart are charged by connecting them to a battery of potential difference $V$. The battery is disconnected, and the plates are slowly moved apart. As the distance between plates increases:\\
(A) the charge on the plates decreases.\\
(B) the electric field intensity between the plates increases.\\
(C) the potential difference between the plates decreases.\\
(D) the potential difference between the plates increases.\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-022(1)}\\
82. In the figure above, equipotential lines are drawn at $0,20.0 \mathrm{~V}$, and 40.0 V . The total work done in moving a point charge of +3.00 mC from position a to position b is:\\
(A) 4.00 mJ\\
(B) 8.00 mJ\\
(C) 12.0 mJ\\
(D) 120 mJ\\
83. Two positive point charges repel each other with force 0.36 N when their separation is 1.5 m . What force do they exert on each other when their separation is 1.0 m ?\\
(A) 0.81 N\\
(B) 0.54 N\\
(C) 0.24 N\\
(D) 0.16 N\\
84. Two isolated conducting spheres ( $\mathrm{S}_{1}$ of radius 0.030 m and initial charge +6.0 nC and $\mathrm{S}_{2}$ of radius 0.040 m and initial charge +2.0 nC ) are connected by a conducting wire. Charge will flow in the wire until:\\
(A) both spheres are equally charged.\\
(B) the force of repulsion between the two spheres becomes equal.\\
(C) both spheres have the same surface charge density.\\
(D) both spheres are at the same potential.\\
85. A point charge $+q$ is placed midway between two point charges $+3 q$ and $-q$ separated by a distance $2 d$. If Coulomb's constant is k , the magnitude of the force on the charge +q is:\\
(A) $2 \frac{k q^{2}}{d^{2}}$\\
(B) $4 \frac{k q^{2}}{d^{2}}$\\
(C) $6 \frac{k q^{2}}{d^{2}}$\\
(D) $8 \frac{k q^{2}}{d^{2}}$\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-022}\\
86. A charged rod is placed between two insulated conducting spheres as shown. The spheres have no net charge. Region II has the same polarity as Region\\
(A) I only\\
(B) III only\\
(C) IV only\\
(D) I \& IV only\\
87. Two large oppositely charged insulated plates have a uniform electric field between them. The distance between the plates is increased. Which of the following statements is true?\\
I. The field strength decreases.\\
II. The field strength increases.\\
III. The potential difference between the plates increases.\\
(A) I only\\
(B) II only\\
(C) III only\\
(D) I and III only\\
88. When two charged point-like objects are separated by a distance $R$, the force between them is $F$. If the distance between them is quadrupled, the force between them is\\
(A) $16 F$\\
(B) $4 F$\\
(C) $F / 4$\\
(D) $F / 16$\\
89. An electroscope is given a positive charge, causing its foil leaves to separate. When an object is brought near the top plate of the electroscope, the foils separate even further. We could conclude\\
(A) that the object is positively charged.\\
(B) that the object is negatively charged.\\
(C) only that the object is charged.\\
(D) only that the object is uncharged.\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-023(1)}\\
90. Four positive point charges are arranged as shown in the accompanying diagram. The force between charges 1 and 3 is 6.0 N ; the force between charges 2 and 3 is 5.0 N ; and the force between charges 3 and 4 is 3.0 N . The magnitude of the total force on charge 3 is most nearly\\
(A) 6.3 N\\
(B) 8.0 N\\
(C) 11 N\\
(D) 14 N\\
91. Two isolated parallel plates are separated by a distance $d$. They carry opposite charges $Q$ and each has surface area $A$. Which of the following would increase the strength of the electric field between the plates?\\
I. Increasing $Q$\\
II. Increasing $A$\\
III. Increasing $d$\\
(A) I only\\
(B) II only\\
(C) III only\\
(D) II \& III only\\
92. When a positive electrically charged glass rod is brought near a neutral hollow metal sphere suspended by an insulating string, the sphere will be attracted to the rod because:\\
(A) the rod removes electron from the sphere\\
(B) the electric charge produces a magnetic field to attract the sphere\\
(C) the charge on the rod causes a separation of charge in the sphere\\
(D) some of the protons from the rod have been given to the sphere\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-023}\\
93. An alpha particle and a proton are placed equal distance between two large charged metal plates as shown. Which of the following would best describe the motion of the two particles if they were free to move?\\
(A) The alpha particle will travel upwards with twice the velocity of the proton.\\
(B) Both particles will travel upwards with the same velocity.\\
(C) Both particles will accelerate upwards with the same acceleration.\\
(D) The alpha particle will accelerate upwards with half the acceleration of the proton.\\
94. Two parallel metal plates carry opposite electrical charges each with a magnitude of $Q$. The plates are separated by a distance $d$ and each plate has an area $A$. Consider the following:\\
I. increasing $Q$\\
II. increasing $d$\\
III. increasing $A$

Which of the following would have the effect of reducing the potential difference between the plates?\\
(A) I only\\
(B) II only\\
(C) III only\\
(D) II and III\\
95. A positive point charge of $+q$ and a negative point charge of $-q$ are separated by a distance $d$. What would be the magnitude of the electric field midway between the two charges?\\
(A) $\mathrm{E}=\frac{k q}{d^{2}}$\\
(B) $\mathrm{E}=\frac{2 k q}{d^{2}}$\\
(C) $\mathrm{E}=\frac{4 k q}{d}$\\
(D) $\mathrm{E}=\frac{8 k q}{d^{2}}$\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-024}\\
96. A positive charge +Q located at the origin produces an electric field $\mathrm{E}_{0}$ at point $\mathrm{P}(\mathrm{x}=+1, \mathrm{y}=0)$. A negative charge -2 Q is placed at such a point as to produce a net field of zero at point P . The second charge will be placed on the\\
(A) $x$-axis where $x>1$\\
(B) x -axis where $\mathrm{x}<0$\\
(C) $y$-axis where $y>0$\\
(D) $y$-axis where $y<0$\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-024(1)}\\
97. A 300 eV electron is aimed midway between two parallel metal plates with a potential difference of 400 V . The electron is deflected upwards and strikes the upper plate as shown. What would be the kinetic energy of the electron just before striking the metal plate?\\
(A) 360 eV\\
(B) 400 eV\\
(C) 500 eV\\
(D) 700 eV\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-024(2)}\\
98. Two small hollow metal spheres hung on insulating threads attract one another as shown. It is known that a positively charged rod will attract ball A.\\
I. Ball A has a positive charge\\
II. Ball B has a negative charge\\
III. Ball A and Ball B have opposite charges

Which of the above can be correctly concluded about the charge on the balls?\\
(A) I only\\
(B) II only\\
(C) III only\\
(D) none of these\\
99. A $5 \times 10^{-6}$ coulomb electric charge is placed midway between two parallel metal plates connected to a 9 -volt battery. If the electric charge experiences a force of $1.5 \times 10^{-4}$ newtons, what is the separation of the metal plates?\\
(A) $6.75 \times 10^{-9} \mathrm{~m}$\\
(B) $2.7 \times 10^{-4} \mathrm{~m}$\\
(C) $3.7 \times 10^{-3} \mathrm{~m}$\\
(D) 0.30 m\\
100. A parallel-plate capacitor is connected with wires of negligible resistance to a battery having emf $\boldsymbol{\mathcal { E }}$ until the capacitor is fully charged. The battery is then disconnected from the circuit and the plates of the capacitor are moved to half of their original separation using insulated gloves. Let $\mathrm{V}_{\text {new }}$ be the potential difference across the capacitor plates when the plates are moved together. Let $\mathrm{V}_{\text {old }}$ be the potential difference across the capacitor plates when connected to the battery. $\frac{\mathrm{V}_{\text {new }}}{\mathrm{V}_{\text {old }}}=$\\
(A) $1 / 2$\\
(B) 1\\
(C) 2\\
(D) 4\\

Assuming the ground is a conductor, it can be shown that the induced charges can be treated as a charge of +30 C at a depth of 2 km below ground and a charge of -30 C at a depth of 3 km , as shown above right.\\
Consider point $\mathrm{P}_{1}$, which is just above the ground directly below the thundercloud, and point $\mathrm{P}_{2}$, which is 1 km horizontally away from $\mathrm{P}_{1}$.\\
a. Determine the direction and magnitude of the electric field at point $\mathrm{P}_{1}$.\\
b. i. On the diagram, clearly indicate the direction of the electric field at point $\mathrm{P}_{2}$\\
ii. How does the magnitude of the field at this point compare with the magnitude at point $\mathrm{P}_{1}$ ? Justify your answer:\\
$\qquad$ Greater $\qquad$ Equal $\qquad$ Less\\
c. Letting the zero of potential be at infinity, determine the potential at these points.\\
i. Point $P_{1}$\\
ii. Point $\mathrm{P}_{2}$\\
d. Determine the electric potential at an altitude of 1 km directly above point $\mathrm{P}_{1}$.\\
e. Determine the total electric potential energy of this arrangement of charges.\\

*2005E1. Consider the electric field diagram above.\\
a. Points $\mathrm{A}, \mathrm{B}$, and C are all located at $\mathrm{y}=0.06 \mathrm{~m}$.\\
i. At which of these three points is the magnitude of the electric field the greatest? Justify your answer.\\
ii. At which of these three points is the electric potential the greatest? Justify your answer.\\
b. An electron is released from rest at point B .\\
i. Qualitatively describe the electron's motion in terms of direction, speed, and acceleration.\\
ii. Calculate the electron's speed after it has moved through a potential difference of 10 V .\\
c. Points B and C are separated by a potential difference of 20 V . Estimate the magnitude of the electric field midway between them and state any assumptions that you make.\\
d. On the diagram, draw an equipotential line that passes through point D and intersects at least three electric field lines.\\

2006E1. The square of side a above contains a positive point charge +Q fixed at the lower left corner and negative point charges -Q fixed at the other three corners of the square. Point P is located at the center of the square.\\
a. On the diagram, indicate with an arrow the direction of the net electric field at point P .\\
b. Derive expressions for each of the following in terms of the given quantities and fundamental constants.\\
i. The magnitude of the electric field at point P\\
ii. The electric potential at point P\\
c. A positive charge is placed at point P . It is then moved from point P to point R , which is at the midpoint of the bottom side of the square. As the charge is moved, is the work done on it by the electric field positive, negative, or zero?\\
$\qquad$ Positive $\qquad$ Negative $\qquad$ Zero

Explain your reasoning.\\
d. i: Describe one way to replace a single charge in this configuration that would make the electric field at the center of the square equal to zero. Justify your answer.\\
ii. Describe one way to replace a single charge in this configuration such that the electric potential at the center of the square is zero but the electric field is not zero. Justify your answer.\\

2009E2 (modified) Electrons created at the filament at the left end of the tube represented above are accelerated through a voltage $\mathrm{V}_{0}$ and exit the tube. The electrons then move with constant speed to the right, as shown, before entering a region in which there is a uniform electric field between two parallel plates separated by a distance D . The electrons enter the field at point P , which is a distance $\mathrm{y}_{0}$ from the bottom plate, and are deflected toward that plate. Express your answers to the following in terms of $\mathrm{V}_{0}, \mathrm{D}, \mathrm{y}_{0}$, and fundamental constants.\\
a. Calculate the speed of the electrons as they exit the tube.\\
b. i. Calculate the magnitude of the electric field required to cause the electrons to land the distance $\mathrm{y}_{0}$ from the edge of the plate.\\
ii. Indicate the direction of the electric field.\\
$\qquad$ To the left $\qquad$ To the right\\
$\qquad$ Toward the top of the page $\qquad$ Toward the bottom of the page\\
$\qquad$ Into the page $\qquad$ Out of the page\\
Justify your answer.\\
c. Calculate the potential difference between the two plates required to produce the electric field determined in part b.

\section*{Solution}
\begin{enumerate}
  \item Since charge is free to move around on/in a conductor, excess charges will repel each other to the outer surface
  \item When the battery is disconnected, Q remains constant. Since C decreases when d increases and $\mathrm{Q}=\mathrm{CV}, \mathrm{V}$ will increase
  \item $\mathrm{W}=\mathrm{qV}$
  \item Since both charges are positive, the electric field vectors point in opposite directions at points between the two. At point A , the magnitudes of the electric field vectors are equal and therefore cancel out, making $\mathrm{E}=0$ at point A
  \item $\quad \mathrm{V}=\Sigma \mathrm{kQ} / \mathrm{r}$ and since both charges are positive, the largest potential is at the closest point to the two charges (it is more mathematically complex than that, but this reasoning works for the choices given)
  \item $\quad \mathrm{C}=\varepsilon_{0} \mathrm{~A} / \mathrm{d}$; if $\mathrm{A} \times 2, \mathrm{C} \times 2$ and if $\mathrm{d} \times 2, \mathrm{C} \div 2$ so the net effect is C is unchanged
  \item The net charge on the two spheres is +Q so when they touch and separate, the charge on each sphere (divided equally) is $1 / 2 \mathrm{Q} . \mathrm{F} \propto \mathrm{Q}_{1} \mathrm{Q}_{2}$ so before contact $\mathrm{F} \propto(2 \mathrm{Q})(\mathrm{Q})=2 \mathrm{Q}^{2}$ and after contact $\mathrm{F} \propto(1 / 2 \mathrm{Q})(1 / 2 \mathrm{Q})=1 / 4 \mathrm{Q}^{2}$ or $1 / 8$ of the original force
  \item $\mathrm{C}=\varepsilon_{0} \mathrm{~A} / \mathrm{d}$ and changing Q or V has no effect on the capacitance
  \item Inside the metal sphere $\mathrm{E}=0$. Once outside the sphere E decreases as you move away so the strongest field will be the closest point to the outside of the sphere
  \item Newton's third law
  \item Where E is zero must be closer to the smaller charge to make up for the weaker field. The vectors point in opposite directions when outside the two opposite charges. These two criteria eliminate 4 of the choices.
  \item Charges flow when there is a difference in potential. Analyzing the other choices: A is wrong because the charge resides on the surface. For $\mathrm{B}, \mathrm{E}=0$ in a charged conducting sphere. $\mathrm{E}=$ $\mathrm{kQ} / \mathrm{r}^{2}$ eliminates choice C . And for D , charge separation will occur, but the object will not acquire any charge.
  \item E is uniform between charged parallel plates therefore the force on a charge is also uniform between the plates
  \item $E \propto 1 / r^{2}$ so if $r \times 2, E \div 4$
  \item While the charges may separate, the forces on the opposite charges are in opposite directions, canceling out
  \item $\quad \mathrm{V}=\mathrm{kQ} / \mathrm{r}$ so the smaller sphere is at the lower potential (more negative $=$ lower) Negative charge flows from low to high potential so the charge will flow from the smaller sphere to the larger. The flow of charge ceases when there is no difference in potential.
  \item $E=V / d$ so if $V \times 2, E \times 2$ and if $d \div 5, E \times 5$ so the net effect is $E \times 10$
  \item The electric field vectors from the two charges point down and to the left (away from the charges) so the resultant field points down and left
  \item The potential energy of a particle at a location is the potential at that location times the charge. In this case, the potential is $\mathrm{kQ} / \mathrm{d}+\mathrm{kQ} / \mathrm{d}=(2 \mathrm{kQ} / \mathrm{d})$
  \item If the battery remains connected, the potential remains constant. C decreases as the separation increases soothe charge $\mathrm{Q}=\mathrm{CV}$ will also decrease
  \item In metals under electrostatic conditions, the electric field is zero everywhere inside.
  \item The electric field vector from the +Q charge points down and from the -Q charge points to the right so the resultant field points down and right
  \item The two vectors, each of magnitude $\mathrm{E}=\mathrm{kQ} / \mathrm{d}^{2}$, point at right angles to each other so the resultant field is $\sqrt{2} \mathrm{E}$
  \item The electric field between charged parallel plates is uniform, which means the potential changes uniformly with distance. For a change of 8 V over 4 cm means the change of potential with position (and the electric field strength) is $2 \mathrm{~V} / \mathrm{cm}$, which gives the potential 1 cm away from the 2 V plate as 4 V
  \item $\mathrm{W}=\Delta \mathrm{K}=\mathrm{QV}$ (mass doesn't have an effect on the kinetic energy, just on the speed in this case)
  \item The field lines point away from Y and toward Z making Y positive and Z negative.
  \item $\mathrm{C}=\varepsilon_{0} \mathrm{~A} / \mathrm{d} ; \varepsilon_{0}(2 \mathrm{~A}) /(2 \mathrm{~d})=\varepsilon_{0} \mathrm{~A} / \mathrm{d}$
  \item Charges arrange themselves on conductors so there is no electric field inside, and no electric field component along the surface
  \item By symmetry $\mathrm{V}_{\mathrm{R}}=\mathrm{V}_{\mathrm{S}}$ so $\Delta \mathrm{V}_{\mathrm{RS}}=0$ and $\mathrm{W}=\mathrm{q} \Delta \mathrm{V}$
  \item The force on the upper charge is to the left and twice the magnitude of the force on the bottom charge, which is to the right. This makes the net force to the left and the torque on the rod to be counterclockwise.
  \item Compared to the +Q charge at the center, the charge on the outer surface of the outer cylinder has twice the magnitude and is of opposite sign (so it is -2 Q ). There is also an equal and opposite charge induced on the inner surface of the outer cylinder making the total charge on the outer cylinder $-2 \mathrm{Q}+-\mathrm{Q}$
  \item Since the capacitor is isolated, Q remains constant. Filling the place with oil (a dielectric) will increase the capacitance, causing the potential ( $\mathrm{V}=\mathrm{Q} / \mathrm{C}$ ) to decrease.
  \item $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{E}} \propto \mathrm{q}_{1} \mathrm{q}_{2} / \mathrm{r}^{2}$; if $\mathrm{q}_{1}$ and $\mathrm{q}_{2} \times 2 ; \mathrm{F} \times 4$ and if $\mathrm{r} \div 2, \mathrm{~F} \times 4$ making the net effect $\mathrm{F} \times 4 \times 4$
  \item Since there is no component of the electric field along a conducting surface under electrostatic conditions, no work is done moving the charge around the surface, meaning no differences in potential
  \item Regardless of velocity, the force on a charge in an electric field is parallel to the field ( $\mathbf{F}=\mathrm{qE}$ )
  \item $\mathrm{W}=\mathrm{Fd}=\mathrm{qEd}$
  \item E points away from + charges and toward - charges. Use symmetry.
  \item D is not symmetric so the field will not point at the midpoint of any side. The field in choice B points at the bottom charge.
  \item Since the capacitor is isolated, Q remains constant. Filling the place with oil (a dielectric) will increase the capacitance, causing the potential ( $\mathrm{V}=\mathrm{Q} / \mathrm{C}$ ) to decrease.
  \item $\quad \mathrm{V}=\mathrm{kQ} / \mathrm{r}$ so the smaller sphere ( Y ) is at the higher potential. Negative charge flows from low to high potential so the charge will flow from X to Y .
  \item Once inside a uniform sphere of charge, the electric field is zero. Since $\mathrm{E}=0$ the potential does not change within the sphere (meaning it is the same value as the surface)
  \item Outside a uniform sphere of charge, it behaves as a point charge.
  \item $\quad \mathbf{E}=\mathbf{F} / \mathrm{q}$. The vector nature of the equation allows one to find the direction of F and the equation itself allows one to find the ratio $\mathrm{F} / \mathrm{q}$, but not q specifically
  \item Outside a uniform sphere of charge, it behaves as a point charge. $E=k Q / r^{2}$
  \item By symmetry, the force on an electron at the center from the top half will be straight down and the force from the bottom half will also be straight down
  \item E is a vector so all the individual E field vectors from the four charges will cancel. V is a scalar and will add since they are all positive charges.
  \item The points where $\mathrm{V}=0$ must lie closer to the smaller charge. Unlike electric field vectors which also require the individual vectors point in opposite directions, there are a locus of points (in this case in a ring surrounding the +Q charge) where $\mathrm{V}=0$ as the two charges are opposite in sign and V is a scalar. So the other point on the x axis is between the two charges, but closer to the +Q charge. This must be a value between 1.5 D and 2 D
  \item When inside a uniform shell of charge, there is an electric field due to the shell. When outside a uniform shell of charge, the electric field is as if the shell was a point charge.
  \item The potential difference between the plates is 4 V and the right side is the positive plate. We need the batteries pointing in the same direction with the positive terminal on the right.
  \item The electron experiences a force toward the positive plate of magnitude $\mathrm{F}=\mathrm{Eq} . \mathrm{E}=\mathrm{V} / \mathrm{d}$ and cannot be calculated without knowing d .
  \item While spheres X and Y are in contact, electrons will repel away from the rod out of sphere X into sphere Y.
  \item While spheres 1 and 2 are in contact, electrons will repel away from the rod out of sphere 1 into sphere 2.
  \item The force vectors from the two +Q charges point down and to the left (away from the charges) so the resultant force points down and left
  \item The two vectors, each of magnitude F , point at right angles to each other so the resultant field is $\sqrt{2} \mathrm{~F}$
  \item Where E is zero must be closer to the smaller charge to make up for the weaker field. The vectors point in opposite directions when between the two like charges. These two criteria eliminate 4 of the choices
  \item Since both charges are positive and V is a scalar equal to $\Sigma \mathrm{kQ} / \mathrm{r}$, the potential will never be zero in the vicinity of these two charges.
  \item The electric field (and hence, the electric force on a charge) is greatest where the potential changes most rapidly with position (the greatest gradient) since $\mathrm{E}=\mathrm{V} / \mathrm{d}$. On this graph, this would be the point where the slope is the greatest
  \item If F is constant and $\mathrm{F}=\mathrm{ma}$, the acceleration is also constant. Negative charges experience forces opposite in direction to electric field lines.
  \item By symmetry, $\mathrm{E}=0$ at the midpoint and goes to infinity near each charge $\left(\mathrm{E}=\mathrm{kQ} / \mathrm{r}^{2}\right)$
  \item If no work is done by the field and there is a field present, the motion must be perpendicular to the field, along an equipotential line, making the force perpendicular to the displacement of the charge (a requirement for zero work). Along an equipotential line, $\Delta \mathrm{V}=0$ and $\mathrm{W}=\mathrm{q} \Delta \mathrm{V}$.
  \item Inside the sphere, $\mathrm{E}=0$ which means the potential does not change with position and is the same value as the surface, which is $\mathrm{kQ} / \mathrm{R}$. At point P , the potential is $\mathrm{kQ} / \mathrm{r} . \mathrm{W}=\mathrm{q} \Delta \mathrm{V}=\mathrm{q}(\mathrm{kQ} / \mathrm{R}-\mathrm{kQ} / \mathrm{r})$
  \item $\quad \mathrm{E}=\mathrm{V} / \mathrm{d}$
  \item If the battery is disconnected, Q remains constant. If a dielectric is inserted between the plates, the capacitance increases and since $\mathrm{Q}=\mathrm{CV}$, the potential difference decreases.
  \item Conductors under electrostatic conditions will arrange their changes so no electric field exists inside (other than those created by charges placed inside the empty cavity). Fields from external charges will not penetrate into conducting enclosures.
  \item $\mathrm{W}=\mathrm{K}=\mathrm{q} \Delta \mathrm{V}$ and $\mathrm{K}=1 / 2 \mathrm{mv}^{2}$
  \item At point P , the field due to charge Q points up and to the right and the field due to charge -4 Q is larger in magnitude and points down and to the right. Due to the asymmetry, no components will cancel.
  \item Where E is zero must be closer to the smaller charge to make up for the weaker field. The vectors point in opposite directions outside the two opposite charges. These two criteria eliminate 3 of the choices. For the magnitudes of the electric fields to be zero the ratios $\mathrm{Q} / \mathrm{r}^{2}$ must be equal giving (in units along the $x$ axis) $Q / r^{2}=4 Q /(r+4 \text { units })^{2}$ giving $r=4$ units
  \item Since $\mathrm{E}=\Delta \mathrm{V} / \mathrm{d}, \mathrm{E}$ represents the slope of the line on the graph which could be choice C or D . since $V \propto 1 / r$ the slope is proportional to $\Delta V / r=(1 / r) / r=1 / r^{2}$ which is choice $C$
  \item By symmetry, all the vectors cancel
  \item $\mathrm{W}=\mathrm{q} \Delta \mathrm{V}=+\mathrm{Q}\left(\mathrm{V}_{\text {center }}-\mathrm{V}_{\infty}\right)=+\mathrm{QV}_{\text {center }}$ where $\mathrm{V}_{\text {center }}=\Sigma \mathrm{V}=\Sigma \mathrm{kQ} / \mathrm{r}=6 \mathrm{kQ} / \mathrm{R}$
  \item E points from high potential to low potential, perpendicular to equipotential lines (the direction of the force on a positive charge)
  \item E is greatest in magnitude where V changes most rapidly with position (the largest gradient) which is where the lines are closest together.
  \item $\Delta \mathrm{V}_{\mathrm{CE}}=\mathrm{V}_{\mathrm{E}}-\mathrm{V}_{\mathrm{C}}=10 \mathrm{~V}$. The amount of work, $\mathrm{W}=\mathrm{q} \Delta \mathrm{V}=1 \mu \mathrm{C} \times 10 \mathrm{~V}=10 \mu \mathrm{C}$. Since the external force must push against the negative charge to keep it from accelerating and bring it to rest at point E , the work done by the external force must be negative.
  \item For charge to be distributed throughout a material, it must be non-conducting
  \item Like gravity inside a uniform sphere of mass, the field is directly proportional to $r$ when inside the sphere (and proportional to $1 / \mathrm{r}^{2}$ when outside)
  \item In I, charge separation occurs (negative charges repel to the leaves). The whole process describes charging by induction, where the electrons leave the electroscope to ground (the finger) and once contact with ground is broken, the electroscope is left with a positive charge (III)
  \item Charged objects attract object with an opposite charge, but also neutral objects by separation of charges.
  \item If $\mathrm{E}=0$, the field vectors point in opposite directions, making q positive. In magnitude we can find q by $(+4 \mu \mathrm{C}) /(0.2 \mathrm{~m})^{2}=\mathrm{q} /(0.3 \mathrm{~m})^{2}$
  \item If $\mathrm{V}=0$ and $\mathrm{V}=\Sigma \mathrm{kQ} / \mathrm{r}$ then q must be negative and $(+4 \mu \mathrm{C}) /(0.2 \mathrm{~m})=\mathrm{q} /(0.3 \mathrm{~m})$
  \item Since the electrostatic force pushes the charge to the right, with the field line, it is a positive charge. $\Sigma \mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{y}}=0$ gives $\mathrm{T} \cos \theta=\mathrm{mg}$ and $\Sigma \mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{x}}=0$ gives $\mathrm{T} \sin \theta=\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{E}}=\mathrm{qE}$. Divide the two expressions to eliminate T .
  \item If the battery is disconnected, the charge on the plates remains constant. If the separation increases, $C$ decreases $\left(C=\varepsilon_{0} A / d\right)$. Since $Q=C V, V$ must increase.
  \item $W=q \Delta V$ (motion along an equipotential line requires no work so only $\Delta V$ matters, not the path)
  \item $\mathrm{F} \propto 1 / \mathrm{r}^{2}$
  \item Charges flow when there is a difference in potential.
  \item The distance between the $+q$ charge and each charge is $d$. The force on the $+q$ charge from each charge is in the same direction, making the net force $\mathrm{kq}^{2} / \mathrm{d}^{2}+\mathrm{k}\left(3 \mathrm{q}^{2}\right) / \mathrm{d}^{2}$
  \item The rod will attract the same charge from each sphere to the side closer to the rod.
  \item Since the plates are insulated, the charge remains constant. If the distance is increased, the capacitance will decrease ( $\mathrm{C} \propto \mathrm{A} / \mathrm{d}$ ) and since $\mathrm{Q}=\mathrm{CV}$, the potential difference must increase by the same factor that the distance increases. This means $\mathrm{E}=\mathrm{V} / \mathrm{d}$ remains the same.
  \item $\mathrm{F} \propto 1 / \mathrm{r}^{2} ;$ if $\mathrm{r} \times 4, \mathrm{~F} \div 16$
  \item If the leaves are positive, further separation means they are becoming more positive, which implies electrons are leaving the leaves, attracted to the top plate of the electroscope. This will occur if the object is positively charged.
  \item Vector addition. Since all the charges are positive, the forces due to charges 2 and 4 point in opposite directions, making the magnitude of the net force along the x axis 2 N . Combine this with a net force along the y axis of 6 N using the Pythagorean theorem
  \item $\mathrm{Q}=\mathrm{CV}$ and $\mathrm{V}=\mathrm{Ed}$ and using $\mathrm{C}=\varepsilon_{0} \mathrm{~A} / \mathrm{d}$ gives $\mathrm{E}=\mathrm{Q} / \varepsilon_{0} \mathrm{~A}$
  \item Charged objects attract neutral objects by separating the charges within the neutral object.
  \item An alpha particle has twice the charge and four times the mass of a proton. Twice the charge means twice the electric force. This, combined with four times the mass gives half the acceleration.
  \item $\mathrm{Q}=\mathrm{CV}$ and $\mathrm{C}=\varepsilon_{0} \mathrm{~A} / \mathrm{d}$ which gives $\mathrm{V}=\mathrm{Qd} / \varepsilon_{0} \mathrm{~A}$
  \item At a point midway between the charges $\mathrm{E}=\mathrm{kq} /(\mathrm{d} / 2)^{2}$ from each charge. Since they are opposite charges, the field vectors between the charges point in the same direction.
  \item For the E field vectors to point in opposite directions, point P must lie outside the two charges. For the magnitudes of E due to each charge to cancel, Point P must be closer to the smaller charge.
  \item The extra kinetic energy gained by the electron is $\mathrm{W}=\mathrm{K}=\mathrm{q} \Delta \mathrm{V}$, where $\Delta \mathrm{V}$ is the potential difference between the midway line and the upper plate, which is 200 V . This makes the additional kinetic energy 200 eV . Kinetic energy is a scalar so the total KE of the electron is now $300 \mathrm{eV}+200 \mathrm{eV}$
  \item If a positive rod attracts ball A , it is either negative or neutral. For ball B to also attract ball A means ball B can be charged positive or negative (if ball A is neutral) or neutral (if ball A is positive)
  \item $\mathrm{F}=\mathrm{Eq}$ and $\mathrm{E}=\mathrm{V} / \mathrm{d}$ giving $\mathrm{d}=\mathrm{qV} / \mathrm{F}$
  \item Since the battery is removed, the charge remains constant. If the distance is decreased, the capacitance will increase ( $\mathrm{C} \propto \mathrm{A} / \mathrm{d}$ ) and since $\mathrm{Q}=\mathrm{CV}$, the potential difference must decrease by the same factor that the distance decreases.
  \item Since the spherical shell is conducting, a charge of -Q is induced on the inner surface. This gives a charge of $+Q$ on the outer surface since the spherical shell is neutral. As $E=0$ inside the conducting shell, the potential inside is constant and the same as on the surface, which is $\mathrm{kQ} / \mathrm{r}$
  \item $\mathrm{F} \propto 1 / \mathrm{r}^{2} ;$ if $\mathrm{r} \times 0.4$ then $\mathrm{F} \div 0.4^{2}$
  \item The force vectors from each charge and their relative magnitude are drawn below\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-062}
  \item Using $\mathrm{F}=\mathrm{ma}=\mathrm{qE}$ and $\mathrm{E}=\mathrm{V} / \mathrm{d}$ gives $\mathrm{a}=\mathrm{qV} / \mathrm{md}$
  \item Since the spherical shell is conducting, a charge of -Q is induced on the inner surface. This gives a charge of $+Q$ on the outer surface since the spherical shell is neutral and the field outside the shell is as if the shell was a point charge.
  \item The process described is charging by induction which gives the electroscope in this case a net negative charge. Bringing a negative charge near the top of the electroscope will cause electrons to repel to the leaves. Since the leaves are already negative, this will cause them to separate further.
  \item The charge density is $\mathrm{Q} /$ area which is $\mathrm{Q} / 4 \pi \mathrm{r}^{2}$ so for the inner surface it is $\mathrm{Q}_{\text {inner }} / 4 \pi \mathrm{a}^{2}$ and for the outer surface it is $\mathrm{Q}_{\text {outer }} / 16 \pi \mathrm{a}^{2}$. For these to be equal $\mathrm{Q}_{\text {outer }}$ must be $4 \mathrm{Q}_{\text {inner }}$. Because of the +Q charge inside, there is a charge of -Q induced on the inner surface, which means the outer surface must have charge -4 Q . Thus the total charge on the shell is -5 Q
  \item The force on a positive charge is in the direction of the electric field at that location.
  \item Once inside a uniform sphere of charge, the electric field is zero. Since $\mathrm{E}=0$ the potential does not change within the sphere (meaning it is the same value as the surface). $\mathrm{V} \propto 1 / \mathrm{r}$ outside the sphere.
  \item Negative potential energy means the system is bound. This means energy input is required to break the system apart.
  \item $\mathrm{F} \propto 1 / \mathrm{r}^{2}$
  \item Newton's third law requires the forces be equal and opposite. This eliminates choices A, B and C. Since they both positive, the force is repulsive.
  \item $\mathrm{q}_{2} / \mathrm{q}_{1}=$ lines on $\mathrm{q}_{2} /$ lines on $\mathrm{q}_{1}$ and since the lines point toward $\mathrm{q}_{2}$ and away from $\mathrm{q}_{1}$ they are oppositely charged, making the ratio negative.
  \item The points where $\mathrm{V}=0$ must lie closer to the smaller charge. Unlike electric field vectors which also require the individual vectors point in opposite directions, there are a locus of points (in this case in a ring surrounding the -Q charge) where $\mathrm{V}=0$ as the two charges are opposite in sign and V is a scalar.
  \item For E to be zero, the electric field vectors from each charge must point in opposite directions and must therefore occur at a point outside the charges. For the electric field vectors from each charge to be equal in magnitude so they can cancel, it must also occur at a point closer to the smaller charge to make up for the weaker field.
  \item Since the battery remains connected, V remains constant. C decreases as d increases ( $\mathrm{C} \propto 1 / \mathrm{d}$ ) and $\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{C}}=1 / 2 \mathrm{CV}^{2}$
  \item Only electrons are transferred in static charging processes.
  \item Any charge will experience a net force of zero where the electric field is zero. This must be where the fields from each charge point in opposite directions and also closer to the smaller charge, which is to the left of the $+Q$ charge (the answer will be to the left of -1 m ). Let the distances to the $+Q$ and the $-2 Q$ charge be $x$ and $(X+2)$, respectively. This gives $E_{1}=E_{2}$ and $\mathrm{kQ} / \mathrm{x}^{2}=\mathrm{k}(2 \mathrm{Q}) /(\mathrm{x}+2)^{2}$. Solve for x and add the extra 1 m to the origin.
  \item Since the battery is removed, the charge remains constant. If the distance is increased, the capacitance will decrease ( $\mathrm{C} \propto \mathrm{A} / \mathrm{d}$ ) and since $\mathrm{Q}=\mathrm{CV}$, the potential difference must increase by the same factor that the distance increases and $\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{C}}=1 / 2 \mathrm{QV}$
  \item $F \propto q_{1} q_{2} / r^{2}$; the original force $F \propto 100 Q^{2} / d^{2}$. The new charges are $15 Q$ and $5 Q$ making the new force $\mathrm{F} \propto 75 \mathrm{Q}^{2} /(2 \mathrm{~d})^{2}=19 \mathrm{Q}^{2} / \mathrm{d}^{2}$
  \item When one sphere is touched, the charged divides equally ( $1 / 2 \mathrm{Q}$ each). When this sphere is then touched to the second sphere, the net charge ( $3 / 2 \mathrm{Q}$ ) is divided equally ( $3 / 4 \mathrm{Q}$ each). Since $\mathrm{F} \propto$ $\mathrm{q}_{1} \mathrm{q}_{2}$, the original force is proportional to $\mathrm{Q}^{2}$ and the new force is then proportional to $(1 / 2 \mathrm{Q})(3 / 4 \mathrm{Q})$ $=3 / 8 \mathrm{Q}^{2}$
  \item $W=K=q \Delta V$ so $\Delta V \propto v^{2}$ and for $v$ to double, $\Delta V$ must increase by 4
  \item Adding the force vectors shown (each 15 N ) with $x$ components that cancel and $y$ components that equal $15 \mathrm{~N} \cos 30^{\circ}$ gives $\mathrm{F}=2 \times 15 \mathrm{~N} \cos 30^{\circ}=26 \mathrm{~N}$\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-063}
  \item Once outside the spheres, they act as point charges and their difference in size is irrelevant
  \item When connected, the potentials become equal. This gives $k Q_{X} / r_{X}=k Q_{Y} / r_{Y}$ and since $E=k Q / r^{2}$, dividing the potentials by their respective radii gives $\mathrm{kQ}_{\mathrm{X}} /\left(\mathrm{r}_{\mathrm{X}}\right)^{2}<\mathrm{kQ}_{\mathrm{Y}} /\left(\mathrm{r}_{\mathrm{Y}}\right)^{2}$
  \item $1.5 \times 10^{12}$ excess electrons is a charge of magnitude $\left(1.5 \times 10^{12}\right) \times\left(1.6 \times 10^{-19}\right)=2.4 \times 10^{-7} \mathrm{C}$. Use $\mathrm{Q}=\mathrm{CV}$
  \item The field is zero everywhere inside a metal sphere.
  \item The force on $\mathrm{Q}_{2}$ from $\mathrm{Q}_{1}$ points downward and the force from $\mathrm{Q}_{3}$ points at right angles to the left. Compute each force using $\mathrm{F}=\mathrm{kq}_{1} \mathrm{q}_{2} / \mathrm{r}^{2}$ and use the Pythagorean theorem.
  \item The field at the center due to $\mathrm{Q}_{1}$ and $\mathrm{Q}_{3}$ cancels. The only contribution to the field then is that due to $\mathrm{Q}_{2} . \mathrm{E}=\mathrm{kQ} / \mathrm{r}^{2}$ where $\mathrm{r}^{2}=0.3^{2}+0.4^{2}$
  \item E inside $=0$ and outside $\mathrm{E} \propto 1 / \mathrm{r}^{2}$
  \item Advanced question (not exactly in the B curriculum, but interesting). Like gravity inside a Initially, when $B$ is removed, $A$ and $C$ are equally and oppositely charged and $B$ is neutral. Touching B to A gives B $1 / 2$ the charge of A (split equally). The charge on B is then $1 / 2$ that of C and oppositely charged. When B and C touch, the total charge between them is $1 / 2$ the charge of $C$ and the same sign as $C$. Each sphere then has $1 / 4$ of the charge of $C$ after contact is made. This makes the end result that the charge on sphere $B$ is $1 / 4$ the original charge of $A$ and the same sign as sphere C , which is opposite that of A uniform sphere of mass, the field is directly proportional to $r$ when inside the sphere (and proportional to $1 / \mathrm{r}^{2}$ when outside)
  \item The field due to the two $+3 q$ charges cancel. The $-q$ in the upper right counters $-q$ from the lower left, leaving the net contribution to the field $\mathrm{a}-\mathrm{q}$ from the lower left.
  \item With equal charge, the forces are the same. The potential energy of the charges is equal in magnitude, but positive for the proton and negative for the electron. For scalars, positive numbers are higher than negative numbers.
  \item The charge $Q$ in the middle will induce a charge $-Q$ on the inner surface of the shell. For the net charge of the shell to be -q , the outer surface must have the rest of the charge such that $\mathrm{q}_{\text {outer }}+$ $\mathrm{q}_{\text {inner }}=-\mathrm{q}$ so $\mathrm{q}_{\text {outer }}=-\mathrm{q}-\mathrm{q}_{\text {inner }}=-\mathrm{q}-(-\mathrm{Q})=\mathrm{Q}-\mathrm{q}$
  \item The potential inside the shell is the same as the potential at the surface of the shell since $E=0$ inside the shell. $V=\mathrm{kq}_{\text {outer }} / \mathrm{b}$
  \item Charges flow when there is a difference in potential.
  \item $\mathrm{V}=\Sigma \mathrm{kQ} / \mathrm{r}$
  \item The electric field cancels from symmetry all but $+Q$ remaining in the upper right corner and $E=$ $\mathrm{kQ} / \mathrm{r}^{2}$
  \item If the E fields are the same, that means $\mathrm{kQ}_{\mathrm{X}} / \mathrm{r}_{\mathrm{X}}{ }^{2}=\mathrm{kQ}_{\mathrm{Y}} / \mathrm{r}_{\mathrm{Y}}{ }^{2}$, or $\mathrm{Q}_{\mathrm{X}} / \mathrm{Q}_{\mathrm{Y}}=\mathrm{r}_{\mathrm{X}}{ }^{2} / \mathrm{r}_{\mathrm{Y}}{ }^{2}$
  \item $\mathrm{U}_{\mathrm{E}} \propto 1 / \mathrm{r}$
  \item $\quad \Sigma \mathrm{F}=0$ so we have $\mathrm{T}+\mathrm{k}(\mathrm{q})(\mathrm{q}) / \mathrm{d}^{2}-\mathrm{Mg}=0$ giving $\mathrm{T}=\mathrm{Mg}-\mathrm{kq}^{2} / \mathrm{d}^{2}$
  \item When lowered inside, the charged sphere induces a negative charge on the inner surface of the cup. The outer surface remains neutral since it is grounded. When the grounding wire is removed, the cup has a net negative charge, which when the sphere is removed, will move to the outer surface of the cup.
  \item $\mathrm{K}=\mathrm{q} \Delta \mathrm{V}$ so $\mathrm{K}_{1} / \mathrm{K}_{2}=\mathrm{q}_{1} / \mathrm{q}_{2}$
  \item The field is zero where the fields from each charge point in opposite directions and also closer to the smaller charge, which is to the left of the -Q charge
  \item $\mathrm{E}=\Delta \mathrm{V} / \mathrm{d}$
  \item E fields point from high potential to low potential, perpendicular to the equipotential lines.
  \item Combining two droplets doubles the charge. The volume is doubled, which means the radius is multiplied by $\sqrt[3]{2}$. This gives $\mathrm{V}=\mathrm{kQ} / \mathrm{r}=\mathrm{k}(2 \mathrm{Q}) /(\sqrt[3]{2} \mathrm{r})$
  \item The work to assemble the charges is the potential energy of the system, which is the sum of the potential energies of each pair of charges $U_{E}=-\mathrm{ke}^{2} / \mathrm{a}-\mathrm{ke}^{2} / \mathrm{a}+\mathrm{ke}^{2} / 2 \mathrm{a}$
\end{enumerate}

a. The x components of the forces due to the charges $\mathrm{q}_{\mathrm{B}}$ cancel making the net force equal to the sum of the y components which are equal in magnitude and both point downward. The distance between $\mathrm{q}_{\mathrm{A}}$ and either $\mathrm{q}_{\mathrm{B}}$ is found by the Pythagorean theorem to be $0.05 \mathrm{~m} . \mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{y}}=\mathrm{F} \sin \theta$ where $\theta$ is the angle between the line joining $\mathrm{q}_{\mathrm{A}}$ and $\mathrm{q}_{\mathrm{B}}$ and the x axis, giving $\sin \theta=3 / 5$.\\
This gives $\mathrm{F}_{\text {net }}=2 \times \mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{y}}=2\left(\mathrm{kq}_{\mathrm{A}} \mathrm{q}_{\mathrm{B}} / \mathrm{r}^{2}\right) \times \sin \theta=2.6 \times 10^{-7} \mathrm{~N}$ down ( -y )\\
b. Particle A will accelerate downward, but as the particle approaches the origin, the force and the acceleration will decrease to zero at the origin. It will then pass through the origin, with a net force now pointing upward, where it will eventually slow down and reverse direction, repeating the process. The short answer is the particle will oscillate vertically about the origin.

d. With the copper inserted, we have two capacitors in series, each with a spacing $1 / 2(b-a)$. The capacitance of each is then $\varepsilon_{0} \mathrm{~A} /(1 / 2(\mathrm{~b}-\mathrm{a}))$ and in series, two equal capacitors have an equivalent capacitance of $1 / 2 \mathrm{C}$ makinf the total capacitance with the copper inserted $1 / 2 \varepsilon_{0} A /(1 / 2(b-a))=\varepsilon_{0} A /(b-a)$ making the ratio $b /(b-a)$. Notice the final capacitance is effectively a new single capacitor with an air gap of ( $b-a$ ). Imagine sliding the copper slab up to touch the top plate, this is the same result. This is why adding capacitors in series decreases the capacitance as if the gap between the plates was increased.

The uncharged capacitor is connected in series with a resistor $\mathrm{R}=2 \times 10^{6}$ ohms, a 30 -volt battery, and an open switch S , as shown above. The switch is then closed.\\
b. What is the initial charging current when the switch S is closed?\\
c. Determine the magnitude and sign of the final charge on the bottom plate of the fully charged capacitor.\\
d. How much electrical energy is stored in the fully charged capacitor?

After the capacitor is fully charged, it is carefully disconnected, leaving the charged capacitor isolated in space. The plastic sheet is then removed from between the metal plates. The metal plates retain their original separation of 1.0 millimeter.\\
e. What is the new voltage across the plates?\\
f. If there is now more energy stored in the capacitor, where did it come from? If there is now less energy, what happened to it?\\

Capacitor 1 is initially charged to a voltage $\mathrm{V}_{0}=50 \mathrm{~V}$ and capacitor 2 is initially uncharged. Both of the switches S are then closed at time $\mathrm{t}=0$.\\

b. Specify the sign of the charge on each plate.

The capacitor above is then connected electrically through a resistor to a second parallel-plate capacitor, initially uncharged, whose plates have the same area A but a separation of only $\mathrm{s} / 2$.\\
c. Indicate on the diagram below the direction of the current in each wire, and explain why the current will eventually cease.\\

\section*{Solution}
\begin{enumerate}
  \item The resistances are as follows: I: $2 \Omega$, II: $4 \Omega$, III: $1 \Omega$, IV: $2 \Omega$
  \item The total resistance of the $3 \Omega$ and $6 \Omega$ in parallel is $2 \Omega$ making the total circuit resistance $6 \Omega$ and the total current $\mathcal{E} / \mathrm{R}=1 \mathrm{~A}$. This 1 A will divide in the ratio of 2:1 through the $3 \Omega$ and $6 \Omega$ respectively so the $3 \Omega$ resistor receives $2 / 3$ A making the potential difference $\operatorname{IR}=(2 / 3 \mathrm{~A})(3 \Omega)$ $=2 \mathrm{~V}$.
  \item The voltage across each branch does not change (the battery is a constant voltage device and energy is conserved on each loop), neither does the current through each branch. The ammeter measures the current feeding both branches, according to the junction rule.
  \item The battery is a constant voltage device and energy is conserved on each loop.
  \item $\quad R=\rho L / A$. Greatest resistance is the longest, narrowest resistor.
  \item In parallel $\mathrm{V}_{1}=\mathrm{V}_{2} . \mathrm{Q}_{1}=\mathrm{C}_{1} \mathrm{~V}_{1}$ and $\mathrm{Q}_{2}=\mathrm{C}_{2} \mathrm{~V}_{2}$ so $\mathrm{Q}_{1} / \mathrm{Q}_{2}=\mathrm{C}_{1} / \mathrm{C}_{2}=1.5$
  \item For steady power dissipation, the circuit must allow current to slow indefinitely. For the greatest power, the total resistance should be the smallest value. These criteria are met with the resistors in parallel.
  \item To retain energy, there must be a capacitor that will not discharge through a resistor. Capacitors in circuits C and E will discharge through the resistors in parallel with them.
  \item $\mathrm{P}=\mathrm{I} \boldsymbol{\varepsilon}$
  \item The resistance of the two resistors in parallel is $\mathrm{r} / 2$. The total circuit resistance is then $10 \Omega+1 / 2$ r , which is equivalent to $\mathcal{E} / \mathrm{I}=(10 \mathrm{~V}) /(0.5 \mathrm{~A})=20 \Omega=10 \Omega+\mathrm{r} / 2$
  \item With more current drawn from the battery for the parallel connection, more power is dissipated in this connection. While the resistors in series share the voltage of the battery, the resistors in parallel have the full potential difference of the battery across them.
  \item The upper branch, with twice the resistance of the lower branch, will have $1 / 2$ the current of the lower branch.
  \item The larger loop, with twice the radius, has twice the circumference (length) and $R=\rho L / A$
  \item By process of elimination, A is the only possible true statement.
  \item $\mathrm{R}=\rho \mathrm{L} / \mathrm{A}$. If $\mathrm{L} \div 2, \mathrm{R} \div 2$ and is $\mathrm{r} \div 2$ then $\mathrm{A} \div 4$ and $\mathrm{R} \times 4$ making the net effect $\mathrm{R} \div 2 \times 4$
  \item Because of the junction rule, I1 must equal the sum of I2 and I3. Resistance is only relevant to the comparison of currents if the potential difference is the same.
  \item Resistance of the $2000 \Omega$ and $6000 \Omega$ in parallel $=1500 \Omega$, adding the $2500 \Omega$ in series gives a total circuit resistance of $4000 \Omega$. Itotal $=\mathrm{I}_{1}=\mathrm{E} / \mathrm{R}_{\text {total }}$
  \item Only C would decrease the total resistance of the circuit, by adding a new parallel branch
  \item The lower-Watt bulb across the same potential difference draws less current. It therefore has a greater resistance.
  \item The current through R is found using the junction rule at the top junction, where $1 \mathrm{~A}+2 \mathrm{~A}$ enter giving $\mathrm{I}=3 \mathrm{~A}$. Now utilize Kirchhoff's loop rule through the left or right loops: (left side) +16 $\mathrm{V}-(1 \mathrm{~A})(4 \Omega)-(3 \mathrm{~A}) \mathrm{R}=0$ giving $\mathrm{R}=4 \Omega$
  \item Utilizing Kirchhoff's loop rile with any loop including the lower branch gives 0 V since the resistance next to each battery drops the 2 V of each battery leaving the lower branch with no current. You can also think of the junction rule where there is 0.04 A going into each junction and 0.04 A leaving to the other battery, with no current for the lower branch.
  \item Summing the potential differences from left to right gives $\mathrm{V}_{\mathrm{T}}=-12 \mathrm{~V}-(2 \mathrm{~A})(2 \Omega)=-16 \mathrm{~V}$. It is possible for $\mathrm{V}_{\mathrm{T}}>\mathcal{E}$.
  \item Current is greatest where resistance is least. The resistances are, in order, $1 \Omega, 2 \Omega, 4 \Omega$, and 2 $\Omega$
  \item See above
  \item Least power is for the greatest resistance $\left(\mathrm{P}=\boldsymbol{\mathcal { E }}^{2} / \mathrm{R}\right)$
  \item In series, the equivalent capacitance is calculated using reciprocals, like resistors in parallel. This results in an equivalent capacitance smaller than the smallest capacitor.
  \item With a load of less resistance, a greater current flows, and there is therefore a greater potential difference over the internal resistance.
  \item Kirchhoff's junction rule applied at point X gives $2 \mathrm{~A}=\mathrm{I}+1 \mathrm{~A}$, so the current in the middle wire is 1 A . Summing the potential differences through the middle wire from X to Y gives $-10 \mathrm{~V}-$ $(1 \mathrm{~A})(2 \Omega)=-12 \mathrm{~V}$
  \item When the switch is closed, the circuit behaves as if the capacitor were just a wire and all the potential of the battery is across the resistor. As the capacitor charges, the voltage changes over to the capacitor over time, eventually making the current (and the potential difference across the resistor) zero and the potential difference across the capacitor equal to the emf of the battery.
  \item See above
  \item See above
  \item $P=I^{2} R$
  \item For each capacitor to have $6 \mu \mathrm{C}$, each branch will have $6 \mu \mathrm{C}$ since the two capacitors in series in each branch has the same charge. The total charge for the three branches is then $18 \mu \mathrm{C} . \mathrm{Q}=\mathrm{CV}$ gives $18 \mu \mathrm{C}=(3 \mu \mathrm{~F}) \mathrm{V}$
  \item Utilizing Kirchhoff's loop rule starting at the upper left and moving clockwise: $-(2 \mathrm{~A})(0.3 \Omega)+$ $12 \mathrm{~V}-6 \mathrm{~V}-(2 \mathrm{~A})(0.2 \Omega)-(2 \mathrm{~A})(\mathrm{R})-(2 \mathrm{~A})(1.5 \Omega)=0$
  \item Summing the potential differences: $-6 \mathrm{~V}-(2 \mathrm{~A})(0.2 \Omega)-(2 \mathrm{~A})(1 \Omega)=-8.4 \mathrm{~V}$
  \item As the capacitor becomes charged, more current flows through R2, thus increasing the equivalent resistance of the circuit and decreasing the current flow - but it never becomes zero.
  \item For the ammeter to read zero means the junctions at the ends of the ammeter have the same potential. For this to be true, the potential drops across the $1 \Omega$ and the $2 \Omega$ resistor must be equal, which means the current through the $1 \Omega$ resistor must be twice that of the $2 \Omega$ resistor. This means the resistance of the upper branch ( $1 \Omega$ and $3 \Omega$ ) must be $1 / 2$ that of the lower branch ( $2 \Omega$ and R ) giving $1 \Omega+3 \Omega=1 / 2(2 \Omega+\mathrm{R})$
  \item The current through the ammeter will flow toward the bottom of the page if that flow is from a higher to a lower potential. To accomplish this, $\mathrm{R} / 2 \Omega$ must be greater than $3 \Omega / 1 \Omega$.
  \item Kirchhoff's loop rule $(\mathrm{V}=\mathrm{Q} / \mathrm{C}$ for a capacitor $)$
  \item To dissipate 24 W means $\mathrm{R}=\mathrm{V}^{2} / \mathrm{P}=6 \Omega$. The resistances, in order, are: $8 \Omega, 4 / 3 \Omega, 8 / 3 \Omega, 12 \Omega$ and $6 \Omega$
  \item The equivalent capacitance between X and Y is twice the capacitance between Y and Z . This means the voltage between X and Y is $1 / 2$ the voltage between Y and Z . For a total of 12 V , this gives 4 V between X and Y and 8 V between Y and Z .
  \item Closing the switch short circuits Bulb 2 causing no current to flow to it. Since the bulbs were originally in series, this decreases the total resistance and increases the total current, making bulb 1 brighter.
  \item Closing the switch reduces the resistance in the right side from $20 \Omega$ to $15 \Omega$, making the total circuit resistance decrease from $35 \Omega$ to $30 \Omega$, a slight decrease, causing a slight increase in current. For the current to double, the total resistance must be cut in half.
  \item $\quad R=\rho L / A \propto L / d^{2}$ where $d$ is the diameter. $R_{x} / R_{y}=L_{x} / d_{x}{ }^{2} \div L_{y} / d_{y}{ }^{2}=\left(2 L_{y}\right) d_{y}{ }^{2} /\left[L_{y}\left(2 d_{y}\right)^{2}\right]=1 / 2$
  \item Summing the potential differences from bottom to top:\\
left circuit: $-(1 \mathrm{~A}) \mathrm{r}+\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}=10 \mathrm{~V}$\\
right circuit: $+(1 \mathrm{~A}) \mathrm{r}+\boldsymbol{\mathcal { E }}=20 \mathrm{~V}$, solve simultaneous equations
  \item If you perform Kirchhoff's loop rule for the highlighted loop, you get a current of 0 A through the $6 \Omega$ resistor.\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-118}
  \item N is in the main branch, with the most current. The current then divides into the two branches, with K receiving twice the current as L and M . The $\mathrm{L} / \mathrm{M}$ branch has twice the resistance of the K branch. L and M in series have the same current.
  \item If K burns out, the circuit becomes a series circuit with the three resistors, $\mathrm{N}, \mathrm{M}$ and L all in series, reducing the current through bulb N .
  \item If M burns out, the circuit becomes a series circuit with the two resistors, N and K in series, with bulb L going out as well since it is in series with bulb M .
  \item Using Kirchhoff's loop rule around the circuit going through either V or R since they are in parallel and will have the same potential drop gives: $-\mathrm{V}-(1.00 \mathrm{~mA})(25 \Omega)+5.00 \mathrm{~V}-(1.00$ $\mathrm{mA})(975 \Omega)=0$
  \item The equivalent resistance in parallel is smaller than the smallest resistance.
  \item $\mathrm{P}=\boldsymbol{\mathcal { E }}^{2} / \mathrm{R}$. Total resistance of n resistors in series is nR making the power $\mathrm{P}=\boldsymbol{\mathcal { E }}^{2} / \mathrm{nR}=\mathrm{P} / \mathrm{n}$
  \item If the current in the $6 \Omega$ resistor is 1 A , then by ratios, the currents in the $2 \Omega$ and $3 \Omega$ resistor are 3 A and 2 A respectively (since they have $1 / 3$ and $1 / 2$ the resistance). This makes the total current 6 A and the potential drop across the $4 \Omega$ resistor 24 V . Now use Kirchhoff's loop rule for any branch.
  \item The voltage across the capacitor is $6 \mathrm{~V}(\mathrm{Q}=\mathrm{CV})$ and since the capacitor is in parallel with the $300 \Omega$ resistor, the voltage across the $300 \Omega$ resistor is also 6 V . The $200 \Omega$ resistor is not considered since the capacitor is charged and no current flows through that branch. The $100 \Omega$ resistor in series with the $300 \Omega$ resistor has $1 / 3$ the voltage ( 2 V ) since it is $1 / 3$ the resistance. Kirchhoff's loop rule for the left loop gives $\boldsymbol{\mathcal { E }}=8 \mathrm{~V}$.
  \item For the currents in the branches to be equal, each branch must have the same resistance.
  \item Resistor D is in a branch by itself while resistors $\mathrm{A}, \mathrm{B}$ and C are in series, drawing less current than resistor D .
  \item Even though the wires have different resistances and currents, the potential drop across each is 1.56 V and will vary by the same gradient, dropping all 1.56 V along the same length.
  \item A and E failing in the main branch would cause the entire circuit to fail. B and C would affect each other.
  \item $\quad \mathrm{V}=\mathrm{IR}$
  \item $\boldsymbol{\mathcal { E }}=\mathrm{IR}_{\text {total }}$ where $\mathrm{R}_{\text {total }}=35 \Omega$
  \item With the switch closed, the resistance of the $15 \Omega$ and the $30 \Omega$ in parallel is $10 \Omega$, making the total circuit resistance $30 \Omega$ and $\boldsymbol{\mathcal { E }}=\mathrm{IR}$
  \item The equivalent resistance through path ACD is equal to the equivalent resistance through path ABD , making the current through the two branches equal
  \item The resistance in each of the two paths is $9 \Omega$, making the current in each branch 1 A . From point A, the potential drop across the $7 \Omega$ resistor is then 7 V and across the $4 \Omega$ resistor is 4 V , making point B 3 V lower than point C
  \item Closing the switch reduces the total resistance of the circuit, increasing the current in the main branch containing bulb 1
  \item Resistors J and N are in the main branch and therefore receive the largest current.
  \item $P=I^{2} R$
  \item Breaking the circuit in the lower branch lowers the total current in the circuit, decreasing the voltage across $R_{1}$. Looking at the upper loop, this means $R_{2}$ now has a larger share of the battery voltage and the voltage across AD is the same as the voltage across BC
  \item In series circuits, larger resistors develop more power
  \item With a total resistance of $10 \Omega$, the total current is 1.2 A . The terminal voltage $\mathrm{V}_{\mathrm{T}}=\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}-\operatorname{Ir}$
  \item Shorting bulb 3 decreases the resistance in the right branch, increasing the current through bulb 4 and decreasing the total circuit resistance. This increases the total current in the main branch containing bulb 1 .
  \item The total charge to be distributed is $+100 \mu \mathrm{C}-50 \mu \mathrm{C}=+50 \mu \mathrm{C}$. In parallel, the capacitors must have the same voltage so the $20 \mu \mathrm{~F}$ capacitor has four times the charge of the $5 \mu \mathrm{~F}$ capacitor. This gives $\mathrm{Q}_{20}=4 \mathrm{Q}_{5}$ and $\mathrm{Q}_{20}+\mathrm{Q}_{5}=4 \mathrm{Q}_{5}+\mathrm{Q}_{5}=5 \mathrm{Q}_{5}=50 \mu \mathrm{C}$, or $\mathrm{Q}_{5}=10 \mu \mathrm{C}$
  \item For more light at a given voltage, more current is required, which requires less resistance. $\mathrm{R}=$ $\rho \mathrm{L} / \mathrm{A}$
  \item Wire CD shorts out bulb \#3 so it will never light. Closing the switch merely adds bulb \#2 in parallel to bulb \#1, which does not change the potential difference across bulb \#1.
  \item For points $a$ and $b$ to be at the same potential, the potential drop across the $3 \Omega$ resistor must be equal to the potential drop across capacitor C . The potential drop across the $3 \Omega$ resistor is three times the drop across the $1 \Omega$ resistor. For the potential drop across capacitor C to be three times the crop across the $1 \mu \mathrm{~F}$ capacitor, C must be $1 / 3$ the capacitance, or $1 / 3 \mu \mathrm{~F}$
  \item Shorting bulb 4 decreases the resistance in the right branch, increasing the current through bulb 3 and in the main branch containing bulb 1.
  \item When the current is 0.5 A , the voltage across the resistor is $\mathrm{V}=\mathrm{IR}=5 \mathrm{~V}$. According to the loop rule, the remaining 7 V must be across the capacitor.
  \item Since there is constant current, bulb 1 remains unchanged and bulbs 2 and three must now split the current. With half the current through bulb 2, the potential difference between A and B is also halved.
  \item The voltmeter is essentially another resistor. The voltmeter in parallel with the $100 \Omega$ resistor acts as a $500 \Omega$ resistor, which will half $1 / 2$ the voltage of the $100 \Omega$ resistor on the left. Thus the 120 V will split into 80 V for the $1000 \Omega$ resistor and 40 V for the voltmeter combination.
  \item The $15 \Omega$ resistor would be in parallel with the $30 \Omega$ resistor when the switch is closed.
  \item $\mathrm{ACD}=9 \Omega, \mathrm{ABD}=9 \Omega$ so the total resistance is $4.5 \Omega$ making the total current $\mathcal{E} / \mathrm{R}=2 \mathrm{~A}$.
  \item The 2 A will divide equally between the two branches with 1 A going through each branch. From $B$ to $D$ we have $-(1 A)(2 \Omega)=-2 V$, with $B$ at the higher potential
  \item When the capacitor is charged, the branch is effectively removed from the circuit, making it a simple parallel circuit. The total resistance is $133.3 \Omega$ and $V=I R$
  \item In a simple series circuit with two batteries opposing one another the voltages subtract from one another. The total effective voltage for this circuit is then 4 V . With a total resistance of $20 \Omega$ the total current is $(4 \mathrm{~V}) /(20 \Omega)$
  \item When the capacitor is charged, the branch is effectively removed from the circuit, making the circuit a $10 \Omega$ resistor in series with two $10 \Omega$ resistors in parallel. The lone $10 \Omega$ resistor has twice the voltage of the two $10 \Omega$ resistors in parallel with an effective resistance of $5 \Omega$. The 10 volts will then divide with 3.3 V going to the parallel combination and 6.7 V going to the single $10 \Omega$ resistor. The capacitor is in parallel with the single $10 \Omega$ resistor. $\mathrm{Q}=\mathrm{CV}$
  \item The resistances are, respectively, $4 / 3 R, 2 / 5 R, R$, and $5 / 3 R$
  \item Closing the switch adds another parallel branch, increasing the total current delivered by the battery. Bulb 3 will get brighter. Bulb 2, in its own loop with bulb 3 and the battery will then lose some of its share of the potential difference from the battery and will get dimmer.
  \item Voltmeters must be placed in parallel and ammeters must be placed in series.
  \item $\mathrm{S}_{1}$ must be closed to have any current. Closing $\mathrm{S}_{2}$ will allow current in $\mathrm{R}_{2}$ but closing $\mathrm{R}_{3}$ would short circuit $\mathrm{R}_{2}$.
  \item $\mathrm{S}_{1}$ must be closed to have any current. Closing $\mathrm{S}_{3}$ will short circuit $\mathrm{R}_{3}$, leaving only resistor $\mathrm{R}_{1}$, which is the lowest possible resistance.
  \item $\quad \mathrm{S}_{1}$ must be closed to have any current. The greatest voltage will occur with the greatest current through $R_{3}$ but closing $S_{2}$ or $S_{3}$ will draw current away from $R_{3}$.
\end{enumerate}
