a. $\mathrm{V}_{\mathrm{T}}=\mathrm{E}-\mathrm{Ir}=6 \mathrm{~V}$\\
b. In parallel, each resistor gets 6 V and $\mathrm{P}=\mathrm{V}^{2} / \mathrm{R}$ gives $\mathrm{R}=3 \Omega$\\
c. For the $3 \Omega$ resistor we have $\mathrm{I}=\mathrm{V} / \mathrm{R}=2 \mathrm{~A}$ leaving 1 A for the branch with $\mathrm{R}_{1} . \mathrm{R}=\mathrm{V} / \mathrm{I}=6 \Omega$

\section*{1981B4}
a. The two batteries are connected with opposing emfs so the total emf in the circuit is $\boldsymbol{\mathcal { E }}=60 \mathrm{~V}-12 \mathrm{~V}=48 \mathrm{~V}$ The resistance of the parallel combination of resistors is $(1 / 4+1 / 4+1 / 2)^{-1}=1 \Omega$ combining with the rest of the resistors in series gives a total circuit resistance of $8 \Omega$.\\
The total current is then $\boldsymbol{\mathcal { E }} / \mathrm{R}=6 \mathrm{~A}$. The voltage across the parallel combination of resistors is $\mathrm{V}_{\mathrm{P}}=\mathrm{IR}_{\mathrm{P}}=6 \mathrm{~V}$ so the current through the $2 \Omega$ resistor is $I=V / R=3 \mathrm{~A}$\\
b. $\quad \mathrm{P}=\mathrm{I}^{2} \mathrm{R}=108 \mathrm{~W}$\\
c. The current is forced through battery Bfrom the positive to the negative terminal, charging the battery. This makes the equation for the terminal voltage $\mathrm{V}_{\mathrm{T}}=\boldsymbol{\mathcal { E }}+\mathrm{Ir}=18 \mathrm{~V}$

\section*{1980B2}
a. The resistance of the device is found from $\mathrm{R}=\mathrm{V} / \mathrm{I}=6 \Omega$. With a 24 volt source, to provide a current of 2 A requires a total resistance of $12 \Omega$. For the additional $6 \Omega$ resistance, place two $3 \Omega$ resistors in series with the device.\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-121}\\
b. Since the device requires 2 A , a resistor in parallel with the device must carry a current of $6 \mathrm{~A}-2 \mathrm{~A}=4 \mathrm{~A}$. In parallel with the device, the resistor will have a potential difference of 12 V so must have a resistance of $\mathrm{V} / \mathrm{I}=$ $3 \Omega$. Thus, a single $3 \Omega$ resistor in parallel will suffice.\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-121(1)}\\
c. $\quad \mathrm{P}=\mathrm{I}^{2} \mathrm{R}=48 \mathrm{~W}$\\
a. Since the clock requires 15 V it must be directly connected between A and B . Since the radio requires less than 15 V , there must be a resistor in series with it.\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-122}\\
b. The current through the radio (and R ) is 10 mA . The voltage across the radio is 9 V , which leaves 6 V across the resistor giving $\mathrm{R}=\mathrm{V} / \mathrm{I}=600 \Omega$\\
c. $\mathrm{P}=\mathrm{IV}$ where $\mathrm{V}=15 \mathrm{~V}$ and $\mathrm{I}=10 \mathrm{~mA}+20 \mathrm{~mA}=30 \mathrm{~mA}$ so $\mathrm{P}=0.45 \mathrm{~W}$ and energy $=\mathrm{Pt}=27 \mathrm{~J}$

\section*{1983B3}
a. The two batteries are connected with opposing emfs so the total emf in the circuit is $\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}=20 \mathrm{~V}-2 \mathrm{~V}=18 \mathrm{~V}$ The equivalent resistance of the two parallel resistors is $(6 \times 12) /(6+12)=4 \Omega$ and since R is in series with the pair, the total circuit resistance is $(4+\mathrm{R}) \Omega=\mathcal{E} / \mathrm{I}=9 \Omega$ giving $\mathrm{R}=5 \Omega$\\
b. Because the voltages of the two resistors in parallel are equal we have $6 \mathrm{I}_{1}=12 \mathrm{I}_{2}$ and $\mathrm{I}_{1}+\mathrm{I}_{2}=2 \mathrm{~A}$ giving\\
i. $4 / 3 \mathrm{~A}$\\
ii. $2 / 3 \mathrm{~A}$\\
c. Summing the potential differences form point X gives $\mathrm{V}_{\mathrm{X}}+\mathrm{IR}=0+(2 \mathrm{~A})(5 \Omega)=\mathrm{V}_{\mathrm{B}}=10 \mathrm{~V}$. Continuing along gives $\mathrm{V}_{\mathrm{B}}-20 \mathrm{~V}=\mathrm{V}_{\mathrm{C}}=-10 \mathrm{~V}$. And $\mathrm{V}_{\mathrm{C}}+(2 / 3 \mathrm{~A})(12 \Omega)=\mathrm{V}_{\mathrm{D}}=-2 \mathrm{~V}$\\
d. $\quad \mathrm{P}=\boldsymbol{\varepsilon} \mathrm{I}=40 \mathrm{~W}$

\section*{1986B3}
a. $\mathrm{P}=\mathrm{V}^{2} \mathrm{R}$ gives $\mathrm{R}=240 \Omega$\\

c. $\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{T}}=\boldsymbol{\mathcal { E }}^{2} / \mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{T}}=40 \mathrm{~W}$\\
d. $\mathrm{I}=\boldsymbol{\mathcal { E }} / \mathrm{R}=1 / 3 \mathrm{~A}$\\

1988B3\\
a. On the right we have two resistors in series: $10 \Omega+2 \Omega=12 \Omega$. This is in parallel with the $4 \Omega$ resistor which is an equivalent resistance of $3 \Omega$ and adding the remaining main branch resistor in series gives a total circuit resistance of $9 \Omega$. The current is then $\mathrm{I}=\boldsymbol{\varepsilon} / \mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{T}}=8 \mathrm{~A}$\\
b. The voltage remaining for the parallel branches on the right is the emf of the battery minus the potential dropped across the $6 \Omega$ resistor which is $72 \mathrm{~V}-(8 \mathrm{~A})(6 \Omega)=24 \mathrm{~V}$. Thus the current in the $10 \Omega$ resistor is the current through the whole $12 \Omega$ branch which is $\mathrm{I}=\mathrm{V} / \mathrm{R}=(24 \mathrm{~V}) /(12 \Omega)=2 \mathrm{~A}$\\
c. $\mathrm{V}_{10}=\mathrm{I}_{10} \mathrm{R}_{10}=20 \mathrm{~V}$\\
d. When charged, the capacitor is in parallel with the $10 \Omega$ resistor so $\mathrm{V}_{\mathrm{C}}=\mathrm{V}_{10}=20 \mathrm{~V}$ and $\mathrm{Q}=\mathrm{CV}=60 \mu \mathrm{C}$\\

Electrons are accelerated from rest through a potential difference $V_{\mathrm{o}}$ and then pass through a region between two parallel metal plates, as shown above. The region between the plates can contain a uniform electric field $\mathbf{E}$ and a uniform magnetic field $\mathbf{B}$. With only the electric field present, the electrons follow path 1 . With only the magnetic field present, the electrons follow path 3. As drawn, the curved paths between the plates show the correct direction of deflection for each field, but not necessarily the correct path shape. With both fields present, the electrons pass undeflected along the straight path 2.\\

i. Which of the following describes the shape of the portion of path 1 between the plates?\\
$\qquad$ Circular $\qquad$ Parabolic $\qquad$ Hyperbolic $\qquad$ Exponential\\
Justify your answer.\\

$\qquad$ To the left $\qquad$ To the top of the page $\qquad$ Into the page\\
\_\_\_ To the right $\qquad$ To the bottom of the page $\qquad$ Out of the page\\

(b)\\
i. Which of the following describes the shape of the portion of path 3 between the plates?\\
\_\_Circular $\qquad$ Parabolic $\qquad$ Hyperbolic $\qquad$ Exponential\\
Justify your answer.\\
ii. What is the direction of the magnetic field?\\
$\qquad$ To the left $\qquad$ To the top of the page $\qquad$ Into the page\\
$\qquad$ To the right $\qquad$ To the bottom of the page $\qquad$ Out of the page\\

Between the plates the magnitude of the electric field is $3.4 \times 10^{4} \mathrm{~V} / \mathrm{m}$, and that of the magnetic field is $2.0 \times 10^{-3} \mathrm{~T}$.\\

\section*{SECTION A - Magnetism}
Solution\\
For the purposes of this solution guide. The following hand rules will be referred to. RHR means right hand rule (for + current). LHR will be substituted for - current\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-177(2)}

RHRflat\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-177}

RHR-Solenoid\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-177(1)}

\begin{enumerate}
  \item Each wire contributes a B field given by $\mu_{\mathrm{o}} \mathrm{I} / 2 \pi \mathrm{a}$ in a direction found using RHRcurl. The direction of each $B$ field is as follows, (1)Top right wire: $B$ up\&left, (2)Top left wire: $B$ up\&right, (3)Bottom left wire: B up\&left, (4)Bottom right wire: B down\&left. Forces from 1 and 4 cancel leaving both 3 and 4 B fields acting up and left and adding together.
  \item The B field at the location of the charge +e is created by the wire next to it and given by $\mathrm{B}=\mu_{\mathrm{o}} \mathrm{I} / 2 \pi \mathrm{R}$. Based on RHRcurl the direction of that B field is into the page at that location. Then the force on that charge is given by $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{b}}=\mathrm{qvB}$, with $\mathrm{q}=\mathrm{e}$ and B from before so $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{b}}=\operatorname{ev}\left(\mu_{\mathrm{o}} \mathrm{I} / 2 \pi \mathrm{R}\right)$. Using the RHRflat for the + charge, the force comes out as down.
  \item Charges moving through magnetic fields move in circles as in the diagram for question 39
  \item The compass is ABOVE the wire. Using RHRcurl on the wire, the B field points towards the right at the location above the wire. Since compasses follow $B$ field lines, the compass will also point right, which is east.
  \item To be undeflected, the electric and magnetic forces must balance.\\
$\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{e}}=\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{b}} \quad \mathrm{Eq}=\mathrm{qvB} \quad \mathrm{B}=\mathrm{E} / \mathrm{v} \quad$ With v related to K by $\mathrm{K}=1 / 2 \mathrm{mv}^{2}$ gives $B=E / \sqrt{ }(2 K / m) \ldots$ which is equivalent to choice $D$
  \item When cutting a magnet, you must end up with two new magnets having 2 poles each. For the top magnet the current N and S must stay as is, so the left of center part becomes a S and the right of center part becomes a N. There are now two opposite poles that attract. For the bottom magnet, by slicing it down the center you now have two magnets on top of each other. The poles would not change their current locations so you have two north and two south poles near each other on top and bottom which makes repulsion.
  \item For this scenario, The circular motion is provided by the magnetic force. So that $\mathrm{F}_{\text {net( } \mathrm{C})}=\mathrm{mv}^{2} / \mathrm{r}$
\end{enumerate}

$$
\mathrm{qvB}=\mathrm{mv}^{2} / \mathrm{r} \quad \mathrm{qBr}=\mathrm{mv} \quad 2 \times \mathrm{V} \rightarrow 2 \times \mathrm{r}
$$

. Focus on a single + charge in the wire that gets pushed to the right. So this + charge is moving in a magnetic field pointing into the page with a force directed right, based on RHRflat, the charge must be moving down.\\
9. When moving parallel to magnetic fields, no forces are experienced.\\
10. Assume R is north. Based on the lines, T would have to be north and so would Y .

This makes X and Z south and S north.\\
11. Using RHRcurl, we get into the page\\
12. Parallel current wires with same direction current attract.\\
13. Focus on a single + charge in the wire that gets pushed to the right. So this + charge is moving down with a force directed right, based on RHRflat, the magnetic field must point into the page.\\
14. By definition, E fields exert forces on + charges in the same direction as the E field. So the force from the E field must be UP. To maintain a constant velocity, this upwards force must be counterbalanced by a downwards force, which in this case it is to be provided by the magnetic field. With a + charge moving right, and a magnetic force down, RHRflat gives a magnetic field pointing out of the page.\\
15. A coil of wire (solenoid) like this becomes an electromagnet when the current runs through it. Use the RHR-solenoid to determine that the right side of this electromagnet becomes the north side. Now pretend that the electromagnet is simply a regular magnet with a N pole on the right and a S pole on the left and draw the field lines. In doing so, the lines end up pointing to the left at the location of the compass. Since compasses follow magnetic field lines, the compass will also point left.\\
16. Due to action reaction the forces must be the same. Another way to look at it is that wire A creates the field that wire $B$ is sitting in based on its current $I, B_{a}=\mu_{o} I_{a} / 2 \pi R$. The force on wire B is dependent on the field from A , and also the current in wire B itself and is given by $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{b}}=\mathrm{B}_{\mathrm{a}} \mathrm{I}_{\mathrm{b}} \mathrm{L} \quad \mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{b}}=\left(\mu_{\mathrm{o}} \mathrm{I}_{\mathrm{a}} / 2 \pi \mathrm{R}\right) \mathrm{I}_{\mathrm{b}} \mathrm{L}$. So since both currents from A and B affect each respective force, they should share the same force.\\
17. Think about this as if you are looking down at a table top with the + particle on it. An $E$ field is pointed down into the table so an electric force acts down into the table also. The electric force pushing down will not move the charge. A magnetic field comes up out of the table, but since the charge is at rest, the magnetic field exerts zero force on it. So $F_{e}>F_{b}$\\
18. First of all we should state that a larger current makes a bigger $B$ field and the further from the wire the less the B field. Using RHRcurl, the 4 A wire has decreasing magnitude B fields pointing down in regions II and III on the axis and upwards on region I. The 3 A wire has B fields pointing upwards in region III and downwards in regions II and I. To cancel, fields would have to oppose each other. Region I is a possibility but since the distance from the 4A wire is smaller at every point and it also has a larger current it will always have a larger B field so there is no way to cancel. Region II has fields in the same direction and cannot cancel. Region III has opposing fields. Since the 4A wire has a larger current but also a larger distance away from any point in Region III and the 3A wire has a smaller current but a closer distance to any point in Region III it is possible that these two factors compensate to make equal B fields that oppose and could cancel out.\\
19. Using RHR-solenoid the top of the loop is N and the bottom is S . Drawing a field line out of the top and looping outside down to the bottom, you have to continue up through the solenoid to complete the field line so the direction is UP. (Note: this may seem counterintuitive because the field line points from the south to the north which is opposite of what you might think but this is INSIDE the solenoid (magnet). Only outside, do lines come out of $N$ and into $S$.)\\
20. Use RHR-flat\\
21. We first need to determine the direction of the B field at P due to the other wires using RHRcurl. The top wire creates a B field pointing up\&right, the bottom wire creates a B field pointing up\&left. The left and right parts of these cancel out making a field only up from these two wires. The wire on the left also produces a field only up so the net B field points up at location P. Now using RHRflat for the right wire, the force is left.\\
22. First determine the B field direction created by the current wire at the location above the wire using RHRcurl. This gives B out of page. Then use LHRflat for the negative charge to get force acting down.\\
23. In region I, the electric field pushes the negative electron with a force opposite the direction of the E field (out of the page). For the charge to not be pushed out, the magnetic field must create a force into the page to resist this. Based on LHRflat the B field must point up. Then in region II based on how the charge gets pushed, its magnetic force is up initially. Using LHRflat again in region II gives B field direction out of the page.\\
24. Based on RHR-flat the magnetic force is directed into the page. To be undeflected, the E field must create a force out of the page to resist this, and since it's a + charge the E field points out.\\
25. This is a loop. Current flows clockwise around the loop. Using the RHR-solenoid for the single loop the B field in the center is pointing into the page.\\
26. Use RHRflat\\
27. Use RHRcurl\\
28. Using RHRcurl we find the direction of the magnetic field from each wire. To the right of the leftmost wire, its field points down along the axis with a decreasing magnitude as you move away from it. For the rightmost wire its field also points down when you move left of it. Since both fields point down between the wires, they will add and cannot cancel. On the far right side of the arrangement, the leftmost wire makes a field down and the rightmost wire makes a field up but since the distances to any location are different from each wire the magnitude of the fields would be different so no way to cancel. The same would happen on the far left of the wires.\\
29. Use LHRflat\\
30. To induce a current, the flux through the spring loop must change. When moving the spring parallel to the magnetic field, the same B field and the same area is enclosed in the loop so the flux stays constant and there is no induced current.\\
31. When moving in a circle at constant velocity, no work is done as explained in previous answers.\\
32. Choose 1 proton moving in the circle. For this proton. $\mathrm{F}_{n e t(\mathrm{C})}=\mathrm{mv}^{2} / \mathrm{r} \quad \mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{b}}=\mathrm{mv}^{2} / \mathrm{r}$ $\mathrm{qvB}=\mathrm{mv}^{2} / \mathrm{r} \quad \mathrm{v}=\mathrm{qBr} / \mathrm{m}=1.6 \times 10^{-19}(0.1)(0.1) /\left(1.67 \times 10^{-27}\right) \sim 10^{-21} / 10^{-27}$\\
33. As described in question 17, the force on either wire is $F_{b}=\left(\mu_{o} I_{a} / 2 \pi R\right) I_{b} L$. So doubling both I's in the equation gives $4 x$ the force.\\
34. Not a magnetism question, but lets review. Since the charge magnitude is the same, they will experience the same forces based on $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{e}}=\mathrm{Eq}$, but move in opposite directions. Since the masses are different, the same forces will affect each object differently so that the smaller mass electron accelerates more, thus gains more speed and covers more distance in equal time periods. So only the force is the same.\\
35. Same as question 33\\
36. Since the particle is moving parallel to the field it does not cut across lines and has no force.\\
37. Using RHRcurl for each wire, the left wire makes a field pointing down\&right at P and the right wire makes a field pointing up\&right. The up and down parts cancel leaving only right.\\
38. The electric force would act upwards on the proton so the magnetic force would act down. Using RHRflat, the B field must point out of the page.\\
39. $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{e}}=\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{b}} \quad \mathrm{Eq}=\mathrm{qvB} \quad \mathrm{E}=\mathrm{vB} \quad \mathrm{B}=\mathrm{E} / \mathrm{v}$\\
40. Based on ... $\mathrm{F}_{\text {net }(\mathrm{C})}=\mathrm{mv}^{2} / \mathrm{r} \quad \ldots \quad \mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{b}}=\mathrm{mv}^{2} / \mathrm{r} \quad \ldots \quad \mathrm{qvB}=\mathrm{mv}^{2} / \mathrm{r} \quad \ldots \quad \mathrm{r}=\mathrm{mv} / \mathrm{qB} \ldots$ inverse\\
42. From question 17, $F_{b}=\left(\mu_{o} I_{a} / 2 \pi R\right) I_{b} L \ldots R$ is $x 2$ and both I's are $x 2$ so it's a net effect of $x 2$.\\
43. Using RHR-solenoid, the B field at the center of that loop is directed right. Since the other loop is further away, its direction is irrelevant at the left loop will dominate.\\
44. Based on the axis given. The left side wire is on the axis and makes no torque. The top and bottom wires essentially cancel each other out due to opposite direction forces, so the torque can be found from the right wire only. Finding the force on the right wire ... $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{b}}=\mathrm{BIL}=(0.05)(2)(0.3)=.03 \mathrm{~N}$, then torque $=\mathrm{Fr}=(0.03)(0.3)$.\\
45. The field from a single wire is given by $\mu_{o} \mathrm{I}_{a} / 2 \pi \mathrm{R}$. The additional field from wire Y would be based on this formula with $R=3 R$, so in comparison it has $1 / 3$ the strength of wire $X$. So adding wire X's field $B_{0}+$ the relative field of wire Y's of $1 / 3 B_{0}$ gives a total of $4 / 3 B_{0}$.\\
46. First we use RHRcurl to find the $B$ field above the wire as into the page, and we note that the magnitude of the $B$ field decreases as we move away from it. Since the left $A B$ and right $C D$ wires are sitting in the same average value of B field and have current in opposite directions, they repel each other and those forces cancel out. Now we look at the wire AD closest to the wire. Using RHRflat for this wire we get down as a force. The force on the top wire BC is irrelevant because the top and bottom wires have the same current but the B field is smaller for the top wire so the bottom wire will dominate the force direction no matter what. Therefore, the direction is down towards the wire.\\
47. Using RHRflat for the magnetic field direction given, the magnetic force would be up (+z). To counteract this upwards force on the + charge, the E field would have to point down ( -z ).\\
48. A little tricky since its talking about fields and not forces. To move at constant velocity the magnetic FORCE must be opposite to the electric FORCE. Electric fields make force in the same plane as the field (ex: a field in the $x$ plane makes a force in the $x$ plane), but magnetic fields make forces in a plane 90 degrees away from it (ex: a field in the $x$ plane can only make magnetic forces in the $y$ or $z$ plane). So to create forces in the same place, the fields have to be perpendicular to each other.\\
49. First we have $\ldots \mathrm{F}_{\text {net }(\mathrm{C})}=\mathrm{mv}^{2} / \mathrm{r} \quad \ldots \quad \mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{b}}=\mathrm{mv}^{2} / \mathrm{r} \quad \ldots \quad \mathrm{qvB}=\mathrm{mv}^{2} / \mathrm{r} \quad \ldots \quad \mathrm{v}=\mathrm{qBr} / \mathrm{m}$ Then using $\mathrm{v}=2 \pi \mathrm{R} / \mathrm{T}$ we have $\mathrm{qBr} / \mathrm{m}=2 \pi \mathrm{R} / \mathrm{T} \ldots$ radius cancels so period is unchanged and frequency also is unaffected by the radius. Another way to think about this with the two equations given above is: by increasing $R$, the speed increases, but the $2 \pi R$ distance term increases the same amount so the time to rotate is the same.\\
50. Pick any small segment of wire. The force should point to the center of the circle. For any small segment of wire, use RHRflat and you get velocity direction is CCW. Equation is the same as the problem above $\ldots \mathrm{qvB}=\mathrm{mv}^{2} / \mathrm{r} \ldots \mathrm{eBr}=\mathrm{mv}$.\\
51. Same as in question $53 \ldots \quad \mathrm{qBr} / \mathrm{m}=2 \pi \mathrm{R} / \mathrm{T} \ldots \quad \mathrm{T}=2 \pi \mathrm{~m} / \mathrm{eB}$.\\
52. The left and right sides of the loop wires are parallel to the field and experience no forces. Based on RHRflat, the top part of the loop would have a force out of the page and the bottom part of the loop would have a force into of the page which rotates as in choice C .\\
53. Each wire creates a magnetic field around itself. Since all the currents are the same, and wire Y is closer to wire X , wire X 's field will be stronger there and dominate the force on wire Y . So we can essentially ignore wire Z to determine the direction of the force. Since X and Y are in the same direction they attract and Y gets pulled to the left.\\
54. Wires with current in the same direction are attractive. Using RHRcurl for each wire at the location shown has the top wire having $B_{\text {in }}$ and the bottom wire making $B_{\text {out }}$. Since its at the midpoint the fields are equal and cancel to zero.

\section*{SECTION B - Electromagnetic Induction}
\section*{Solution}
\begin{enumerate}
  \item A complex problem. On the left diagram, the battery shows how + current flows. Based on this it flows left through the resistor and then down on the front side wires of the solenoid. Using the RHR-solenoid, the right side of the solenoid is the North pole. So field lines from the left solenoid are pointing to the right plunging into the solenoid core of the right side circuit. As the resistance in the left side increases, less current flows, which makes the magnetic field lines created decrease in value. Based on Lenz law, the right side solenoid wants to preserve the field lines so current flows to generate field lines to the right in order to maintain the flux. Using the RHR-solenoid for the right hand solenoid, current has to flow down on the front side wires to create the required $B$ field. Based on this, current would then flow down the resistor and to the left through the ammeter.
  \item Similar to the problem above. The field lines from the bar magnet are directed to the left through the solenoid. As the magnet is moved away, the magnitude of the field lines directed left in the solenoid decrease so by Lenz law the solenoid makes additional leftward field to maintain the flux. Based on RHR-solenoid, the current would flow up the front side wires of the solenoid and then to the right across the resistor. This also means that the left side of the solenoid is a N pole so it attracts the S pole of the nearby magnet.
  \item As the magnet falls down towards the pipe, which is a looped conductor, the magnetic field lines plunging into that conductor increase in magnitude. Based on Lenz's law, current flows in the conductor to oppose the gain in field and maintain the flux. The copper loop will create a B field upwards to maintain flux and this upwards B field will be opposite from the magnets B field which will make it slow.
  \item Plug into $\varepsilon=\mathrm{BLv}$
  \item Based on $\varepsilon=\mathrm{BLv}$
  \item This is a fact. It is best thought about through example and thinking about how non-conservative forces are at play. Lenz law says opposing fields are induced for moving magnets, this slows them ... if the opposite was true you would get accelerated systems where energy would not be conserved
  \item Use $\varepsilon=\Delta \Phi / \mathrm{t}$
\end{enumerate}

$$
\varepsilon=(\mathrm{BAf}-\mathrm{BAi}) / \mathrm{t}
$$

$$
\varepsilon=(0-(2)(0.5 \times 0.5)) / 0.1
$$

\begin{enumerate}
  \setcounter{enumi}{7}
  \item The rail makes a loop of wire as shown by the current flow. Using Lenz law, as the loop expands with the motion of the bar, it is gaining flux lines in whatever direction the B field is and the loop current flows in a direction to oppose that gain. Using RHR-solenoid for the single loop, the B field induced is directed out of the page so it must be opposing the gain of B field that is already there going into the page.
  \item Take a small section of wire on the loop at the top, bottom, right and left hand sides and find the forces on them. For example, the section of wire on the top has current pointing left and B pointing out ... using RHRflat for that piece gives a force pointing up. At all of the positions, the force acts in a manner to pull the loop outwards and expand it.
  \item The induced emf occurs in the left side vertical wire as that is where the charge separation happens. Looking at that wire, the induced emf is given by $\varepsilon=$ BLv. This emf then causes a current I to flow in the loop based on V=IR, so I is given as BLv / R. The direction of that current is found with Lenz law as there is a loss of flux into the page, RHR-solenoid shows\\
current must flow CW to add back flux into the page and maintain it.
  \item As long as the flux inside the loop is changing, there will be an induced current. Since choice E has both objects moving in the same direction, the flux through the loop remains constant so no need to induce a current.
  \item Same as question 7, different numbers.
  \item Looking at the primary coil, current flows CCW around it so based on RHR-solenoid the magnetic field lines from that coil are pointing to the left and they extend into the secondary coil. To induce a current in the secondary coil, the flux through the secondary coil needs to be changed so an induced current will flow based on Lenz law. Choice A means spinning the coil in place like a hula-hoop or a spinning top and this will not cause a change in flux.
  \item Based on Lenz law, as the flux pointing up decreases, current flows in the loop to add back that lost flux and maintain it. Based on RHR-solenoid, current would have to flow CCW
  \item Based on $\varepsilon=\mathrm{BLv}$, its a linear variation
  \item We are looking to find rate of change of magnetic field $\Delta \mathrm{B} / \mathrm{t}$ so we need to arrange equations to find that quantity. Using induced emf for a loop we have. $\varepsilon=\Delta \Phi / \mathrm{t}=\Delta \mathrm{BA} / \mathrm{t}$, and substituting $V=I R$, and area $=a 2$ we have $\ldots \quad I R=\Delta B(a 2) / t \ldots$ isolate $\Delta B / t$ to get answer.
  \item $\Phi=\mathrm{BA}=(2)(0.05)(0.08)$
  \item From Lenz law, as the flux decreases the loop induces current to add back that declining field. Based on RHR-solenoid, current flows CCW to add field coming out of page.
  \item Since both loops contain the same value of BA and it is changing the same for both of them, the quantity $\Delta \mathrm{BA} / \mathrm{t}$ is the same for both so both have the same induced emf.
  \item Above the wire is a B field which is directed into the page based on RHRcurl. That B field has a decreasing magnitude as you move away from the wire. Loop 1 is pulled up and therefore is loosing flux lines into the page. By Lenz Law current flows to maintain those lines into the page and by RHR-solenoid current would have to flow CW to add lines into the page and maintain the flux. Loop 2 is moving in a direction so that the magnitude of flux lines is not changing and therefore there is no induced current
  \item This is best done holding a small circular object like a small plate and rotating it towards you keeping track of the current flow. Grab the top of the plate and pull it towards you out of the page and move down at the same time to rotate it. This will increase the flux lines into the loop as you rotate and cause a current to flow to fight the increase until it becomes flat and you have moved 90 degrees in relations to the rotation you are making. Then as you pass this point and begin pushing the part of the loop you are holding down and into the page away from you, you start to lose field lines and current will flow the other way to try and maintain the flux lines until your hand has moved what was once the top of the loop all the way to the bottom. At this point you are 180 degrees through the rotation and have changed direction once. As you pass through 180, you will notice that the current flows the same way to maintain the zero flux you get at the 180 location (even though you might think there should be a change here, this is where the physical object helps). Then as you move up the back and do the same thing on the reverse side to return the part of the loop you are holding to the top you will undergo another direction change at 270 degrees so you have 2 direction changes total in one revolution. Do it two more times and you get 6 reversals.
  \item Since the bar is not cutting across field lines and has no component in a perpendicular direction to the field line there will be no induced emf.
  \item As you enter region II, flux into the page is gained. To counteract that, current flows to create a field out of the page to maintain flux. Based on RHR-solenoid, that current is CCW. When leaving the region, the flux into the page is decreasing so current flows to add to that field which gives CW.
  \item First use $\varepsilon=\Delta \Phi / \mathrm{t} \quad \varepsilon=(\mathrm{BAf}-\mathrm{BAi}) / \mathrm{t} \quad \varepsilon=(0-(0.4)(0.5 \times 0.5)) / 2 \quad \varepsilon=0.05 \mathrm{~V}$ Then use $\mathrm{V}=\mathrm{IR} \quad 0.05 \mathrm{~V}=\mathrm{I}(.01) \quad \mathrm{I}=5 \mathrm{~A}$\\
Direction is found with Lenz law. As the field out decreases, the current flows to add outward field to maintain flux. Based on RHR-solenoid, current flows CCW.
  \item Loop 2 initially has zero flux. When the circuit is turned on, current flows through loop 1 in a CW direction, and using RHR-solenoid it generates a B field down towards loop 2. As the field lines begin to enter loop 2, loop 2 has current begin to flow based on lenz law to try and maintain the initial zero flux so it makes a field upwards. Based on RHR-solenoid for loop 2, current would have to flow CCW around that loop which makes it go from X to Y.
  \item After a long time, the flux in loop 2 becomes constant and no emf is induced so no current flows. In circuit 1 , the loop simply acts as a wire and the current is set by the resistance and $\mathrm{V}=\mathrm{IR}$
  \item As the magnet moves down, flux increase in the down direction. Based on Lenz law, current in the loop would flow to create a field upwards to cancel the increasing downwards field. Using RHR-solenoid, the current would flow CCW. Then, when the magnet is pulled upwards, you have downward flux lines that are decreasing in magnitude so current flows to add more downward field to maintain flux. Using RHR-solenoid you now get CW.
  \item Since the wire is not cutting across the field lines, there is no force and no charge separation
  \item As the loop is pulled to the right, it loses flux lines right so current is generated by Lenz law to add more flux lines right. This newly created field to the right from the loop is in the same direction as the magnetic field so makes an attractive force pulling the magnet right also.
  \item Use a 1 second time period, the field would decrease to 2.5 T in that time.
\end{enumerate}

Then apply $\varepsilon=\Delta \Phi / \mathrm{t}$\\
$\varepsilon=\left(\mathrm{BA}_{\mathrm{f}}-\mathrm{BA}_{\mathrm{i}}\right) / \mathrm{t} \ldots \varepsilon=\mathrm{A}\left(\mathrm{B}_{\mathrm{f}}-\mathrm{B}_{\mathrm{i}}\right) \quad \ldots \quad \varepsilon=(0.4)(3-2.5) / 1$

1994B4.\\
a) $\mathrm{W}=\mathrm{K} \quad \ldots \quad \mathrm{Vq}=1 / 2 \mathrm{mv}^{2} \quad \ldots \quad \mathrm{~V}=\left(1.67 \times 10^{-27}\right)\left(3.1 \times 10^{6}\right)^{2} / 2\left(1.6 \times 10^{-19} \mathrm{C}\right) \quad \ldots \quad 50000 \mathrm{~V}$\\
b) Method I - The thermal energy produced by a single proton will be equal to the conversion of the kinetic energy into internal energy. The kinetic energy can be found with $1 / 2 \mathrm{mv}^{2}$ and the v is the same at the target as it was when it entered the B field.

For a single proton we have $1 / 2 \mathrm{mv}^{2}=1 / 2\left(1.67 \times 10^{-27}\right)\left(3.1 \times 10^{6}\right)^{2}=8 \times 10^{-15} \mathrm{~J}$.\\
Now we have to find out how many protons hit the target in 1 minute using the current.\\
$\mathrm{I}=\mathrm{Q} / \mathrm{t} \quad \ldots \quad 2 \times 10^{-6} \mathrm{Amp}=\mathrm{Q} / 60 \mathrm{sec} \quad \ldots \mathrm{Q}=1.2 \times 10^{-4} \mathrm{C}$ total charge.\\
$1.2 \times 10^{-4} \mathrm{C} / 1.6 \times 10^{-19} \mathrm{C} /$ proton $\rightarrow 7.5 \times 10^{14}$ protons.\\
Now multiply the number of protons by the energy for each one. $7.5 \times 10^{14} * 8 \times 10^{-15}=6 \mathrm{~J}$\\
Alternate (easier) solution - Find the power of the beam $\mathrm{P}=\mathrm{IV}$. Then, $\mathrm{W}=\mathrm{Pt}$, directly gives the energy that will be delivered in 1 minute. $\quad W=I V t=\left(2 \times 10^{-6}\right)(50000)(60)=6 \mathrm{~J}$\\

ii) Set the equilibrium position (at $\Delta \mathrm{x}=\mathrm{d}$ ) as zero spring energy to use the turn horizontal trick. This is the maximum speed location and we now set the kinetic energy here to the spring energy and the $\Delta \mathrm{d}$ stretch position.\\

Now sub in for k. $\quad v=\Delta d \sqrt{\frac{2 \frac{m g}{2 d}}{m}}=\Delta d \sqrt{\frac{g}{d}}$

v=\left(2^{*} 1.6 \times 10^{-19}\right)(0.09)(1.75 / 2) /\left(1.45 \times 10^{-25}\right)=1.74 \times 10^{5} \mathrm{~m} / \mathrm{s}
$$

d) The speed we are looking for is the speed when the charge exits the accelerating plates at the bottom of the diagram. In those plates:

$$
\mathrm{W}=\mathrm{K} \quad \ldots \quad \mathrm{Vq}=1 / 2 \mathrm{mv}^{2} \ldots \quad \mathrm{~V}\left(2 * 1.6 \times 10^{-19}\right)=1 / 2\left(1.45 \times 10^{-25}\right)\left(1.74 \times 10^{5}\right)^{2} \ldots \mathrm{~V}=6860 \mathrm{~V}
$$

\section*{2008B3.}
a) This circuit has two resistance elements that are in series on it: the internal resistance ( $0.5 \Omega$ ) and the wires resistance. First find the total resistance of the circuit $\quad \boldsymbol{\varepsilon}=\mathrm{IR}_{\text {tot }} \quad 16=4 \mathrm{R} \quad \mathrm{R}=4 \Omega$. Since the internal resistance makes up 0.5 of this total, the wires resistance must be $3.5 \Omega$ Now find the wires length with $\mathrm{R}=\rho \mathrm{L} / \mathrm{A} \quad 3.5=\left(1.7 \times 10^{-8}\right) \mathrm{L} / 3.5 \times 10^{-9} \quad \mathrm{~L}=0.72 \mathrm{~m}$\\
b) Tricky, this asks for the force on the magnet which is the opposite of the force on the wire. Based on action-reaction, the force on the magnet is equal and opposite of the force on the wire. Now finding the force on the wire: the field points right, the current is into the page as shown, so the RHR gives the force down on the wire. Therefore the force on the magnet would be up (this will make the scale reading lighter).\\
c) The change in the scales weight is caused by the magnetic force pulling up on the magnet and this extra force is exactly equal to $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{b}} \ldots$ so $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{b}}=\mathrm{BIL} \quad 0.06 \mathrm{~N}=\mathrm{B}(4)(0.02) \quad \mathrm{B}=0.75 \mathrm{~T}$\\
d) Since the length of the wire is not perpendicular, only a component of the B field is used to determine the force. So in the equation $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{b}}=\mathrm{BIL}$, the B value is reduced and for increasing lengths, there should be less and less force compared to the ideal line shown.\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-192}

Dotted line is the relationship for the misaligned wire.\\
e) The new graph shows decreasing magnetic force but the explanation in part d cannot be applied because the wire was not misaligned. This means there must be a reason that the B field was having lessened effects as the wire lengthened. Looking the original diagram in the problem, one explanation could be that as the wire segment in between the magnets got longer, it moved outward away from the poles of the $\mathrm{N}-\mathrm{S}$ magnet and some of the wire had a smaller field acting on it compared to the parts in the center of the magnet.

Another possible source of error is that as the bottom part of the loop gets longer, the sides would get shorter brining the top part of the loop lower down and the top part of the loop will begin to exert a force in the opposite direction lessening the net force on the wire.

\section*{B2008B3.}
a) Based on the RHR for a current wire creating a field, the magnetic field at the location in question is directed down (south). So the students reading was less than it should have been for the wire since the meter was measuring both fields and the earths field was acting against the wires field. If the earth's field was not present, the meter reading would have been larger. So each Field data point should be shifted up by the amount that the earths field had reduced it reading, as shown.\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-193(1)}\\
b) The field in the wire is given by $\mathrm{B}=\mu_{\mathrm{o}} \mathrm{I} /(2 \pi \mathrm{~d})$. Use one of the new data points.

$$
10 \times 10^{-4}=4 \pi \times 10^{-7}(\mathrm{I}) / 2 \pi(0.01) \quad \mathrm{I}=50 \mathrm{~A}
$$

c) To figure out the direction the needle points, we first need to determine how strong the wires field is with the 35 A current in the given location. $B=\mu_{0} I /(2 \pi d) \quad \ldots \quad B=4 \pi \times 10^{-7}(35) / 2 \pi(0.04) \quad \ldots \quad B=17.5 \times 10^{-5} T$, as compared to the earths field given in the problem, $5 \times 10^{-5} \mathrm{~T}$. Since the fields are on the same order of magnitude, the compass will not be totally overpowered by the external field and will point in a North-westerly direction though it would be angled more towards west since the external western field is larger.\\
d) Using the value of the earths field, and the value of the external field, we can determine the exact angle by setting them up head to tail.\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-193}

The angle $\theta$ is found using $\tan \theta=\mathrm{o} / \mathrm{a}$

$$
\begin{aligned}
& \tan \theta=17.5 \times 10^{-5} / 5 \times 10^{-5} \\
& \theta=74 \text { degrees }
\end{aligned}
$$

e) $\mathrm{V}=\mathrm{IR} \quad \ldots \quad 120=35 \mathrm{R} \quad \ldots \quad \mathrm{R}=3.4 \Omega$\\
f) Rate of energy is power. $P=I V=(35)(120)=4200 \mathrm{~W}$

\section*{B2009B2.}
a) The distance between A and B on each side is a diagonal and can be found using the graph and Pythagorean theorem $\ldots x^{2}+y^{2}=d^{2} \ldots(0.04)^{2}+(0.03)^{2}=d^{2} \ldots d=0.05 m(3-4-5$ triangle $)$. Since charge $q_{a}$ is equidistant from both $\mathrm{q}_{\mathrm{b}}$ ' s , and the charges on either side are identical since they are both $\mathrm{q}_{\mathrm{b}}$, the forces on $\mathrm{q}_{\mathrm{a}}$ from each $\mathrm{q}_{\mathrm{b}}$ are identical in magnitude and given by $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{e}}=\mathrm{k} \mathrm{q}_{\mathrm{a}} \mathrm{q}_{\mathrm{b}} / \mathrm{r}^{2}=\left(9 \times 10^{9}\right)\left(0.2 \times 10^{-9}\right)\left(0.3 \times 10^{-9}\right) /(0.05)^{2}=2.16 \times 10^{-7} \mathrm{~N}$. Each force acts in the direction down along the diagonal in the 3-4-5 triangle from $\mathrm{A}-\mathrm{B}$ at $53.13^{\circ}$ measured away from the $y$ axis. Since both forces are equal and act at the same angle, the $x$ components of these forces cancel leaving only the y components to add together. So the total net force is simply double the y component of one of the forces. $\rightarrow 2 \mathrm{~F}_{\mathrm{y}}=2 * 2.16 \times 10^{-7}(\cos 53.13)=2.6 \times 10^{-7} \mathrm{~N}$ directed down.\\
b) The x components of the forces on particle $a$ will always cancel so it will only move along the y axis. It will be accelerated down along the axis and at the origin the net force will be zero. It will move past the origin on the negative $y$ axis at which point the force starts to pull upwards towards $+y$ and it will slow down until it stops and is pulled back up towards the origin again. It will oscillate up and down.\\
c) Based on the LHR for the negative charge and the given v and B , the force on the particle would be directed towards the left initially and act as a centripetal force to make the particle circle.\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-194}

Depending on the strength and width of the B field, the particle may take a different radius turn in the field.\\
d) $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{b}}=\mathrm{qvB}=\left(0.2 \times 10^{-9}\right)(6000)(0.5)=6 \times 10^{-7} \mathrm{~N}$\\
e) $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{e}}=\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{b}} \quad \ldots \quad \mathrm{Eq}=\mathrm{qvB} \quad \ldots \quad \mathrm{E}=\mathrm{vB} \quad \ldots \quad \mathrm{E}=(6000)(0.5)=3000 \mathrm{~N} / \mathrm{C}$.

The force would need to oppose the magnetic force and point to the right. Since this is a negative charge, the E field would have to point left to create a rightward electric force.

\section*{C1983E3.}
a) The field from a wire is given by $\mathrm{B}=\mu_{\mathrm{o}} \mathrm{I}$ / ( $2 \pi \mathrm{R}$ ), with R and I equal for both wires at point O . Based on the RHR for the current wires, the right wire makes a field down and the left wire makes a field up so cancel to zero.\\
b)\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-195}

Based on the RHR, the resultant fields from each wire are directed as shown. Since the distance to each wire is the same, the resultant B field will simply be twice the x component of one of the wire's B fields.

The distance to point N is $\sqrt{a^{2}+y^{2}}$ so the total field at that location from a single wire is $B=\frac{\mu_{o} I}{2 \pi \sqrt{a^{2}+y^{2}}}$

The x component of that field is given by $\mathrm{B} \cos \theta$, where $\cos \theta$ can be replaced with $\cos \theta=\mathrm{a} / \mathrm{h}=\mathrm{y} / \sqrt{a^{2}+y^{2}}$

Giving $\mathrm{B}_{\text {net }}=2 \mathrm{~B} \cos \theta=2 \mathrm{By} / \sqrt{a^{2}+y^{2}}=\frac{2 y \mu_{o} I}{2 \pi \sqrt{a^{2}+y^{2}} \sqrt{a^{2}+y^{2}}}=\frac{y \mu_{o} I}{\pi\left(a^{2}+y^{2}\right)}$

c) Using region III $\ldots \mathrm{F}_{\text {net }(\mathrm{C})}=\mathrm{mv}^{2} / \mathrm{r} \ldots \mathrm{qvB}=\mathrm{mv}^{2} / \mathrm{r} \ldots \mathrm{m}=\mathrm{QBR} / \mathrm{v}$ (sub in v ) $\ldots=\mathrm{QB}{ }^{2} \mathrm{R} / \mathrm{E}$\\

e) In region three, the acceleration is the centripetal acceleration. $a_{c}=v^{2} / R \ldots$ (sub in $v$ ) ... $E^{2} / R B^{2}$\\
f) Time of travel can be found with $\mathrm{v}=\mathrm{d} / \mathrm{t}$ with the distance as half the circumference ( $2 \pi \mathrm{R} / 2$ ) then sub in v giving $\ldots \mathrm{t}=\pi \mathrm{RB} / \mathrm{E}$

\section*{Supplemental.}
a) i) Parabolic. The electrons have constant speed to the right. The constant electric force provides a constant acceleration toward the top of the page. This is similar to a projectile under the influence of gravity, so the shape is parabolic.\\
ii) Down. To create path 1, the electric force must be toward the top of the page. The electron is negatively charged, so the field must point in the opposite direction to the electric force.\\
b) i) Circular. The magnetic force is always perpendicular to the velocity of the electrons and has constant magnitude. Thus it acts as a centripetal force making the electrons follow a circular path.\\
ii) Into the page. To create path 3, the initial magnetic force must be toward the bottom of the page. With the initial velocity to the right, the right-hand rule gives a field pointing out of the page. But the electron is negatively charged, so the field must point in the opposite direction.\\
c) $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{e}}=\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{b}} \quad \ldots \quad \mathrm{Eq}=\mathrm{qvB} \quad \ldots \quad \mathrm{v}=\mathrm{E} / \mathrm{B} \quad \ldots \quad \mathrm{v}=\left(3.4 \times 10^{-4}\right) /\left(2 \times 10^{-3}\right)=1.7 \times 10^{7} \mathrm{~m} / \mathrm{s}$\\
d) $\mathrm{W}=\mathrm{K} \quad \ldots \quad \mathrm{Vq}=1 / 2 \mathrm{mv}^{2} \quad \ldots \mathrm{~V}=\mathrm{mv}^{2} / 2 \mathrm{Q} \quad \ldots \quad \mathrm{V}=\left(9.11 \times 10^{-31}\right)\left(1.7 \times 10^{7}\right)^{2} / 2\left(1.6 \times 10^{-19}\right)=823 \mathrm{~V}$

\section*{1978B4.}

To find the current, find the emf induced in the bar then use $\mathrm{V}=\mathrm{IR} \quad \ldots \quad \varepsilon=\mathrm{Blv} \quad \ldots \quad \mathrm{IR}=\mathrm{Blv} \quad \ldots \quad(2)(3)=(2)(2) \mathrm{v} \quad \ldots \quad \mathrm{v}=1.5 \mathrm{~m} / \mathrm{s}$\\
b) When the bar slides, it will experience a magnetic force pushing left based on the RHR so a pulling force equal to this magnetic force would need to be applied to move the bar at a constant $\mathrm{v} . \mathrm{F}=\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{b}}=\mathrm{BIL}=(2)(2)(2)=8 \mathrm{~N}$\\
c) Rate of heat dissipated $=$ rate of work $=$ power. In resistor, $\mathrm{P}=\mathrm{I}^{2} \mathrm{R}=(2)^{2}(3)=12 \Omega$.

Mechanical power $=\mathrm{P}=\mathrm{Fv}=(8)(1.5)=12 \Omega$.\\
d) All of the kinetic energy will be removed and that is the amount dissipated. $\mathrm{K}=1 / 2 \mathrm{mv}^{2}=1 / 2(4)(1.5)^{2}=4.5 \mathrm{~J}$

c) First, the directions are found based on Lenz Law. From 0-1 the downward flux is decreasing so current flows CW to add back the downward field. Then we hit zero flux at 1 second. Moving 1-2 seconds we want to maintain the zero flux and we have an increasing upwards flux so the current still flows CW to add downward field to cancel the gaining flux. At 2 seconds the flux becomes constant so current does not flow up until 4 seconds. From 4-6 seconds we are loosing upwards flux so current flows CCW to add back that upwards field.

To determine the current magnitudes. Use V=IR.\\
From $0-2$ sec. $\quad 0.06=I(0.2) \quad I=0.3 \mathrm{~A}$\\
From 2-4, $\mathrm{I}=0$.\\
From 4-6, first determine new emf. Since the slope is half as much as $0-2 \sec$, the emf should be half as much as well. Then V=IR ... $0.03=\mathrm{I}(0.2)$

$$
\mathrm{I}=0.15 \mathrm{~A}
$$

\section*{C1973E3.}
a) The rod forms a loop with the upper part of the rails. With $\mathrm{V}=\mathrm{IR} \ldots$ This gives, $\mathrm{IR}=\mathrm{BLv} \cos \theta$ and the current is $\mathrm{I}=\mathrm{BLv} \cos \theta / \mathrm{R}$\\
c) $\mathrm{F}=\mathrm{BIL}$, again using $\mathrm{B} \cos \theta$ we have $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{b}}=\mathrm{B} \cos \theta \mathrm{I} \mathrm{L}$, now sub in I from above. $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{b}}=\mathrm{B} \cos \theta(\mathrm{BLv} \cos \theta / \mathrm{R}) \mathrm{L}$ $F_{b}=B^{2} L^{2} v \cos ^{2} \theta / R$. Based on the RHR for the bar, the force acts up the inclined rails parallel to them.\\
d) The terminal velocity will occur when $\mathrm{F}_{\text {net }(\mathrm{x})}=0$ with x being the direction along the rails. This gives: $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{gx}}=\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{b}} \quad \mathrm{mg} \sin \theta=\mathrm{B}^{2} \mathrm{~L}^{2} \mathrm{v} \cos ^{2} \theta / \mathrm{R} \quad \mathrm{v}=\mathrm{mg} \sin \theta \mathrm{R} / \mathrm{B}^{2} \mathrm{~L}^{2} \cos ^{2} \theta$

$(\mathrm{M}-\Delta \mathrm{m}) \mathrm{g}=\mathrm{BIL}$\\
$\mathrm{Mg}-\Delta \mathrm{mg}=\mathrm{B}(\mathrm{Mg} / \mathrm{BL}-\mathrm{BLv} / \mathrm{R}) \mathrm{L}$\\
$\mathrm{Mg}-\Delta \mathrm{mg}=\mathrm{Mg}-\mathrm{B}^{2} \mathrm{~L}^{2} \mathrm{v} / \mathrm{R}$\\
$\Delta \mathrm{m}=\mathrm{B}^{2} \mathrm{~L}^{2} \mathrm{v} / \mathrm{Rg}$

\section*{C1991E3.}
a) i) Consider the wire as a tube full of charges and focus on a single charge in the tube. That single charge is moving to the right in a B field pointing into the page. Using the RHR, that charge is pushed up to the satellite so the shuttle side is negative.\\
ii) Induced emf is given by $\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}=\mathrm{BLv}=\left(3.3 \times 10^{-5}\right)(20000 \mathrm{~m})(7600)=5016 \mathrm{~V}$\\
b) $V=I R$\\
$5016=\mathrm{I}(10000)$ $\mathrm{I}=0.5016 \mathrm{~A}$\\
c) i) $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{b}}=\mathrm{BIL}=\left(3.3 \times 10^{-5}\right)(0.5016)(20000) \quad \mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{b}}=0.331 \mathrm{~N}$\\
ii) The current flows up, away from the shuttle as indicated. Using the RHR for the given I and B gives the force direction on the wire pointing left which is opposite of the shuttles velocity.

\section*{C1998E3.}
a) At constant speed $\mathrm{F}_{\text {net }}=0 \quad \mathrm{~F}_{\mathrm{b}}=\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{gx}} \quad \mathrm{BIL}=\mathrm{mg} \sin \theta \quad \mathrm{I}=\mathrm{mg} \sin \theta / \mathrm{BL}$\\
b) Using the induced emf and equating to $\mathrm{V}=\mathrm{IR}$ we have\\
$\mathrm{IR}=\mathrm{BLv}$, sub in I from above $(\mathrm{mg} \sin \theta / \mathrm{BL}) \mathrm{R}=\mathrm{BLv} \quad \ldots$ solve for $\mathrm{v}=\mathrm{mgR} \sin \theta / \mathrm{B}^{2} \mathrm{~L}^{2}$\\
c) Rate of energy is power. P $=\mathrm{I}^{2} \mathrm{R} \quad \mathrm{P}=(\mathrm{mg} \sin \theta / \mathrm{BL})^{2} \mathrm{R}$\\
d) Since the resistor is placed between the rails at the bottom, it is now in parallel with the top resistor because the current has two pathways to chose, the top loop with resistor R or the new bottom loop with the new resistor R . This effectively decreases the total resistance of the circuit. Based on the formula found in part b, lower resistance equates to less velocity.

\section*{C2003E3.}
a) Looking at the side view and then transferring back to the top view, we see that in the top view the magnetic field is basically pointing into the page. The component of the magnetic field we are concerned with actually does point directly into the page. Now using the LHR rule for the electrons they get pushed down, which is towards east.\\
b) The emf is induced and given by $\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}=\mathrm{Blv}_{\perp}$. The perpendicular B is $\mathrm{B} \sin \theta$ as can be seen in the side view $\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}=\mathrm{B} \sin \theta \mathrm{Lv}=\left(6 \times 10^{-5}\right)(\sin 55)(15)(75)=.055 \mathrm{~V}$\\
c) To induce a current, the flux needs to change. The earths magnetic field strength cannot be changed, but the component of that B field in the enclosed area can be altered which will change the flux and induce current flow. In order to do this, the plane could rise up, or down, or to sustain a current the plane could follow a wavelike pattern.\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-201}

Or the plane could rock its wings back and forth. However, simply turning left or right would not work.

Direction: Let's say the plane rises up, this will increase the ${ }_{\perp}$ component of the B field in the loop so current will flow to reduce this increased downward field in the loop. Current flows CCW to create and upwards field to cancel out the increased downwards B field based on Lenz Law.

A) The wood.\\
B) The iron.\\
C) Both have the same buoyant force.\\
D) Cannot be determined without knowing their densities.\\

Position 3: The copper is completely submerged, but just above the bottom surface of the container.\\
Assume that the water is incompressible. What is the ranking of the buoyant forces $(B)$ acting on the copper blocks for these positions, from least to greatest?\\
(A) $B_{1}<B_{2}<B_{3}$\\
(B) $B_{3}<B_{2}<B_{1}$\\
(C) $B_{1}=B_{2}=B_{3}$\\
(D) $B_{1}<B_{2}=B_{3}$\\

(A) The buoyant force is greater on Object K since it has a lower density and displaces more water.\\
(B) The buoyant force is greater on Object K since it has lower density and lower density objects always float "higher" in the fluid.\\
(C) The buoyant force is greater on Object L since it is denser than K and therefore "heavier."\\
(D) The buoyant forces are equal on the objects since they have equal mass.\\

A drinking fountain projects water at an initial angle of $50^{\circ}$ above the horizontal, and the water reaches a maximum height of 0.150 m above the point of exit. Assume air resistance is negligible.\\
(a) Calculate the speed at which the water leaves the fountain.\\
(b) The radius of the fountain's exit hole is $4.00 \times 10^{-3} \mathrm{~m}$. Calculate the volume rate of flow of the water.\\
(c) The fountain is fed by a pipe that at one point has a radius of $7.00 \times 10^{-3} \mathrm{~m}$ and is 3.00 m below the fountain's opening. The density of water is $1.0 \times 10^{3} \mathrm{~kg} / \mathrm{m}^{3}$. Calculate the gauge pressure in the feeder pipe at this point.

An underground pipe carries water of density $1000 \mathrm{~kg} / \mathrm{m}^{3}$ to a fountain at ground level, as shown above. At ground level, the pipe has a cross-sectional area of $0.50 \times 10^{-4} \mathrm{~m}^{2}$. The water leaves the pipe at point $B$ at a speed of $8.2 \mathrm{~m} / \mathrm{s}$.\\

\section*{Supplemental Problems}
SUP1. A block of wood has a mass of 12 kg and dimensions 0.5 m by 0.2 m by 0.2 m .\\
(a) Find the density $\rho_{0}$ of the wooden block.\\
(b) If the block is placed in water $\left(\rho=1000 \mathrm{~kg} / \mathrm{m}^{3}\right)$ with the square sides parallel to the water surface, how far beneath the surface of the water is the bottom of the block?\\
(c) A weight is placed on the top of the block. The block sinks to a point that the top of the block is exactly even with the water surface. Find the mass of the added weight.

SUP2. A tapered horizontal pipe carries water from one building to another on the same level. The wider end has a cross-sectional area of $4 \mathrm{~m}^{2}$. The narrower end has a cross-sectional area of $2 \mathrm{~m}^{2}$. Water enters the wider end at a velocity of $10 \mathrm{~m} / \mathrm{sec}$.\\
(a) What is the speed of the water at the narrow end of the pipe?\\
(b) The gauge pressure of the water at the wide end of the pipe is $2 \times 10^{5}$ pascals. Using Bernoulli's equation, find the gauge pressure at the narrow end of the pipe.

SUP3. A small airplane has wings with surface area $9 \mathrm{~m}^{2}$ each. The speed of the air across the top of the wing is $50 \mathrm{~m} / \mathrm{sec}$, and across the bottom of the wing, $40 \mathrm{~m} / \mathrm{sec}$. Take the density of air to be $1.2 \mathrm{~kg} / \mathrm{m}^{3}$.\\
(a) Find the difference in the pressure between the top and the bottom of the wing.\\
(b) i) Find the net lift upward on the plane.\\
ii) If there is no other lift on the plane, what would be the mass of the plane? Assume the plane is not accelerating up or down.

SUP4. A block of wood floats in water, with $2 / 3$ of it submerged. The wood is then placed in oil, and $9 / 10$ of it is submerged. Find the density of the wood, and of the oil.

\section*{Solution}
\begin{enumerate}
  \item $\quad \mathrm{FBD}$ has $\mathrm{F}_{t}$ pointing down $\mathrm{F}_{b}$ pointing up and weight ( mg ) down. $\mathrm{F}_{\text {net }}=0 \quad \mathrm{~F}_{b}-\mathrm{F}_{t}-\mathrm{mg}=0$
\end{enumerate}

The buoyant force is given by the weight of the displaced water. Since the waters displaced volume is equal to the corks displaced volume and the water weight for the same volume would be 4 times heavier (based on the given cork weight $=25 \%$ water weight) compared to the cork, the buoyant force is equal to 4 x the cork weight $=4 \mathrm{mg}$. Using the force equation created initially. $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{t}}=\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{b}}-\mathrm{mg}=4 \mathrm{mg}-\mathrm{mg}=3 \mathrm{mg}$\\
2. Using fluid continuity. $\mathrm{A}_{1} \mathrm{v}_{1}=\mathrm{A}_{2} \mathrm{v}_{2} \quad \pi \mathrm{R}^{2} \mathrm{v}_{1}=\pi(2 \mathrm{R})^{2} \mathrm{v}_{2} \quad \mathrm{v}_{1}=4 \mathrm{v}_{2}$\\
3. This is based on two principles. 1 - Bernoulli's principle says that when speed increases pressure drops. Second, continuity says more area means less speed based on $A_{1} v_{1}=A_{2} v_{2}$ So the smallest area would have the largest speed and therefore most pressure drop.\\
4. Since A and B have the same mass and density, they have the same volume. C has the same volume as A and B since it's the same shape as B. So all three objects have the same volume. When submerged, they will all displace the same amount of water and therefore all have the same buoyant force acting on them. Note: if the objects were floating instead of submerged then the heavier ones would have larger buoyant forces.\\
5. Buoyant force is equal to weight of displaced fluid. Since the density is constant and the volume displaced is always the same, the buoyant force stays constant\\
6. The wood is floating and is only partially submerged. It does not displace a weight of water related to its entire volume. The iron however is totally submerged and does displace a weight of water equal to its entire volume. Since the iron displaces more water, it has a larger buoyant force acting on it.\\
7. For floating objects, the weight of the displaced fluid equals the weight of the object. For a more dense fluid, less of that fluid needs to be displaced to create a fluid weight equal to the weight of the object. Since the salt water is more dense, it will not need as much displaced.\\
8. More area $\rightarrow$ less speed $\rightarrow$ more pressure\\
9. Flow continuity. $\mathrm{A}_{1} \mathrm{v}_{1}=\mathrm{A}_{2} \mathrm{v}_{2} \quad \pi(0.02)^{2}(1)=\pi(0.01)^{2} \mathrm{v}_{2}$\\
10. Buoyant force is based on how much weight of water is displaced. Since all three are completely submerged they all displace the same amount of water so have equal buoyant forces.\\
11. For floating objects, the buoyant force equals the weight of the objects. Since each object has the same weight, they must have the same buoyant force to counteract that weight and make them float. IF the equal mass objects sunk, then the one with the smaller density would have a larger volume and displace more water so have a larger buoyant force. But that is not the case here.\\
12. Three forces act on the block, $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{t}}$ down, mg down and $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{b}}$ up. $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{net}}=0 \quad \mathrm{~F}_{\mathrm{b}}-\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{t}}-\mathrm{mg}=0$

$$
\begin{array}{ll}
\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{b}}-3-5=0 & \mathrm{~F}_{\mathrm{b}}=8 \mathrm{~N}-\text { weight of displaced water }=\rho_{\mathrm{h} 20} \mathrm{~V}_{\text {disp }} \mathrm{g} \\
& 8=(1000) \mathrm{V}(10) \rightarrow \mathrm{V}=0.0008 \mathrm{~m}^{3}
\end{array}
$$

\begin{enumerate}
  \setcounter{enumi}{12}
  \item For floating objects\\
$\mathrm{mg}=\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{b}}$
\end{enumerate}

$$
\begin{aligned}
& \rho_{\text {obj }} \mathrm{V}_{\text {obj }} \mathrm{g}=\rho_{\mathrm{h} 20} \mathrm{~V}_{\text {disp }} \mathrm{g} \\
& \rho_{\text {obj }}(\mathrm{V}) \mathrm{g}=1000(0.6 \mathrm{~V}) \mathrm{g}
\end{aligned}
$$

$$
\rho_{\mathrm{obj}}=600
$$

In oil the same is true

$$
\rho_{\text {obj }} \mathrm{V}_{\text {obj }} \mathrm{g}=\rho_{\text {oil }} \mathrm{V}_{\text {disp }} \mathrm{g} \quad(600) \mathrm{Vg}=(800) \mathrm{x} \% \mathrm{Vg} \quad \mathrm{x} \%=0.75
$$

\begin{enumerate}
  \setcounter{enumi}{13}
  \item Using fluid continuity. $\mathrm{A}_{1} \mathrm{v}_{1}=\mathrm{A}_{2} \mathrm{v}_{2} \quad \pi \mathrm{R}^{2} \mathrm{v}_{1}=\pi(2 \mathrm{R})^{2} \mathrm{v}_{2} \quad \mathrm{v}_{1}=4 \mathrm{v}_{2} \quad \mathrm{~A}$
  \item Based on the continuity principle, less area means more speed and based on Bernoulli's principle, B\\
more speed means less pressure
  \item The weight of the object The weight of the mass is 4 N . The scale reading apparent weight is $3 \mathrm{~N} \quad \mathrm{~B}$\\
so there must be a 1 N buoyant force acting to produce this result.
  \item . Since the pressure in a fluid is only dependent on the depth, they all have the same fluid\\
pressure at the base. Since all of the bases have the same area and the same liquid pressure there, the force of the liquid given by $\mathrm{P}=\mathrm{F} / \mathrm{A}$ would be the same for all containers. Note: IF instead this question asked for the pressure of the container on the floor below it, the container with more total mass in it would create a greater pressure, but that is not the case here.
  \item As the fluid flows into the smaller area constriction, its speed increases and therefore the pressure drops. Since the pressure in the constriction is less than that outside at the water surface, fluid is forced up into the lower tube.
  \item Both objects are more dense than water and will sink in the pool. Since both have the same volume, they will displace the same amount of water and will have the same buoyant forces.
  \item Again both samples sink. Also, both samples have the same mass but different densities. For the same mass, a smaller density must have a larger volume, and the larger volume displaces more water making a larger buoyant force. So the smaller density with the larger volume has a larger buoyant force.
  \item V of this ball is $4 / 3 \pi \mathrm{r}^{3}=4 / 3 \pi(0.4)^{3}=0.2681 \mathrm{~m}^{3}$. For the ball to just sink, it is on the verge of floating, meaning the weight of the ball equals the buoyant force of the fully submerged ball. $\mathrm{mg}=\rho_{\mathrm{fl}} \mathrm{V}_{\text {disp }} \mathrm{g} \quad \mathrm{m}(10)=1400(0.2681)(10) \quad \mathrm{m}=375 \mathrm{~kg}$
  \item Using fluid continuity. $\mathrm{A}_{1} \mathrm{v}_{1}=\mathrm{A}_{2} \mathrm{v}_{2} \quad \pi(7 \mathrm{R})^{2} \mathrm{v}_{1}=\pi(\mathrm{R})^{2} \mathrm{~V} \quad \mathrm{v}_{1}=\mathrm{V} / 49 \quad \mathrm{~A}$
  \item The fluid flow is occurring in a situation similar to the diagram for question \#27. B\\
Apply Bernoulli's equation. $P_{1}+\rho g y_{1}+1 / 2 \rho v_{1}{ }^{2}=P_{2}+\rho g y_{2}+1 / 2 \rho v_{2}{ }^{2}$ Apply Bernoulli's equation. $P_{1}+\rho g y_{1}+1 / 2 \rho v_{1}^{2}=P_{2}+\rho g y_{2}+1 / 2 \rho v_{2}^{2}$
\end{enumerate}

$$
\begin{aligned}

& P_{2}=P+1 / 2 \rho v^{2}-1 / 24 \rho v^{2}-\rho g y

\section*{2002B6.}
a) Example 1: Measure the unstretched length of the spring. Hang it with the object at rest and measure the stretched length. Call the difference in these lengths $\Delta \mathrm{x}$. Equating the weight of the object and the force exerted by the extended spring gives $m g=k \Delta \mathrm{x}$ from which $k$ can be determined.\\
Example 2: Set the hanging mass into oscillation. Determine the period $T$ by timing $n$ oscillations and dividing that time by $n$. The equation $T=2 \pi \sqrt{ } \mathrm{~m} / \mathrm{k}$ can then be used to find $k$.\\
b) The spring is stretched less when the object is at rest in the fluid. The fluid exerts an upward buoyant force on the object. Since the net force on the object is still zero, the spring does not need to exert as much force as before and thus stretches less.

\section*{c\&d)}
\begin{enumerate}
  \item Measure the length of the spring when the object is immersed in the liquid, and subtract the unstretched length to determine the amount the spring is stretched. This will allow calculation of the force exerted by the spring on the object.
  \item The volume of fluid displaced is equal to the volume of the object, which can be determined from the given mass and density of the object.
  \item The buoyant force on the object is equal to the difference of the object's weight and the force exerted by the spring.
  \item The buoyant force also equals the weight of the displaced fluid, which equals the product of the fluid density, displaced volume, and $g$.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{cll}
Symbol &  & Quantity \\
\cline { 1 - 1 }
$\rho$ &  & fluid density \\
$V$ &  & object volume $=$ displaced water volume \\
$g$ &  & acceleration of gravity \\
$m$ &  & mass of object \\
$x$ &  & spring stretch in air \\
$x_{w}$ &  & spring stretch in water \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

First solving for k in air. $\mathrm{mg}=\mathrm{kx}$\\
Then in the fluid. $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{sp}}=\mathrm{kx}_{\mathrm{w}}$\\
$\mathrm{F}_{\text {net }}=0 \quad \mathrm{~F}_{\mathrm{b}}=\mathrm{mg}-\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{sp}} \quad \rho \mathrm{Vg}=\mathrm{mg}^{-} \mathrm{kx}_{\mathrm{w}} \quad \rho \mathrm{Vg}=\mathrm{mg}-(\mathrm{mg} / \mathrm{x}) \mathrm{x}_{\mathrm{w}} \quad$ solve for $\rho$

ii) The force needed to move the water $=$ the weight of the water $(\mathrm{mg})$.

Using. $\mathrm{P}=\mathrm{Fd} / \mathrm{t}=(350)(9.8)(85) /(2 \mathrm{hrs} * 3600 \mathrm{~s} / \mathrm{hr})=40.5 \mathrm{~W}$\\
b) i) Using fluid continuity. $\mathrm{A}_{1} \mathrm{v}_{1}=\mathrm{A}_{2} \mathrm{v}_{2} \quad \pi(.03 / 2)^{2}(0.5)=\pi(0.0125 / 2)^{2} \mathrm{v}_{2} \quad \mathrm{v}_{2}=2.88 \mathrm{~m} / \mathrm{s}$\\

$\mathrm{y}_{1}=0 \quad \mathrm{P}_{2}=$ atmospheric (open faucet)\\
$\mathrm{P}_{1}+0+1 / 2(1000)(0.5)^{2}=1.01 \times 10^{5}+(1000)(9.8)(85)+1 / 2(1000)(2.88)^{2} \quad \mathrm{P}_{1}=938000 \mathrm{~Pa}$

b) $\mathrm{P}_{\text {abs }}=\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{o}}+\rho \mathrm{gh}=1.01 \times 10^{5}+343,000=452,600 \mathrm{~Pa}$\\

$\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{t}}+\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{b}}-\mathrm{mg}=0 \quad \mathrm{~F}_{\mathrm{t}}=\mathrm{mg}-\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{b}} \quad \mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{t}}=\left(\rho_{\text {obj }} \mathrm{V}_{\text {obj }}\right) \mathrm{g}-\left(\rho_{\mathrm{h} 20} \mathrm{~V}_{\text {disp }}\right) \mathrm{g} \quad \mathrm{V}_{\text {disp }}=\mathrm{V}_{\text {obj }}$ call it V\\
$\rightarrow \mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{t}}=\operatorname{Vg}\left(\rho_{\mathrm{al}}-\rho_{\mathrm{h} 20}\right)=(1 \times 2 \times 0.03)(9.8)(2700-1025)=985 \mathrm{~N}$\\
d) $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{t}}+\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{b}}-\mathrm{mg}=\mathrm{ma} \quad \mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{t}}=\mathrm{mg}-\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{b}}+\mathrm{ma}$. Comparing this tension equation to the one in part c you see that the tension will increase since the quantity "ma" is being added here

2004B2.\\
a) $P_{\text {abs }}=P_{o}+P_{\text {gauge }}$\\
$413 \mathrm{~atm}=1 \mathrm{~atm}-\mathrm{P}_{\text {gauge }}$\\
$\mathrm{P}_{\text {gauge }}=412 \mathrm{~atm}$\\
b) $P_{\text {gauge }}=\rho g h$\\
$412\left(1.01 \times 10^{5}\right)=1024(9.8)(\mathrm{h})$\\
$\mathrm{h}=4140 \mathrm{~m}$\\
c) The fluid pressures acting are the outside water pressure (which includes the atmosphere at the surface acting down on it) and, the inside air pressure which is atmospheric. Since the atmospheric pressure acts both inside and is also included in the water pressure, the net force due to fluid pressure can be found by using the water's gauge pressure since the air pressures effectively cancel each other out.\\
$P_{\text {gauge }}=F / A$\\
$412\left(1.01 \times 10^{5}\right)=\mathrm{F} / 0.01$\\
$\mathrm{F}=416,000 \mathrm{~N}$\\
d) The force from c is not the true net force. The actual net force is zero as the window is at rest. This force from c is due to fluid pressures and is resisted by normal forces acting on the edges of the window where it is connected to the submarine.\\
e) $v_{f}=v_{i}+$ at\\
$10=0+\mathrm{a}(30)$\\
$\mathrm{a}=0.33 \mathrm{~m} / \mathrm{s}^{2}$\\
f) $d=v_{i} t+1 / 2 a t^{2}$\\
$d=0+1 / 2(0.33)(30)^{2}$\\
$\mathrm{d}=150 \mathrm{~m}$\\
g) The total depth is 4140 m . There are two parts to the trip, the first 150 m covered while accelerating and the second (3990m) covered while moving at constant speed. The parts must be calculated separately. Part one, during acceleration, was already given as taking 30 second. The second part at a constant speed can simply be found using $\bar{v}=\mathrm{d} / \mathrm{t}, \quad 10=3990 / \mathrm{t}, \quad \mathrm{t}_{2}=399$ seconds. So the total time of travel was 429 seconds.

\section*{B2004B2.}
a) The descent occurs at two different accelerations and must be analyzed in the two sections.

Section 1 starts from rest and accelerates, find the time in that part\\
$\mathrm{v}_{1 \mathrm{f}}=\mathrm{v}_{1 \mathrm{i}}+\mathrm{a}_{1} \mathrm{t}_{1}$\\
$2=0+0.10 \mathrm{t}$\\
$\mathrm{t}_{1}=20$ seconds.\\
$\mathrm{d}_{1}=\mathrm{v}_{1 \mathrm{i}} \mathrm{t}+1 / 2 \mathrm{a}_{1} \mathrm{t}_{1}{ }^{2}$\\
$\mathrm{d}_{1}=1 / 2(0.10)(20)^{2}$\\
$\mathrm{d}_{1}=20 \mathrm{~m}$\\
Section 2 occurs at a constant speed equal to the final speed in section 1 and will occur over the remaining distance $\mathrm{d}_{2}=60 \mathrm{~m}$.\\
$v_{2}=\mathrm{d}_{2} / \mathrm{t}_{2}$\\
$2=60 / \mathrm{t}_{2}$\\
$\mathrm{t}_{2}=30$ seconds\\
$\mathrm{t}_{\text {total }}=\mathrm{t}_{1}+\mathrm{t}_{2}=50$ seconds\\
b) Weight of water above the bell is a cylindrical column with a height of $\mathrm{h}=80 \mathrm{~m}$ and area of $\mathrm{A}=9 \mathrm{~m}^{2}$. This gives us the volume of the water above the bell given by $\mathrm{V}=\mathrm{Ah}=720 \mathrm{~m}^{3}$.\\
The weight of this column $=\mathrm{m}_{\mathrm{h} 20} \mathrm{~g}=\left(\rho_{\mathrm{h} 20} \mathrm{~V}\right) \mathrm{g}=(1025)(720)(9.8)=7.2 \times 10^{5} \mathrm{~N}$\\
c) $P_{a b s}=P_{o}+\rho g h=1.01 \times 10^{5}+(1025)(9.8)(80)=9 \times 10^{5} \mathrm{~Pa}$\\
d) Since there is air pressure inside the bell, and the absolute pressure on the outside also includes the air pressure, these two pressures essentially cancel each other out and we only need to push against the water pressure alone so we should use the gauge pressure to find the needed force.\\
$\mathrm{P}_{\text {abs }}=\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{o}}+\mathrm{P}_{\text {gauge }} 9 \times 10^{5}=1.01 \times 10^{5}+\mathrm{P}_{\text {gauge }}$

$$
\begin{aligned}
& P_{\text {gauge }}=8 \times 10^{5} \mathrm{~Pa} \\
& F=P A=\left(8 \times 10^{5}\right)\left(\pi(0.25)^{2}\right)=1.58 \times 10^{5} \mathrm{~N}
\end{aligned}
$$

e) To reduce the pushing force needed, you could increase the pressure inside the bell to create a smaller pressure difference between inside and outside. Or, by making the area of the hatch smaller the pushing force would be less. Or, you could use a lever inside that uses torque to provide mechanical advantage to amplify an applied force to one side of the lever. This would makes the force pushing the hatch open the same but the required pushing force of a person less.

\section*{2005B5.}
a) We are given the volume of the raft and the surface area as well. Use this to first find the total height of the raft $h_{t}$

$$
\mathrm{V}=\mathrm{Ah}_{\mathrm{t}} \quad 1.8=8.2 \mathrm{~h}_{\mathrm{t}} \quad \mathrm{~h}_{\mathrm{t}}=0.22 \mathrm{~m}
$$

Since the raft is floating, the weight of the raft must equal the weight of the displaced fluid. We will define " $h_{s}$ " as being the portion of the height of the raft below the water so that the displaced volume is given by $\mathrm{V}=\mathrm{Ah}_{\mathrm{s}}$\\
$\mathrm{m}_{\text {raft }} \mathrm{g}=\rho_{\mathrm{h} 20} \mathrm{~V}_{\text {disp }} \mathrm{g}$\\
$\rho_{\text {raft }} \mathrm{V}_{\text {raft }} \mathrm{g}=\rho_{\mathrm{h} 20} \mathrm{~V}_{\text {disp }} \mathrm{g} \quad \rho_{\text {raft }} \mathrm{V}_{\text {raft }}=\rho_{\mathrm{h} 20}\left(\mathrm{Ah}_{\mathrm{s}}\right) \quad(650)(1.8)=(1000)(8.2) \mathrm{h}_{\mathrm{s}} \quad \mathrm{h}_{\mathrm{s}}=0.143 \mathrm{~m}$\\
$\mathrm{h}=\mathrm{h}_{\mathrm{t}}-\mathrm{h}_{\mathrm{s}}=0.22-0.143=0.077 \mathrm{~m}$ (the visible portion of the raft)\\
b) $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{B}}$ equals weight of displaced water $=\rho_{\mathrm{h} 20} \mathrm{~V}_{\text {disp }} \mathrm{g}=\rho_{\mathrm{h} 20}\left(\mathrm{Ah}_{\mathrm{s}}\right) \mathrm{g}=(1000)(8.2)(0.143)(9.8)=11500 \mathrm{~N}$ directed $\uparrow$\\
c) Determine the extra buoyant force that will come from submerging the exposed raft volume $\mathrm{V}_{\exp }=\mathrm{Ah}$\\
$\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{b}(\text { extra })}=\rho_{\mathrm{h} 20} \mathrm{~V}_{\text {disp }} \mathrm{g}=\rho_{\mathrm{h} 20}(\mathrm{Ah}) \mathrm{g}=(1000)(8.2)(0.077)(9.8)=6187.7 \mathrm{~N}$\\
1 persons weight $=\mathrm{mg}=735 \mathrm{~N} . \quad$ Total weight allowed $/$ person weight $=6187.7 / 735=8.41$\\
So, an extra 8 people could come on without submerging the raft. You could also chop some arms or legs off and throw them on there also until you get up to the extra 0.41 of a person limit.

\section*{B2005B5.}
a) The force on the plug from the water inherently includes the atmosphere above it, so we use the absolute pressure. $\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{abs}}=\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{o}}+\rho \mathrm{gh}=1.01 \times 10^{5}+(1025)(9.8)(20 \mathrm{~m})=3 \times 10^{5} \mathrm{~Pa}$\\
The force is then found with $\mathrm{P}=\mathrm{F} / \mathrm{A} \quad 3 \times 10^{5}=\mathrm{F} /\left(4 \times 10^{-5}\right) \rightarrow \mathrm{F}=12 \mathrm{~N}$\\
Note: This calculation of pressure ( $\rho g h$ ) only works since the fluid is at rest (static). For moving fluids, only Bernoulli's equation (or $F / A$ in rare cases) can be applied for determining pressures.\\
b) Though many of you may know the Torricelli theorem shortcut to this problem, when the AP exam graded this question, simply stating that equation and plugging in lost points. To be safe you should always start with Bernoulli's equation in its full form, cancel out terms that don't exist or are assumed zero, and solve from there. $\mathrm{P}_{1}+\rho \mathrm{gy}_{1}+1 / 2 \rho \mathrm{v}_{1}^{2}=\mathrm{P}_{2}+\rho \mathrm{gy}_{2}+1 / 2 \rho \mathrm{v}_{2}^{2}$\\
$\mathrm{P}_{1}$ and $\mathrm{P}_{2}$ are both open to atmosphere so are at $\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{o}}$ and cancel. Tank is large so $\mathrm{v}_{1}$ is assumed small enough to be $0, y_{2}$ is set as zero height.\\
$\rho \mathrm{gy}_{1}=1 / 2 \rho \mathrm{v}_{2}^{2} \quad \mathrm{v}_{2}=\sqrt{ } 2 \mathrm{gy}_{1}$ (as expected from Torricelli) $\ldots \mathrm{v}_{2}=\sqrt{ } 2(9.8)(20)=19.8 \mathrm{~m} / \mathrm{s}$\\
c) Volume flow rate $=\mathrm{Q}=\mathrm{Av}=\left(4 \times 10^{-5}\right)(19.8)=7.92 \times 10^{-4} \mathrm{~m}^{3} / \mathrm{s}$

\section*{2007B4.}
a) Volume flow rate $=\mathrm{Q}=\mathrm{V} / \mathrm{t}=7.2 \times 10^{-4} /(2 \mathrm{~min} * 60 \mathrm{sec} / \mathrm{min})=6 \times 10^{-6} \mathrm{~m}^{3} / \mathrm{s}$\\
b) Your first thought is probably Bernoulli, but there are too many unknowns so this does not work. We can use the volume flow rate above the find the velocity.\\
$\mathrm{Q}=\mathrm{Av} \quad 6 \times 10^{-6}=\left(2.5 \times 10^{-6}\right) \mathrm{v} \quad \mathrm{v}=2.4 \mathrm{~m} / \mathrm{s}$\\
c) Use Bernoulli, same derivation as in the problem above (B2005B5) ... v $v_{2}=\sqrt{ } 2 g h \quad(2.4)=\sqrt{ } 2(9.8) h \quad h=0.29 m$\\
d) Left of beaker. Based on the formula derived above, the exit velocity is dependent on the height and with less horizontal exit velocity the range will be less ( $d_{x}=v_{x} t$ ). This makes sense because less height would result in less pressure and decrease the speed the fluid is ejected at, thus lessening the range.

\section*{B2007B4.}
a) Use Bernoulli, same derivation as problem B2005B5 ... v ${ }_{2}=\sqrt{ } 2 g h \ldots v_{2}=\sqrt{ } 2(9.8)(0.7) \ldots v_{2}=3.7 \mathrm{~m} / \mathrm{s}$\\
b) Volume flow rate $\mathrm{Q}=\mathrm{Av}=\pi(0.001)^{2}(3.7)=1.16 \times 10^{-5} \mathrm{~m}^{3} / \mathrm{s}$\\
c) $\mathrm{Q}=\mathrm{V} / \mathrm{t} \quad 1.16 \times 10^{-5}=\mathrm{V} /(2 \mathrm{~min} * 60 \mathrm{~s} / \mathrm{min}) \quad \mathrm{V}=0.0014 \mathrm{~m}^{3}$\\
d) Free fall. $\quad d=v_{i} t+1 / 2 g t^{2} \quad-0.25=(-3.7 t)+1 / 2(-9.8) t^{2} \quad$ solve quadratic $t=0.062 s$

Alternatively, first determine $\mathrm{v}_{\mathrm{f}}$ at the 0.25 m location then use $\mathrm{v}_{\mathrm{f}}=\mathrm{v}_{\mathrm{i}}+$ at to solve for t .

\section*{2008B4.}
a) Using projectile methods. $\quad \mathrm{v}_{\mathrm{fy}}{ }^{2}=\mathrm{v}_{\mathrm{iy}}{ }^{2}+2 \mathrm{ad}_{\mathrm{y}} \quad 0=\left(\mathrm{v}_{\mathrm{i}} \sin 50\right)^{2}+2(-9.8)(0.15) \quad \mathrm{v}_{\mathrm{i}}=2.24 \mathrm{~m} / \mathrm{s}$\\
b) Volume flow rate $=\mathrm{Q}=\mathrm{Av}=\pi\left(4 \times 10^{-3}\right)^{2}(2.24)=1.13 \times 10^{-4} \mathrm{~m}^{3} / \mathrm{s}$\\
c) If you don't understand the wording, here is what the problem is saying\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-226}

First we need to find the velocity of the water at the feeder using continuity $\mathrm{A}_{1} \mathrm{v}_{1}=\mathrm{Q}_{2} \quad \pi\left(7 \times 10^{-3}\right)^{2}\left(\mathrm{v}_{1}\right)=1.13 \times 10^{-4} \quad \mathrm{v}_{1}=0.73 \mathrm{~m} / \mathrm{s}$

Bernoulli. Position 2 is the fountain spigot which is open so at atmospheric pressure. $y_{1}=0$ no height.

$$
\mathrm{P}_{1}+\rho \mathrm{gy}_{1}+1 / 2 \rho \mathrm{v}_{1}^{2}=\mathrm{P}_{2}+\rho \mathrm{gy}_{2}+1 / 2 \rho \mathrm{v}_{2}^{2}
$$

$\mathrm{P}_{1}+0+1 / 2(1000)(0.73)^{2}=\left(1.01 \times 10^{5}\right)+(1000)(9.8)(3 \mathrm{~m})+1 / 2(1000)(2.24)^{2}$\\
$\mathrm{P}_{1}=1.32 \times 10^{5} \mathrm{~Pa}$ which is the absolute pressure of the feeder.\\
To find the gauge pressure of the feeder. $\mathrm{P}_{\text {abs }}=\mathrm{P}_{\text {gauge }}+\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{o}} \quad 1.32 \times 10^{5}=\mathrm{P}_{\text {gauge }}+1.01 \times 10^{5}$

$$
\mathrm{P}_{\text {gauge }}=31600 \mathrm{~Pa} .
$$

Note: This gauge pressure could be determined directly in Bernoulli's equation by realizing that $P_{1}$ includes atmospheric pressure as part of its total value and that $P_{2}$ was equal to atmospheric pressure, so by elimination of the term $P_{2}, P_{1}$ becomes the gauge pressure. This should be stated in the solution if it is the chosen solution method.

\section*{B2008B4}
a) Volume flow rate $=\mathrm{Q}=\mathrm{Av}=\pi(0.015)^{2}(6)=0.0042 \mathrm{~m}^{3} / \mathrm{s}$\\
b) First we need to find the velocity of the water in the pipe below using continuity\\
$A_{1} v_{1}=Q_{2}$\\
$\pi(0.025)^{2}\left(\mathrm{v}_{1}\right)=0.0042$\\
$\mathrm{v}_{1}=2.16 \mathrm{~m} / \mathrm{s}$

Bernoulli. Position 2 is the fountain spigot which is open so at atmospheric pressure. $y_{1}=0$ no height.\\
$\mathrm{P}_{1}+\rho \mathrm{gy}_{1}+1 / 2 \rho \mathrm{v}_{1}{ }^{2}=\mathrm{P}_{2}+\rho \mathrm{gy}_{2}+1 / 2 \rho \mathrm{v}_{2}{ }^{2}$\\
$P_{1}+0+1 / 2(1000)(2.16)^{2}=\left(1.01 \times 10^{5}\right)+(1000)(9.8)(2.5 m)+1 / 2(1000)(6)^{2}$\\
$\mathrm{P}_{1}=141000 \mathrm{~Pa}$\\
