\section*{2009B5.}
a) There are three forces acting on the masses in each case. Tension up, buoyant force up, weight down. Since they are at rest we have. $\mathrm{F}_{\text {net }}=0 \quad \mathrm{~F}_{\mathrm{t}}+\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{b}}=\mathrm{mg}$\\
$\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{t}}=\mathrm{mg}-\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{b}}$ so the largest $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{b}}$ makes the largest $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{t}}$

We are to assume the diagram is to scale and that clearly the volumes of the three containers are different. The one with the largest volume displaces the largest amount and weight of water and will have the largest buoyant force acting on it. So since they all displace different volumes (and weights) of water they all have different buoyant forces, and based on the equation shown above will have different tensions.\\
b) The mass of the object is given by $\mathrm{m}=\rho_{\text {obj }} \mathrm{V}_{\text {obj }}$.

Using the equation from part a ,

$$
\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{t}}+\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{b}}=\mathrm{mg}, \quad \mathrm{~F}_{\mathrm{t}}+\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{b}}=\left(\rho_{\text {obj }} \mathrm{V}_{\text {obj. }}\right) \mathrm{g} \quad(0.0098)+\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{b}}=(1300)\left(1 \times 10^{-5}\right)(9.8) \quad \mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{b}}=0.1176 \mathrm{~N}
$$

c) The buoyant force is by definition equal to the weight of the displaced fluid.

$$
\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{b}}=\left(\rho_{\text {fluid }} \mathrm{V}_{\text {disp. }}\right) \mathrm{g} \quad 0.1176=\rho_{\text {fluid }}\left(1 \times 10^{-5}\right)(9.8) \quad \rho_{\text {fluid }}=1200 \mathrm{~kg} / \mathrm{m}^{3}
$$

d) With only half of the volume submerged, $1 / 2$ as much water will be displaced and the buoyant force will be half the size. Based on the formula from part A, less buoyant from will make a larger tension. This also makes sense conceptually. Objects have large apparent weights in air than water so having some of it in the air will increase its apparent weight.

\section*{B2009B3.}
a) Using fluid continuity. $\mathrm{A}_{1} \mathrm{v}_{1}=\mathrm{A}_{2} \mathrm{v}_{2} \quad\left(1 \times 10^{-4}\right)\left(\mathrm{v}_{1}\right)=\left(0.5 \times 10^{-4}\right)(8.2) \quad \mathrm{v}_{\mathrm{a}}=4.1 \mathrm{~m} / \mathrm{s}$\\
b) Bernoulli. Position B is the fountain spigot which is open so at atmospheric pressure. $\mathrm{y}_{1}=0$ no height.

$$
\begin{aligned}
& \mathrm{P}_{1}+\rho \mathrm{gy}_{1}+1 / 2 \rho \mathrm{v}_{1}^{2}=\mathrm{P}_{2}+\rho \mathrm{gy}_{2}+1 / 2 \rho \mathrm{v}_{2}^{2} \\
& \mathrm{P}_{1}+0+1 / 2(1000)(4.1)^{2}=\left(1.01 \times 10^{5}\right)+(1000)(9.8)(0.5 \mathrm{~m})+1 / 2(1000)(8.2)^{2}
\end{aligned} \quad \mathrm{P}_{1}=1.3 \times 10^{5} \mathrm{~Pa}
$$

c) Free fall of a water droplet. $\quad \mathrm{v}_{\mathrm{f}}^{2}=\mathrm{v}_{\mathrm{i}}^{2}+2 \mathrm{gd} \quad(0)^{2}=(8.2)^{2}+2(-9.8)(\mathrm{d}) \quad \mathrm{d}=3.43 \mathrm{~m}$\\
d) Projectile method, in y direction.\\
$d_{y}=v_{i y} t+1 / 2 g t^{2}$\\
$d_{y}=\left(v_{i} \sin \theta\right) t+1 / 2 g t^{2}$\\
$0=(8.2 \sin 60) \mathrm{t}+1 / 2(-9.8) \mathrm{t}^{2}$\\
$\mathrm{t}=1.45 \mathrm{sec}$\\
X direction. $\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{x}}=\mathrm{v}_{\mathrm{x}} \mathrm{t}$\\
$\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{x}}=\left(\mathrm{v}_{\mathrm{i}} \cos \theta\right) \mathrm{t}$\\
$\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{x}}=(8.2 \cos 60)(1.45)$\\
$\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{x}}=5.95 \mathrm{~m}$

b) The block will float based on its density. For floating, block weight $=$ buoyant force

$$
\mathrm{m}_{\text {obj }} \mathrm{g}=\mathrm{p}_{\mathrm{h} 20} \mathrm{~V}_{\text {disp }} \mathrm{g} \quad \mathrm{~m}_{\text {obj }}=\mathrm{p}_{\mathrm{h} 20} \mathrm{~A}_{\text {sqaure }}\left(\mathrm{h}_{\text {submerged }}\right) \quad 12=1000(0.2 \times 0.2) \mathrm{h}_{\text {sub }} \quad \mathrm{h}_{\text {sub }}=0.3 \mathrm{~m}
$$

$$

\section*{SUP2.}
a) Using fluid continuity.\\
$\mathrm{A}_{1} \mathrm{v}_{1}=\mathrm{A}_{2} \mathrm{v}_{2}$\\
$(4)(10)=(2)\left(\mathrm{v}_{2}\right)$\\
$\mathrm{v}_{2}=20 \mathrm{~m} / \mathrm{s}$\\
b) Bernoulli. $\operatorname{pgy}_{1}$ terms cancel out since the pipe stays on the same level.\\
$\mathrm{P}_{1}+\rho \mathrm{gy}_{1}+1 / 2 \rho \mathrm{v}_{1}^{2}=\mathrm{P}_{2}+\rho \mathrm{gy}_{2}+1 / 2 \rho \mathrm{v}_{2}^{2}$\\
$2 \times 10^{5}+0+1 / 2(1000)(10)^{2}=\mathrm{P}_{2}+0+1 / 2(1000)(20)^{2}$

$$
\mathrm{P}_{2}=50000 \mathrm{~Pa} .
$$

Since $P_{1}$ was the gauge pressure and did not include $P_{o}, P_{2}$ will also come out as the gauge pressure.

\section*{SUP3.}
a) Bernoulli. $\rho g y_{1}$ terms cancel out since the height difference is negligible.\\
$\mathrm{P}_{1}+\rho \mathrm{gy}_{1}+1 / 2 \rho \mathrm{v}_{1}{ }^{2}=\mathrm{P}_{2}+\rho \mathrm{gy}_{2}+1 / 2 \rho \mathrm{v}_{2}{ }^{2} \ldots$ rearrange equation so we can find $\mathrm{P}_{2}-\mathrm{P}_{1}$ which is the $\Delta \mathrm{P}$

$$
\mathrm{P}_{2}-\mathrm{P}_{1}=1 / 2 \rho \mathrm{v}_{1}^{2}-1 / 2 \rho \mathrm{v}_{2}^{2} \quad \Delta \mathrm{P}=1 / 2(1.2)\left(50^{2}-40^{2}\right)=540 \mathrm{~Pa}
$$

b)\\
i) $\Delta P=F_{\text {lift }} / A$

$$
540=\mathrm{F}_{\text {lift }} /(9 \times 2 \text { wings })
$$

$$
\mathrm{F}_{\text {lift }}=9720 \mathrm{~N}
$$

ii) $\mathrm{F}_{\text {net }}=0$\\
$\mathrm{F}_{\text {lift }}=\mathrm{mg}$\\
$9720=\mathrm{m}(9.8)$\\
$\mathrm{m}=992 \mathrm{~kg}$.

\section*{SUP4.}
This problem involves floating objects, so weight of object $=$ buoyant force $m_{\text {obj }} g=\rho_{\text {fluid }} V_{\text {disp }} g$ In general $\ldots \mathrm{m}_{\text {obj }}=\rho_{\text {obj }} \mathrm{V}_{\text {obj }}$

$$
\text { Giving } \ldots \rho_{\text {obj }} \mathrm{V}_{\text {obj }} \mathrm{g}=\rho_{\text {fluid }} \mathrm{V}_{\text {disp }} \mathrm{g} \quad \ldots \quad \rho_{\text {obj }} \mathrm{V}_{\text {obj }}=\rho_{\text {fluid }} \mathrm{V}_{\text {disp }}
$$

Water\\
$\rho_{\text {obj }} \mathrm{V}_{\text {obj }}=\rho_{\text {fluid }} \mathrm{V}_{\text {disp }}$\\
$\rho_{\mathrm{w}} \mathrm{V}=(1000)(2 / 3 \mathrm{~V})$\\
$\rho_{\mathrm{w}}=666.67 \mathrm{~kg} / \mathrm{m}^{3}$

Oil

$$
\begin{gathered}
\rho_{\text {obj }} \mathrm{V}_{\text {obj }}=\rho_{\text {fluid }} \mathrm{V}_{\text {disp }} \\
(666.67) \mathrm{V}=\rho_{\text {oil }}(9 / 10 \mathrm{~V}) \\
\rho_{\text {oil }}=740.74 \mathrm{~kg} / \mathrm{m}^{3}
\end{gathered}
$$

\section*{SUP5.}
a) $\mathrm{P}_{\text {abs }}=\mathrm{P}_{\text {gauge }}+\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{o}} \quad \mathrm{P}_{\text {abs }}=\rho \mathrm{gh}+1.01 \times 10^{5} \quad \mathrm{P}_{\text {abs }}=(1000)(9.8)(16)+1.01 \times 10^{5}=260000 \mathrm{~Pa}$\\
b) Use Bernoulli, same derivation as problem B2005B5 $\ldots \mathrm{v}_{2}=\sqrt{ } 2 \mathrm{gh} \ldots \mathrm{v}_{2}=\sqrt{ } 2(9.8)(11) \ldots \quad \mathrm{v}_{2}=14.7 \mathrm{~m} / \mathrm{s}$\\
c) Using projectile methods.

$$
\begin{aligned}
& \mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{y}}=\mathrm{v}_{\mathrm{iy}} \mathrm{t}+1 / 2 \mathrm{at}^{2} \quad-3=0+1 / 2(-9.8) \mathrm{t}^{2} \quad \mathrm{t}=0.78 \mathrm{sec} \\
& \mathrm{~d}_{\mathrm{x}}=\mathrm{v}_{\mathrm{x}} \mathrm{t}=(14.7)(0.78)=11.5 \mathrm{~m}
\end{aligned}
$$

d) An increase in atmospheric pressure around the damn increases both $P_{1}$ and $P_{2}$ equally so there is no net effect on these terms in Bernoulli's equation, which means the exit velocity would be the same.

\multicolumn{1}{c}{Pressure} & Density \\
(A) Remains the same & Remains the same \\
(B) Remains the same & Doubles \\
(C) Doubles & Remains the same \\
(D) Doubles & Doubles \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\begin{enumerate}
  \setcounter{enumi}{17}
  \item Which of the following statements is NOT a correct assumption of the classical model of an ideal gas?\\
(A) The molecules are in random motion.\\
(B) The volume of the molecules is negligible compared with the volume occupied by the gas.\\
(C) The molecules obey Newton's laws of motion.\\
(D) The collisions between molecules are inelastic.
  \item A sample of an ideal gas is in a tank of constant volume. The sample absorbs heat energy so that its temperature changes from 300 K to 600 K . If $v_{l}$ is the average speed of the gas molecules before the absorption of heat and $v_{2}$ is their average speed after the absorption of heat, what is the ratio $v_{2} / v_{1}$ ?\\
(A) 4\\
(B) 2\\
(C) $\sqrt{2}$\\
(D) $1 / 2$
  \item Two blocks of steel, the first of mass 1 kg and the second of mass 2 kg , are in thermal equilibrium with a third block of aluminum of mass 2 kg that has a temperature of 400 K . What are the respective temperatures of the first and second steel blocks?\\
(A) 400 K and 200 K\\
(B) 200 K and 400 K\\
(C) 400 K and 400 K\\
(D) 800 K and 400 K
  \item An ideal gas may be taken from one state to another state with a different pressure, volume, and temperature along several different paths. Quantities that will always be the same for this process, regardless of which path is taken, include which of the following?\\
I. The change in internal energy of the gas\\
II. The heat exchanged between the gas and its surroundings\\
III. The work done by the gas\\
(A) I only\\
(B) II only\\
(C) I and III only\\
(D) II and III only
  \item A square steel plate with sides of length 1.00 m has a hole in its center 0.100 m in diameter. If the entire plate is heated to such a temperature that its sides become 1.01 m long, the diameter of the hole will be\\
(A) 0.090 m\\
(B) 0.099 m\\
(C) 0.101 m\\
(D) 0.110 m
  \item Which of the following will occur if the average speed of the gas molecules in a closed rigid container is increased?\\
(A) The density of the gas will decrease.\\
(B) The density of the gas will increase.\\
(C) The pressure of the gas will increase.\\
(D) The pressure of the gas will decrease.\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-235}
  \item In time $t$, an amount of heat $Q$ flows through the solid door of area $A$ and thickness $d$ represented above. The temperatures on each side of the door are $T_{2}$ and $T_{1}$, respectively. Which of the following changes would be certain to decrease $Q$ ?\\
(A) Increasing $A$ only\\
(B) Decreasing $d$ only\\
(C) Increasing $d$ and $T_{2}-T_{1}$ only\\
(D) Decreasing $A$ and $T_{2}-T_{1}$ only
  \item A gas with a fixed number of molecules does 32 J of work on its surroundings, and 16 J of heat are transferred from the gas to the surroundings. What happens to the internal energy of the gas?\\
(A) It decreases by 48 J .\\
(B) It decreases by 16 J .\\
(C) It increases by 16 J .\\
(D) It increases by 48 J .
  \item A mass $m$ of helium gas is in a container of constant volume $V$. It is initially at pressure $p$ and absolute (Kelvin) temperature $T$. Additional helium is added, bringing the total mass of helium gas to $3 m$. After this addition, the temperature is found to be $2 T$. What is the gas pressure?\\
(A) $2 / 3 p$\\
(B) $3 / 2 p$\\
(C) $3 p$\\
(D) $6 p$\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-235(1)}
  \item A gas can be taken from state $a$ to $c$ by two different reversible processes, $a \Rightarrow c$ or $a \Rightarrow b \Rightarrow c$. During the direct process $a \Rightarrow c, 20.0 \mathrm{~J}$ of work are done by the system and 30.0 J of heat are added to the system. During the process $a \Rightarrow b \Rightarrow c, 25.0 \mathrm{~J}$ of heat are added to the system. How much work is done by the system during $a \Rightarrow b \Rightarrow c$ ?\\
(A) 5.0 J\\
(B) 10.0 J\\
(C) 15.0 J\\
(D) 20.0 J
  \item When an ideal gas is isothermally compressed:\\
(A) thermal energy flows from the gas to the surroundings.\\
(B) thermal energy flows from the surroundings to the gas.\\
(C) no thermal energy enters or leaves the gas.\\
(D) the temperature of the gas increases.
  \item A 200 gram sample of copper is submerged in 100 grams of water until both the copper and water are at the same temperature. Which of the following statements would be true?\\
(A) the molecules of the water and copper would have equal average speeds\\
(B) the molecules of the water and copper would have equal average momenta\\
(C) the molecules of the water and copper would have equal average kinetic energies\\
(D) the water molecules would have twice the average speed of the copper molecules\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-236(1)}
  \item A rectangular piece of metal 3 cm high by 6 cm wide has a hole cut in its center 1 cm high by 4 cm wide as shown in the diagram at right. As the metal is warmed from $0^{\circ} \mathrm{C}$ to $100^{\circ} \mathrm{C}$, what will happen to the dimensions of the hole?\\
(A) both height and width will increase\\
(B) both height and width will decrease\\
(C) height will increase while width will decrease\\
(D) height will decrease while width will increase
  \item A gas is enclosed in a cylindrical piston. When the gas is heated from $0^{\circ} \mathrm{C}$ to $100^{\circ} \mathrm{C}$, the piston is allowed to move to maintain a constant pressure. According to the Kinetic-Molecular Theory of Matter\\
(A) the molecules continue to strike the sides of the container with the same energy\\
(B) the number of molecules of gas must increase\\
(C) the size of the individual molecules has increased\\
(D) the average speed of the molecules has increased\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-236}
  \item Two containers are filled with gases at the same temperature. In the container on the left is a gas of molar mass $2 M$, volume $2 V$, and number of moles $2 n$. In the container on the right is a gas of molar mass $M$, volume $V$, and moles $n$. Which is most nearly the ratio of the pressure of the gas on the left to the pressure of the gas on the right?\\
(A) $1: 1$\\
(B) $2: 1$\\
(C) $4: 1$\\
(D) $8: 1$
  \item A fan blows the air and gives it kinetic energy. An hour after the fan has been turned off, what has happened to the kinetic energy of the air?\\
(A) it turns into thermal energy\\
(B) it turns into sound energy\\
(C) it turns into potential energy\\
(D) it turns into electrical energy
  \item According to the kinetic theory of gases, when the absolute temperature of an ideal gas doubles, the average kinetic energy of the molecules of the gas\\
(A) quadruples\\
(B) doubles\\
(C) is cut in half\\
(D) is quartered
  \item When gas escapes from a pressurized cylinder, the stream of gas feels cool. This is because\\
(A) work is being done at the expense of thermal energy\\
(B) of the convection inside the cylinder\\
(C) pressurized cylinders are good thermal insulators\\
(D) the moisture in the air condenses and cools
  \item Two completely identical samples of the same ideal gas are in equal volume containers with the same pressure and temperature in containers labeled A and B . The gas in container A performs non-zero work W on the surroundings during an isobaric (constant pressure) process before the pressure is reduced isochorically (constant volume) to $1 / 2$ its initial amount. The gas in container B has its pressure reduced isochorically (constant volume) to $1 / 2$ its initial value and then the gas performs non-zero work W on the surroundings during an isobaric (constant pressure) process.
\end{enumerate}

After the processes are performed on the gases in containers A and B , which is at the higher temperature?\\
(A) The gas in container A\\
(B) The gas in container B\\
(C) The value of the work W is necessary to answer this question.\\
(D) The value of the work W is necessary, along with both the initial pressure and volume, in order to answer the question.\\

2001B6. A cylinder is fitted with a freely moveable piston of area $1.20 \times 10^{-2} \mathrm{~m}^{2}$ and negligible mass. The cylinder below the piston is filled with a gas. At state 1 , the gas has volume $1.50 \times 10^{-3} \mathrm{~m}^{3}$, pressure $1.02 \times 10^{5} \mathrm{~Pa}$, and the cylinder is in contact with a water bath at a temperature of $0^{\circ} \mathrm{C}$. The gas is then taken through the following four-step process.

\begin{itemize}
  \item A 2.50 kg metal block is placed on top of the piston, compressing the gas to state 2 , with the gas still at $0^{\circ} \mathrm{C}$.
  \item The cylinder is then brought in contact with a boiling water bath, raising the gas temperature to $100^{\circ} \mathrm{C}$ at state 3 .
  \item The metal block is removed and the gas expands to state 4 still at $100^{\circ} \mathrm{C}$.
  \item Finally, the cylinder is again placed in contact with the water bath at $0^{\circ} \mathrm{C}$, returning the system to state 1 .\\
a. Determine the pressure of the gas in state 2.\\
b. Determine the volume of the gas in state 2.\\
c. Indicate below whether the process from state 2 to state 3 is isothermal, isobaric, or adiabatic.\\
$\qquad$ Isothermal $\qquad$ Isobaric $\qquad$ Adiabatic Explain your reasoning.\\

\section*{Solution}
\begin{enumerate}
  \item While all collisions are elastic and $\mathrm{K}_{\text {avg }} \propto \mathrm{T}$, the molecules move with a wide range of speeds represented by the Maxwellian distribution.
  \item For $\mathrm{X} \Rightarrow \mathrm{Y}$, the process is isobaric. Since the gas is expanding, $\mathrm{W}<0$ and since the temperature is increasing, $\Delta \mathrm{U}>0$ and $\Delta \mathrm{U}=\mathrm{Q}+\mathrm{W}$ so $\mathrm{Q}>0$ (it is also true because process XY lies above an adiabatic expansion from point X )
  \item For $\mathrm{Y} \Rightarrow \mathrm{Z}$, the process is isochoric, which means no work is done $(\mathrm{W}=0)$ and since the temperature is increasing, $\Delta \mathrm{U}>0$
  \item $\quad \mathrm{PV} \propto \mathrm{T}$, or $\mathrm{P} \propto \mathrm{T} / \mathrm{V}$ and if $\mathrm{T} \times 2$ then $\mathrm{P} \times 2$ and if $\mathrm{V} \times 4$ then $\mathrm{P} \div 4$ so the net effect is $\mathrm{P} \times 2 \div 4$
  \item $\quad \mathrm{PV} \propto \mathrm{T}$ so to triple the temperature, the product of P and V must be tripled
  \item Changes in internal energy are path independent on a pV diagram as it depends on the change in temperature, which is based on the beginning and end points of the path and not the path taken
  \item $\mathrm{K}_{\text {avg }} \propto \mathrm{T}$
  \item $H=\frac{k A \Delta T}{L}$
  \item No work is done in an isochoric process, or a process where $\Delta \mathrm{V}=0$ (a vertical line on the pv graph)
  \item The temperature at any point is proportional to the product of P and V . Point A at temperature $\mathrm{T}_{0}$ is at pressure $\times$ volume $\mathrm{p}_{0} \mathrm{~V}_{0}$. Point C is at $3 \mathrm{p}_{0} \times 3 \mathrm{~V}_{0}=9 \mathrm{~T}_{0}$ and point D is at $2 \mathrm{p}_{0} \times 4 \mathrm{~V}_{0}=8 \mathrm{~T}_{0}$
  \item For the entire cycle, $\Delta \mathrm{U}=0$ and $\mathrm{W}=-8 \mathrm{~J}$ so $\mathrm{Q}=\Delta \mathrm{U}-\mathrm{W}=+8 \mathrm{~J}$ ( 8 J added). This means $\mathrm{Q}_{\mathrm{AB}}+$ $\mathrm{Q}_{B C}+\mathrm{Q}_{C A}=+8 \mathrm{~J}=+12 \mathrm{~J}+0 \mathrm{~J}+\mathrm{Q}_{C A}=+8 \mathrm{~J}$
  \item $Q=+275 \mathrm{~J} ; \mathrm{W}=+125 \mathrm{~J}+(-50 \mathrm{~J})=+75 \mathrm{~J} ; \Delta \mathrm{U}=\mathrm{Q}+\mathrm{W}$
  \item Work is the area under the curve, the line bounding the greatest area indicates the most work done
  \item Temperature rises as you travel up and to the right on a pV diagram. Since processes 1,2 and 3 are at the same volume, the highest point is at the highest temperature
  \item $Q=+400 \mathrm{~J} ; \mathrm{W}=-100 \mathrm{~J} ; \Delta \mathrm{U}=\mathrm{Q}+\mathrm{W}$
  \item Isothermal means the temperature is constant. Points to the right or above are at higher temperatures.
  \item $\mathrm{P} \propto \mathrm{T}$ at constant volume. If $\mathrm{T} \times 2$, then $\mathrm{P} \times 2$. Since the mass and volume are unchanged, the density is unchanged as well
  \item If the collisions were inelastic, the gas would change its temperature by virtue of the collisions with no change in pressure or volume.
  \item related to average speed, $v_{r m s}=\sqrt{\frac{3 R T}{M}}$
  \item Being in thermal equilibrium means the objects are at the same temperature. Mass is irrelevant. The question describes the zeroth law of thermodynamics.
  \item Changes in internal energy are path independent on a pV diagram as it depends on the change in temperature, which is based on the beginning and end points of the path and not the path taken. Different paths, with different areas under them will do different amounts of work and hence, different amounts of heat exchanged.
  \item In linear expansion, every linear dimension of an object changes by the same fraction when heated or cooled.
  \item "rigid container" = constant volume. If the speed increases, the temperature will increase, and if the temperature increases at constant volume, the pressure will increase.
  \item $H=\frac{k A \Delta T}{L}$
  \item $\mathrm{Q}=-16 \mathrm{~J} ; \mathrm{W}=-32 \mathrm{~J} ; \Delta \mathrm{U}=\mathrm{Q}+\mathrm{W}$
  \item $\mathrm{P} \propto \mathrm{nT} / \mathrm{V}$; if $\mathrm{n} \times 3$ then $\mathrm{P} \times 3$ and if $\mathrm{T} \times 2$ then $\mathrm{P} \times 2$, the net effect is $\mathrm{P} \times 3 \times 2$
  \item Consider the isothermal line as the "dividing line" between process that increase the temperature of the gas (above the isotherm) and process that lower the temperature of the gas (below the isotherm). A similar analysis can be done to identify heat added or removed from a gas by comparing a process to an adiabat drawn from the same point.
  \item $\mathrm{K}_{\text {avg }}=3 / 2 \mathrm{k}_{\mathrm{B}} \mathrm{T}$ (use absolute temperature)
  \item In linear expansion, every linear dimension of an object changes by the same fraction when heated or cooled. Since each side increases by $4 \%$,the area increases by $(1.04)^{2}=1.08$
  \item $\mathrm{pV}=\mathrm{nRT}$ and $\mathrm{n}=\mathrm{N} / \mathrm{N}_{\mathrm{A}}$
  \item $\mathrm{K}_{\text {avg }} \propto \mathrm{T}$ (absolute)
  \item $Q_{a b d}=+60 \mathrm{~J}+20 \mathrm{~J}=+80 \mathrm{~J} . \mathrm{W}_{a b d}=$ area, negative due to expansion $=-24 \mathrm{~J}$ so $\Delta \mathrm{U}=\mathrm{Q}+\mathrm{W}=$\\
+56 J and $\Delta \mathrm{U}_{\text {abd }}=\Delta \mathrm{U}_{\text {acd }}$ and $\mathrm{W}_{\text {acd }}=\operatorname{area}=-9 \mathrm{~J}$ so $\mathrm{Q}_{\text {acd }}=\Delta \mathrm{U}-\mathrm{W}_{\text {acd }}=+56 \mathrm{~J}-(-9 \mathrm{~J})$
  \item Since there is no area under the line (and no change in volume) $\mathrm{W}=0$. The temperature (and internal energy) decrease so $Q$ cannot be zero ( $Q=\Delta U-W$ )
  \item $\mathrm{pV}=\mathrm{nRT}$
  \item Pressure is the collisions of the molecules of the gas against the container walls. Even though the speed of the molecules is unchanged (constant temperature), the smaller container will cause the molecules to strike the walls more frequently.
  \item $\quad Q=0$ in adiabatic processes (choices $B$ and $D) . Q=\Delta U-W$. Choices $A$ and $C$ have the same $\Delta \mathrm{T}$ and hence, same $\Delta \mathrm{U}$ and since doubling the volume at constant pressure involves negative work, while doubling the pressure at constant volume does no work, $\Delta \mathrm{U}-\mathrm{W}$ is greater for the constant pressure process. (The constant temperature process has $\Delta U=0$ and less work than the constant pressure process)
  \item $\mathrm{pV}=\mathrm{nRT}$ (watch those units!)
  \item Isochoric cooling is a path straight down on a pV diagram (to lower pressures)
  \item Work = area under the curve on a $p V$ diagram. In the convention stated, work is negative for any expansion. Be careful with the graph since it is a graph of pressure vs. temperature. We can find the work by using $|\mathrm{W}|=\mathrm{p} \Delta \mathrm{V}=\mathrm{nR} \Delta \mathrm{T}$
  \item Work = area enclosed by the parallelogram. Since the work done on the gas is negative for a clockwise cycle and they are asking for the work done by the gas, the answer will be positive.
  \item At constant volume $\Delta \mathrm{U}=\mathrm{Q}=3 / 2 \mathrm{nR} \Delta \mathrm{T}$ where in an isochoric process $\mathrm{nR} \Delta \mathrm{T}=\Delta \mathrm{pV}$ so $\mathrm{Q}=3 / 2 \Delta \mathrm{pV}$, or $\Delta \mathrm{p}=2 \times(+40 \mathrm{~J}) /\left(3 \times 0.008 \mathrm{~m}^{3}\right)$
  \item $v_{r m s}=\sqrt{\frac{3 R T}{M}}$ Since hydrogen is 16 times lighter and $v_{\mathrm{rms}} \propto \frac{1}{\sqrt{M}}, v_{\mathrm{H}}=4 \times v_{\mathrm{O}}$
  \item $v_{r m s}=\sqrt{\frac{3 R T}{M}}$ since $\mathrm{M}_{\mathrm{O}}>\mathrm{M}_{\mathrm{H}}$ for them to have the same $v_{\mathrm{rms}} \mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{O}}>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{H}}$
  \item $v_{r m s}=\sqrt{\frac{3 R T}{M}}$ if $v_{\mathrm{rms}}$ is doubled, then T is quadrupled. If $\mathrm{T} \times 4$ at constant volume, then $\mathrm{p} \times 4$
  \item The "energy" lost or gained would be the sum of the work done on the gas and the net heat added to the gas, which is the change in internal energy of the gas. Since the gas returns to its original state, $\Delta \mathrm{U}=0$.
  \item $H=\frac{k A \Delta T}{L}$
  \item An adiabatic expansion is shaped like an isotherm, but brings the gas to a lower temperature
\end{enumerate}

C\\
61. $Q_{\text {cycle }}=Q_{12}+Q_{23}+Q_{31}=+60 \mathrm{~J}-40 \mathrm{~J}+0 \mathrm{~J}=+20 \mathrm{~J}$

D\\
$\mathrm{W}_{\text {cycle }}=\Delta \mathrm{U}_{\text {cycle }}-\mathrm{Q}_{\text {cycle }}=0 \mathrm{~J}-(+20 \mathrm{~J})=-20 \mathrm{~J}=\mathrm{W}_{12}+\mathrm{W}_{23}+\mathrm{W}_{31}$\\
where $W_{12}=-Q_{12}$ since $\Delta U_{12}=0$ and $W_{23}=0$\\
so we have $-20 \mathrm{~J}=-60 \mathrm{~J}+0 \mathrm{~J}+\mathrm{W}_{31}$ which gives $\mathrm{W}_{31}=+40 \mathrm{~J}$\\
Process $3 \Rightarrow 1$ is adiabatic so $\Delta \mathrm{U}_{31}=\mathrm{W}_{31}$

1983B4\\

1979B5\\
a. Process II is isothermal. An isothermal process is one in which the temperature is constant. Thus, from the ideal gas law, the product of pressure and volume is a constant. This condition is satisfied by process II.\\
b. Process III is adiabatic. In an adiabatic process, both the pressure and the volume must change. Thus, processes I and IV are eliminated. Since process II is isothermal, process III is the only possible adiabatic one.\\
c. The gas does work in processes I, II and III. Work is done by the gas whenever the volume increases. (negative work is done by the gas when the volume decreases as well)\\
d. In process IV, no work is done. Since the pressure decreases at constant volume, the temperature also decreases, giving $\Delta \mathrm{U}$ is negative and with $\mathrm{W}=0, \Delta \mathrm{U}=\mathrm{Q}$ and therefore Q is negative. One could also use the adiabatic process as the dividing line between process in which heat is added and those for which heat is removed. On the adiabatic line, $\mathrm{Q}=0$. For any process from the same initial point that lies above the adiabat, heat is added and for any process that lies below the adiabat, heat is removed.\\
e. RMS speed is proportional to the kinetic energy which, in turn, is proportional to the temperature. Only in process I does the temperature increase.

\section*{SECTION A - Geometric Optics}
\begin{enumerate}

\section*{SECTION B - Physical Optics}
\begin{enumerate}
  \item In Young's double slit experiment, the second order bright band of one light source overlaps the third order band of another light source. If the first light source has a wavelength of 660 nm , what is the wavelength of the second light source?\\

B) 990 nm\\
C) 440 nm\\
D) 330 nm
  \item A diffraction grating of 1000 lines $/ \mathrm{cm}$ has red light of wavelength 700 nm pass through it. The distance between the first and third principal bright spots on a screen 2 m away is\\
A) 14 cm\\
B) 28 cm\\
C) 42 cm\\
D) 140 cm
  \item In a Young's double-slit experiment, the slit separation is doubled. To maintain the same fringe spacing on the screen, the screen-to-slit distance $D$ must be changed to\\
A) $D / 2$\\
B) $\frac{D}{\sqrt{2}}$\\
C) $\sqrt{2} D$\\
D) $2 D$
  \item Two sources, in phase and a distance $d$ apart, each emit a wave of wavelength $\lambda$. See figure below. Which of the choices for the path difference $\Delta L=L_{1}-L_{2}$ will always produce destructive interference at point P ?\\

B) $\left(x / L_{2}\right) d$\\
C) $\lambda / 2$\\
D) $2 \lambda$\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-276(1)}
  \item In an experiment to measure the wavelength of light using a double slit apparatus, it is found that the bright fringes are too close together to easily count them. To increase only the spacing between the bright fringes, one could\\
A) increase the slit width\\
B) decrease the slit width\\
C) increase the slit separation\\
D) decrease the slit separation
  \item Plane sound waves of wavelength 0.12 m are incident on two narrow slits in a box with nonreflecting walls, as shown. At a distance of 5.0 m from the center of the slits, a first-order maximum occurs at point $P$, which is 3.0 m from the central maximum. The distance between\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-276} the slits is most nearly\\
(A) 0.09 m\\
(B) 0.16 m\\
(C) 0.20 m\\
(D) 0.24 m
  \item If one of the two slits in a Young's double-slit demonstration of the interference of light is covered with a thin filter that transmits only half the light intensity, which of the following occurs?\\
(A) The bright lines are brighter and the dark lines are darker.\\
(B) The bright lines and the dark lines are all darker.\\
(C) The bright lines and the dark lines are all brighter.\\
(D) The dark lines are brighter and the bright lines are darker.
\end{enumerate}

\section*{2003B4.}
In your physics lab, you have a concave mirror with radius of curvature $r=60 \mathrm{~cm}$. You are assigned the task of finding experimentally the location of a lit candle such that the mirror will produce an image that is 4 times the height of the lit candle.\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-297(1)}

You have an optical bench, which is a long straight track as shown above. Objects in holders can be attached at any location along the bench. In addition to the concave mirror and the lit candle in holders, you also have the following equipment.\\
$\qquad$ convex mirror in holder $\qquad$ concave lens in holder $\qquad$ convex lens in holder\\
$\qquad$ meter stick $\qquad$ ruler $\qquad$ screen in holder\\
(a) Briefly list the steps in your procedure that will lead you to the location of the lit candle that produces the desired image. Include definitions of any parameters that you will measure.\\
(b) On the list of equipment before part (a) place check marks beside each additional piece of equipment you will need to do this experiment.\\
(c) On the scale below, draw a ray diagram of your lab setup in part (a) to show the locations of the candle, the mirror, and the image.\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-297}\\
(d) Check the appropriate spaces below to indicate the characteristics of your image.\\
$\qquad$ real $\qquad$ upright $\qquad$ larger than object\\
$\qquad$ virtual $\qquad$ inverted $\qquad$ smaller than object\\
(e) You complete your assignment and turn in your results to your teacher. She tells you that another student, using equipment from the same list, has found a different location for the lit candle. However, she tells both of you that the labs were done correctly and that neither experiment need be repeated. Explain why both experiments can be correct.\\

\section*{SECTION A - Geometric Optics}
\section*{Solution}
\begin{enumerate}
  \item Using the math, $1 / f=1 / d_{o}+1 / d_{i}$, and $M=-d_{i} / d_{o} \ldots \quad d_{i}+0.6 \quad M=-3 \ldots$
  \item Use $\mathrm{n}_{1} \lambda_{1}=\mathrm{n}_{2} \lambda_{2}$
  \item More-Less dense bend away, Less-More dense bend towards. The more the bend, the bigger the difference in $n$ 's.
  \item If you look carefully you can see these are both 3-4-5 triangles and are also the same triangle flipped. The hypotenuse of each is 1.5 m . Using the sides of the triangles, we have $\sin \theta_{1}=\mathrm{o} / \mathrm{h}=0.8 / 1.5$ for the bottom triangle, and $\sin \theta_{2}=\mathrm{o} / \mathrm{h}=1.2 / 1.5$ for the top triangle. Now use $\mathrm{n}_{1} \sin \theta_{1}=\mathrm{n}_{2} \sin \theta_{2} \ldots \mathrm{n}_{1}(0.8 / 1.5)=(1)(1.2 / 1.5) \ldots \mathrm{n}_{1}=1.2 / 0.8=3 / 2=1.5$
  \item Less-More bend towards. But it can't be E because that would only happen if the incoming angle was also 0 .
  \item The lens shown has thick in the center and thin on the outside which makes a converging lens. In converging lenses, all of the real images are inverted and can be any size, but the virtual images are formed in a magnifying lens scenario and are always larger and upright.
  \item A horizontal beam approaching a converging lens bends and converges through the focal point.
  \item More-Less dense bend away, Less-More dense bend towards. The more the bend, the bigger the difference in $n$ 's.
  \item Assuming total internal reflection didn't happen, More-Less dense bend away.
  \item Need a magnifying glass which is choice B.
  \item Generally when we go from more-less we should always check the critical angle first rather than assuming the ray will refract and bend away. Choice D might be correct, but not until we first check the critical angle for total internal reflection. Use $\mathrm{n}_{\mathrm{i}} \sin \theta_{\mathrm{c}}=\mathrm{n}_{\mathrm{r}} \sin (90), \mathrm{n}_{\mathrm{i}}=1.5, \mathrm{n}_{\mathrm{r}}=1$ $\theta_{\mathrm{c}}=41.8^{\circ}$. Since our incoming angle (60) is larger than the critical angle, total internal reflection will occur and you will get choice E.
  \item Using the math, $1 / f=1 / d_{o}+1 / d_{i}$, and $M=-d_{i} / d_{o} \ldots \quad d_{i}+20 \quad M=-1 \ldots$
  \item When light from multiple locations pass through a given part of a lens to form an image, only a small portion of a lens is needed to form the image. The more of a lens, the more light rays that can be bent by it to each image location. This simply makes the image brighter. By covering half the lens, all of the incoming rays still bend all the same ways but there are less total rays being bent to given locations on the image so it is dimmer. This can easily be seen by looking at a lens that has only horizontal rays approaching it. All of these rays converge to the focal point; covering a portion of the lens still focuses the rays on the focal point, just less of them.
  \item More-Less dense bend away, Less-More dense bend towards. The more the bend, the bigger the difference in $n$ 's ... this shows that $n_{2}>n_{1}>n_{3}$. More $n$ means less speed so $v_{3}>v_{1}>v_{2}$
  \item It's a diverging lens so light bends away from what the horizontal path would be without the lens.
  \item The focal point is $=R / 2$. Then use the math $1 / f=1 / d_{0}+1 / d_{i} \ldots$ and $d_{i}=10$
  \item From $\mathrm{n}=\mathrm{c} / \mathrm{v}$.
\end{enumerate}

$$
\begin{array}{lllll}
\mathrm{n}_{1}=\mathrm{c} / \mathrm{v}_{1} & \ldots & 1.5=\mathrm{c} / \mathrm{v}_{\mathrm{X}} & \ldots & \mathrm{v}_{\mathrm{X}}=\mathrm{c} / 1.5 \\
\mathrm{n}_{2}=\mathrm{c} / \mathrm{v}_{2} & \ldots & 2.0=\mathrm{c} / \mathrm{v}_{\mathrm{Y}} & \ldots & \mathrm{v}_{\mathrm{Y}}=\mathrm{c} / 2
\end{array}
$$

C

The problem defines $\mathrm{v}_{\mathrm{Y}}=\mathrm{v}$\\
So $\mathrm{v}=\mathrm{c} / 2, \mathrm{c}=2 \mathrm{v} \ldots$ then subbing that into the $\mathrm{v}_{\mathrm{x}}$ equation we have $\mathrm{v}_{\mathrm{X}}=(2 \mathrm{v}) / 1.5=1.33 \mathrm{v}$\\
18. First find the $\lambda$ in the film. $\mathrm{n}_{\text {air }} \lambda_{\text {air }}=\mathrm{n}_{\text {film }} \lambda_{\text {film }} \ldots(1)(600)=(1.5) \lambda_{\text {glass }} \ldots \lambda_{\text {glass }}=400 \mathrm{~nm}$ As the light travels through the two boundaries, you get a $1 / 2 \lambda$ phase shift (flip) at the first boundary but no shift at the second boundary. Therefore, you need to make another $1 / 2 \lambda$ of phase difference total by traveling in the film thickness to produce constructive interference to reinforce the orange wavelength. When the glass thickness is $1 / 4$ of the $\lambda$ in the glass, the light will travel up and down to make the extra $1 / 2 \lambda$ needed. So $1 / 4$ of the $\lambda$ in the glass gives you 100 nm thickness needed.\\
19. Do the math twice. For the first lens. $1 / f=1 / d_{o}+1 / d_{i} \ldots d_{i}=+14 \mathrm{~cm}$ (real). So this first 'pre-image' is formed 14 cm to the right of the first lens, which means it is 16 cm from the second lens. Now redo the math using this 'pre-image' as the object located 16 cm away from the second lens. $1 / \mathrm{f}=1 / \mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{o}}+1 / \mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{i}} \quad \ldots \quad \mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{i}}=+26.67 \mathrm{~cm}$.\\
20. The magnification is $M=2$. Using $M=-d_{i} / d_{o} \quad \ldots d_{i}=-2 d_{o}$ Lets assume a value of $d_{o}=10$, then $\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{i}}=-20$, and from $1 / \mathrm{f}=1 / \mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{o}}+1 / \mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{i}}$, the focal point is 20 . Now redo the math with the focal point for the diverging lens being negative and the new $d_{i}=-6.67$, giving a new $M=0.67$\\
21. A convex lens is a converging lens. When the object is in front of the focal point, it acts as a magnifying glass.\\
22. Similar to question 18, except both boundaries undergo phase shifts, so 1 full extra wavelength is needed using the soap thickness. This requires the thickness to be $1 / 2 \lambda_{\text {soap }}$ giving the answer.\\
23. Draw ray diagrams for each, or make up numbers and do the math for each to see which works.\\
24. When traveling between mediums, sound behaves opposite from light. As given in the problem the sound travels faster in the denser rock. When the sound speeds up, the wavelength increases and the frequency stays the same.\\
25. Diverging lens always produces the same object type no matter what.\\
26. The transmitted wave never has a phase change, but hitting the more dense block causes the reflection to flip 180 degrees.\\
27. More-Less dense bend away, Less-More dense bend towards. The more the bend, the bigger the difference in $n$ 's ... this shows $n_{2}>n_{1}>n_{3}$. More $n$ means less speed, so $v_{3}>v_{1}>v_{2}$. Frequency does not change as the light passes from one medium to another.\\
28. Based on various ray diagrams drawn with the object behind the focal point, the image is always real but its size depends on where it is in location to the focal point.\\
29. First determine the $\lambda_{\text {film }} . \quad \mathrm{n}_{1} \lambda_{1}=\mathrm{n}_{\text {film }} \lambda_{\text {film }} \quad \ldots \quad(1)(640)=(1.33) \lambda_{\text {film }} \quad \ldots \quad \lambda_{\text {film }}=481 \mathrm{~nm}$.

When the wave reaches each boundary is undergoes a $1 / 2 \lambda$ phase shift at each boundary so this essentially cancels out the phase shift. To not reflect any light, we want to have destructive interference. In order to get destructive interference we need to get a total of $1 / 2 \lambda$ or $1 \frac{1}{2} \lambda$ or $2 \frac{1}{2} \lambda \ldots$ phase differences from moving in the film thickness. These phase differences require a thickness equal to $1 / 4 \lambda_{\text {film }}, 3 / 4 \lambda_{\text {film }}, 5 / 4 \lambda_{\text {film }} \ldots 360 \mathrm{~nm}$ thickness matches the $3 / 4 \lambda_{\text {film }}$ possibility.\\
30. For air-film-glass of progressively increasing index, to produce destructive interference we need $1 / 4$ of a wavelength in the coating. See question 43 for the reason.\\
31. For all three diagrams, there is a $1 / 2 \lambda$ phase shift when entering the film but no phase shift when exiting. To produce constructive interference, a total extra phase different of $1 / 2 \lambda$ from moving in the film thickness is needed so odd multiples of $1 / 4 \lambda$ will produce constructive interference.\\
32. Draw a ray diagram.\\
33. A magnifying glass is a lens, and is produced by a converging lens. It is virtual.\\
34. Using the math, $1 / f=1 / d_{o}+1 / d_{i}$, and $M=-d_{i} / d_{o} \quad \ldots . d_{i}=-0.10 m, M=+0.33$\\
35. Converging lenses make real images but they are always inverted.\\
48. The larger the difference between n's the more the rays bend. When the water is added, the difference between n's is less so the amount of bending is less.\\
49. When an object is placed in front of the focal point of a converging lens, the lens acts as a magnifying glass.\\
50. The film has a higher n compared to both sides, such as soap surrounded by air. So as the light ray hits the first boundary it makes a $1 / 2 \lambda$ phase flip, but does not make the flip at the second boundary. To be constructive, we need to cover a total of $1 / 2 \lambda$ extra phase shift due to traveling in the film thickness. So the thickness should be $1 / 4 \lambda_{\text {film }}$.\\
51. Medium I (air) is surrounding the sphere on both sides. As it enters the sphere, it goes less-more so bends towards the normal line (leaving D or E as the possibly answers). When the ray reaches the far edge of the sphere, it goes from more-less so should bend away from the normal line. Note the normal line drawn below.\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-317}\\
52. This should be the opposite of the scenario in the last question.\\
53. When light from multiple locations pass through a given part of a lens to form an image, only a small portion of a lens is needed to form the image. The more of a lens, the more light rays that can be bent by it to each image location. This simply makes the image brighter. By covering half the lens, all of the incoming rays still bend all the same ways but there are less total rays being bent to given locations on the image so it is dimmer. This can easily be seen by looking at a lens that has only horizontal rays approaching it. All of these rays converge to the focal point; covering a portion of the lens still focuses the rays on the focal point, just less of them.\\
54. All waves demonstrate the listed choices.\\
55. Bending of a wave (refraction) is due to the speed change at an angle. The more the speed changes, the more the bending. Hence, the violet bends more so must have a larger speed change (more slowing), so the red is faster. Additionally, we can note that since the violet slows and bends more, the index of refraction in glass for a violet light is higher than the index for a red light.\\
56. Based on the law of reflection, the angle of reflection must be the same as the incoming angle. When the light enters the ice it is going more-less so bends away from the normal. This means that $\theta_{\mathrm{r}}$ is larger than $\theta_{\mathrm{i}}$.\\
57. Using the math, $1 / f=1 / d_{o}+1 / d_{i}, \quad d_{i}=-1.2$. Its virtual so its on the same side as the object, which puts the image on the left side of the lens.\\
58. This is a magnifying glass, which can be memorized or the math can be done to prove the answer.\\
59. From the diagram, the angle at the bottom of the small top triangle is $30^{\circ}$ so when we draw the normal line on that slanted interface, the angle of incidence there is $60^{\circ}$. We are told this is the critical angle which means the angle of refraction of the scenario is $90^{\circ}$. Now we use\\
$n_{i} \sin \theta_{c}=n_{r} \sin (90) \quad \ldots n_{i} \sin (60)=(1)(1) \quad \ldots n_{i}=1 / \sin 60 \quad \ldots n_{i}=\frac{1}{\left(\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\right)}$\\
Rationalizing gives us the answer.

\section*{SECTION B - Physical Optics}
\section*{Solution}
\begin{enumerate}
  \item Using $\mathrm{m} \lambda=\mathrm{d} \sin \theta$, the value of $\sin \theta$ is the same for both sources since the location of the spot is the same, but the first source is at $\mathrm{m}=2$, and the second source is at $\mathrm{m}=3$. Equating $\mathrm{d} \sin \theta$ for each gives $\mathrm{m}_{1} \lambda_{1}=\mathrm{m}_{2} \lambda_{2} \ldots$ (2)(660)=3( $\lambda_{2}$ ) $\ldots \lambda_{2}=440 \mathrm{~nm}$.
  \item 1000 lines $/ \mathrm{cm}$ gives a line spacing $d=1 / 1000 \mathrm{~cm} /$ line $=1 \times 10^{-5} \mathrm{~m} /$ line. $\lambda=7 \times 10^{-7} \mathrm{~m}$. With diffraction gratings, we usually assume the small angle approximation does not work, so we find $\theta$ then use the geometry with $\tan \theta$ or another trig function to find Y . Do this for each spot.\\
$\mathrm{m}=1 . \quad \mathrm{m} \lambda=\mathrm{d} \sin \theta \quad(1)\left(7 \times 10^{-7}\right)=\left(1 \times 10^{-5}\right) \sin \theta \quad \theta=4.01^{\circ} \ldots \tan \theta=\mathrm{o} / \mathrm{a} \ldots \mathrm{Y}_{1}=0.14 \mathrm{~m}$\\
Repeat for $\mathrm{m}=3 \ldots \mathrm{Y}_{3}=0.43 \mathrm{~m}$. Subtract $\mathrm{Y}_{3}-\mathrm{Y}_{1}$ to find the distance between $=0.29 \mathrm{~m}$\\
Note: Since the angle $\theta$ here actually came out to be small, the $x / L$ small angle approximation could be used and the spacing $x$ between spots could be assumed to be equal as well, so you could simply find $x$ for the first spot and double it to find the spacing 1 to 3.
  \item From $\mathrm{m} \lambda=\mathrm{dx} / \mathrm{L}, \mathrm{d} \mathrm{x} 2$ needs L x 2 also.
  \item By definition, when the path difference equals $1 / 2 \lambda$ or any odd multiple of $1 / 2 \lambda$ 's for sources of the same $\lambda$, there will be destructive interference.
  \item Based on $m \lambda=d x / L$ we want to increase $x$. $d$ is separation of slits and less $d$ means more $x$
  \item Since the slits are narrow, we can use $\mathrm{m} \lambda=\mathrm{d} \sin \theta$, but since $\theta$ is clearly large we cannot use the $\mathrm{x} / \mathrm{L}$ small angle approximation. From the given diagram, the geometry shows $\sin \theta=\mathrm{o} / \mathrm{h}=3 / 5$.. rather than finding $\theta$, we will just use this value for $\sin \theta$ and plug in ... $\mathrm{m} \lambda=\mathrm{d} \sin \theta \quad \ldots \quad(1)(0.12)=\mathrm{d}(3 / 5)$
  \item This is still a double slit pattern because there is still light making it through both slits. One of the light sources has reduced its amplitude; which means when it meets the second light source it will cause less interference than it originally did. This means less constructive interference and less destructive interference also. So bright spots become less bright, and dark spots become brighter.
\end{enumerate}

For an oil thickness of 100 nm (as given), these correspond to wavelength in the oil of $400 \mathrm{~nm}, 133.33 \mathrm{~nm}, 80 \mathrm{~nm} \ldots$

Since the visible spectrum in air ranges between $400-700 \mathrm{~nm}$, only the $600 \mathrm{~nm} \lambda$ qualifies.

b) i) $\quad \mathrm{c}=\mathrm{f} \lambda \quad \ldots \quad 3 \times 10^{8}=\mathrm{f}\left(5.25 \times 10^{-7}\right) \quad \ldots \quad \mathrm{f}=5.71 \times 10^{14} \mathrm{~Hz}$.\\
ii) frequency does not change when it enters the film, same as air ... $\mathrm{f}=5.71 \times 10^{14} \mathrm{~Hz}$\\
iii) $\mathrm{n}_{1} \lambda_{1}=\mathrm{n}_{2} \lambda_{2} \quad \ldots \quad \mathrm{n}_{\text {air }} \lambda_{\text {air }}=\mathrm{n}_{\text {film }} \lambda_{\text {film }} \quad \ldots \quad(1)(525)=(1.38) \lambda_{\text {film }} \quad \ldots \quad \lambda_{\text {film }}=380 \mathrm{~nm}$.\\
iv) To see the green reflection, you would need to have constructive interference from the film. As the light travels from the air to film to glass, it undergoes a $1 / 2 \lambda$ phase shift at each boundary. These two phase shifts essentially cancel each other out to get zero phase shift from the flips. To produce constructive interference, a total of one wavelength needs to be covered from traveling in the film. This requires a film thickness of $1 / 2$ of the wavelength in the film. $\mathrm{t}=1 / 2 \lambda_{\text {film }}=1 / 2(380)=190 \mathrm{~nm}$.

\section*{Supplemental.}
a) i. $\mathrm{c}=\mathrm{f} \lambda \quad \ldots \quad 3 \times 10^{8}=\mathrm{f}(520 \mathrm{~nm}) \quad \ldots \quad \mathrm{f}=5.77 \times 10^{14} \mathrm{~Hz}$.\\
ii. frequency in film is the same as in the air, it doesn't change between mediums $=5.77 \times 10^{14} \mathrm{~Hz}$\\
iii. $\mathrm{n}_{1} \lambda_{1}=\mathrm{n}_{2} \lambda_{2} \quad \ldots \quad \mathrm{n}_{\text {air }} \lambda_{\text {air }}=\mathrm{n}_{\text {film }} \lambda_{\text {film }} \quad \ldots \quad(1)(520 \mathrm{~nm})=(1.4) \lambda_{\text {oil }} \quad \ldots \quad \lambda_{\text {oil }}=371 \mathrm{~nm}$\\
b) There will be $1 / 2 \lambda$ phase shifts at each boundary essentially canceling out each 'flip'. To make constructive interference, a total of $1 \lambda$ of phase shift is needed from traveling in the film and this requires the thickness of the film to be $1 / 2 \lambda_{\text {film }}=1 / 2(371)=186 \mathrm{~nm}$\\
c) As the angle changes, the effective thickness of the film changes as well since the rays travel at angles in the film. The white light source has all frequency colors in it, and different thicknesses traveled will cause different constructive interference with different wavelength colors of light. (This is why a soap bubble has many colors reflected in it. The variation in thickness along the bubble makes different wavelength constructive in different regions)

b) Looking at the intensity pattern, P is the second point of zero intensity which means it's the $2^{\text {nd }}$ dark spot ( $\mathrm{m}=1.5$ ). Using Path Diff $=\mathrm{m} \lambda \ldots \mathrm{m} \lambda=\mathrm{dx} / \mathrm{L} \ldots$ path diff $=\left(2 \times 10^{-3}\right)\left(1.8 \times 10^{-3}\right) / 5 \ldots$ path diff $=7.2 \times 10^{-7} \mathrm{~m}$\\
c) Path Diff $=\mathrm{m} \lambda \quad \ldots \quad 7.2 \times 10^{-7}=1.5 \lambda \quad \ldots \quad \lambda=4.8 \times 10^{-7} \mathrm{~m}$\\
d) ii) Covering a slit makes this a single slit pattern. In the single slit, $\mathrm{m}=1$ becomes the first dark region (the end of the central bright spot) instead of $\mathrm{m}=0.5$ being the end of the central bright spot. All of the other integer m's also become dark spot locations. The effect of this is to make the central max wider and widen the pattern. Additionally, in single slit diffraction, the intensities generally lose intensity more rapidly when moving away from the center.\\
iii) Increasing d . Based on $\mathrm{m} \lambda=\mathrm{dx} / \mathrm{L}$, this would make x less so would compress the pattern.

Now find $\mathrm{Y} \ldots \tan \theta=\mathrm{o} / \mathrm{a} \ldots \tan (5.25)=\mathrm{Y} / 5 \quad \ldots \mathrm{Y}=0.459 \mathrm{~m}$\\

  \item Correct statements about the binding energy of a nucleus include which of the following?\\
I. It is the energy needed to separate the nucleus into its individual protons and neutrons.\\
II. It is the energy liberated when the nucleus is formed from the original nucleons.\\
III. It is the energy equivalent of the apparent loss of mass of its nucleon constituents.\\
A) I only\\
B) III only\\
C) I and II only\\
D) I, II, and III
\end{enumerate}

1989B6. A lithium nucleus, while at rest, decays into a helium nucleus of rest mass $6.6483 \times 10^{-27}$ kilogram and a proton of rest mass $1.6726 \times 10^{-27}$ kilogram, as shown by the following reaction.

$$

$$

(a) Determine the kinetic energy of the electron.\\
(b) Determine the de Broglie wavelength of the electron.

The electron encounters a particle with the same mass and opposite charge (a positron) moving with the same speed in the opposite direction. The two particles undergo a head-on collision, which results in the disappearance of both particles and the production of two photons of the same energy.\\
(c) Determine the energy of each photon.\\
(d) Determine the wavelength of each photon.\\
(e) Explain why there must be two photons produced instead of just one.

B2007B7. In the vicinity of a heavy nucleus, a high-energy photon can be converted into two particles: an electron and a positron. A positron has the same mass as the electron and equal but opposite charge. This process is called pair production.\\
(a) Calculate the rest energy of an electron, in eV .\\
(b) Determine the minimum energy, in eV , that a photon must have to give rise to pair production.\\
(c) Calculate the wavelength corresponding to the photon energy found in part (b).\\
(d) Calculate the momentum of the photon.

2007B7. It is possible for an electron and a positron to orbit around their stationary center of mass until they annihilate each other, creating two photons of equal energy moving in opposite directions. A positron is a particle that has the same mass as an electron and equal but opposite charge. The amount of kinetic energy of the electronpositron pair before annihilation is negligible compared to the energy of the photons created.\\
(a) Calculate, in eV , the rest energy of a positron.\\
(b) Determine, in eV , the energy each emitted photon must have.\\
(c) Calculate the wavelength of each created photon.\\
(d) Calculate the magnitude of the momentum of each photon.\\
(e) Determine the total momentum of the two-photon system.

\section*{SECTION A - Quantum Physics and Atom Models}
\section*{Solution}
\begin{enumerate}
  \item The de Broglie wavelength is given by $p=h / \lambda$. Since $p=m v, \lambda=h / m v$. Therefore, $\lambda_{\text {new }}=$ $\mathrm{h} /[(3 \mathrm{~m})(2 \mathrm{v})]=\lambda / 6$.
  \item From $\mathrm{K}=\mathrm{hf}-\phi \quad \ldots \quad \mathrm{y}=\mathrm{mx}+\mathrm{b} \quad \ldots$ the work function is the y intercept, extend the line.
  \item $4-3,3-2,2-1 \ldots$ or $4-2,2-1 \ldots$ or $4-3,3-1 \ldots$ or $4-1 \ldots$ this is a total of 6 different transitions.
  \item Stopping potential is given by. $\mathrm{K}=\mathrm{V}_{\text {stop }} \mathrm{q} \ldots$ combined with $\mathrm{K}=\mathrm{hf}-\phi$, we see the stopping potential is related to the incoming light energy minus the work function. However, none of the choices give a proper result. The answer depends on what that actual incoming energy hf and work function are. Here is an example. Lets say hf was 3 eV and the $\phi$ was 2 eV initially. From $\mathrm{Vq}=\mathrm{hf}-\phi \ldots$ the stopping potential for an electron (1e) would be equal to 1 eV . Now if we were to half the work function, the new stopping potential would be $3 \mathrm{eV}-1 \mathrm{eV}=2 \mathrm{eV}$ so it appears that the stopping potential doubled. But that result only works for those sample numbers. Lets assume instead that $\mathrm{hf}=10 \mathrm{eV}$ and $\phi=2 \mathrm{eV}$. Now initially the $\mathrm{V}_{\text {stop }}=8 \mathrm{eV}$. Then we again half the work function ... $10 \mathrm{eV}-1 \mathrm{eV}$ and we get a stopping potential of 9 V .. not nearly doubled this time. The results depend on the actual numbers used because of the minus sign in the equation and not a simple multiplier effect.
  \item To transition to the $-12 e V$ state with only two photon emissions, the only options are for the electron to make the following transitions: $-1 \mathrm{eV} \rightarrow-3 \mathrm{eV} \rightarrow-12 \mathrm{eV}$ giving us photons of energy $2 e V$ and $9 e V$ or $-1 e V \rightarrow-7 e V \rightarrow-12 e V$ giving photons of energy $6 e V$ and $5 e V$. This means that the 4 eV photon is not possible with only two transitions.
  \item $\mathrm{K}=\mathrm{hf}-\phi \ldots$ now double $\mathrm{hf} \ldots \mathrm{K}_{\text {new }}=2 \mathrm{hf}-\phi \ldots$\\
(now sub in the first equation rearranged for hf , into the second equation) ...
\end{enumerate}

$$
\mathrm{K}_{\text {new }}=2(\mathrm{~K}+\phi)-\phi=2 \mathrm{~K}+2 \phi-\phi \quad \ldots \quad \mathrm{K}_{\text {new }}=2 \mathrm{~K}+\phi
$$

So the new energy is increased by double the old energy + the work function value.\\
7. From above $\lambda=\mathrm{h} / \mathrm{mv}$... Since $\mathrm{K}=1 / 2 \mathrm{mv}^{2}$, 2 xK means $\sqrt{ } 2 \mathrm{xv}$. So when we plug in the new velocity of $\sqrt{ } 2 v$, the wavelength decreases by this factor since we divide.\\
8. 3 to 1 would be a higher energy emission. More energy means more frequency, and less $\lambda$ but these are not choices. From $\mathrm{p}=\mathrm{h} / \lambda$, less $\lambda$ means more momentum.\\
9. Below a threshold frequency, there would be no emissions and thus zero K for everything below that point. Above that threshold, more frequency means more K based on $\mathrm{K}=\mathrm{hf}-\phi$, with h as the constant slope. Graph A has all these properties.\\
10. The following formulas apply. $\mathrm{K}=\mathrm{Vq} \ldots \mathrm{K}=1 / 2 \mathrm{mv}^{2} \ldots \mathrm{p}=\mathrm{mv} \ldots \mathrm{p}=\mathrm{h} / \lambda$ To get half the $\lambda$, the p must be doubled ...\\
To double the momentum, the velocity must be doubled ...\\
When the velocity is doubled, the Kinetic energy is 4 x as much ...\\
To get 4 x the K , we need 4 x the potential.\\
11. The ground state is at -14 eV . The next excited state is 4 eV higher (at -10 eV ) which cant be reached since we are only putting in a range of $7-10$. So we try the next jump to the -5 eV state. This would require and input of 9 eV and this is possible since it falls in the range. The next state -3 eV is not possible since it would require 11 eV input. So the only excited state we can be brought to with this energy input is the -5 eV state. From this state we will now go through\\
emissions as the electrons fall back down to the ground state. This can be done through three possible jumps:\\
$-5 \mathrm{eV} \rightarrow-10 \mathrm{eV}$, then $-10 \mathrm{eV} \rightarrow-14 \mathrm{eV}$ or it could go directly from $-5 \mathrm{eV} \rightarrow-14 \mathrm{eV}$.\\
In these three scenarios, the emissions possible are 5, 4 and 9 .\\
12. Big $\lambda$ means the least energy based on $\mathrm{E}=\mathrm{hc} / \lambda$. The least energy corresponds to the smallest energy level jump which is 4-3.\\
13. The quantization of energy levels is from de Broglie and the relationship of momentum to wavelength through matter-waves. de Broglie theorized that electrons have wavelike properties and must exist in whole number multiples of wavelengths around an orbit to so they interfere constructively and do not get knocked out.\\
14. From $p=h / \lambda$, and $c=f \lambda \ldots \quad p=h f / c$. There is a direct relationship between $p$ and $f$.\\
15. The K of each photoelectron is given by. $\mathrm{K}=\mathrm{hf}-\phi$. To reduce the energy of each photon, we need less $f$ (which means more $\lambda$ ) for the incoming light. Since intensity is directly related to the number of photoelectrons emitted we want to increase the intensity.\\
16. The photoelectric current is directly related to the number of photoelectrons emitted; the more photoelectrons the more the current. Also, the \# of photoelectrons is directly related to the intensity. This means that photoelectric current and intensity also have a direct relationship. When we are above the threshold frequency, 0 intensity would correspond to 0 current, but as intensity increases, the current increases proportionally.\\
17. We find the total energy produced in 1 second and then use the energy of 1 photon to determine how many photons would be emitted.\\
Total energy $=\mathrm{W}=\mathrm{Pt}=50000(1 \mathrm{sec})=50000 \mathrm{~J}=5 \times 10^{4}$\\
Energy of 1 photon $\mathrm{E}=\mathrm{hc} / \lambda=2 \times 10^{-25} / 4=0.5 \times 10^{-25}=5 \times 10^{-26}$\\
Total Energy / Energy of 1 photon $=\#$ photons released.\\
$5 \times 10^{4} / 5 \times 10^{-26}=10^{30}$\\

19. The energy of the electrons is the kinetic energy given by $\mathrm{W}=\mathrm{Vq}=\mathrm{K}$. Doubling the voltage energy per photon. Intensity is the total energy of the beam. To have the same intensity, there would need to be more of the lower energy red photons. doubles the energy of the electrons. The emitted x -ray energy coming from the electron energy is given by $\mathrm{E}=\mathrm{hf}$ and with double the energy there should be double the frequency.

\section*{SECTION B - Nuclear Physics}
\section*{Solution}
\begin{enumerate}
  \item For a positron to be emitted, the oxygen must have undergone beta+ decay, which is the opposite of beta $^{-}$decay. In beta+ decay a proton turns into a neutron + the emitted beta particle. The mass number stays the same ( $\mathrm{P}+\mathrm{N}$ still the same), but the atomic number goes down by 1 since there is 1 less proton.
  \item An alpha particle is ${ }^{4} \mathrm{He}_{2}$ so reduce the atomic mass by 4 and the atomic number by 2 .
  \item In beta ${ }^{-}$decay, a neutron turns into a proton. Atomic mass same, mass number +1 .
  \item Equate $\mathrm{E}=\mathrm{hf}$ to $\mathrm{E}=\mathrm{mc}^{2} \ldots \mathrm{~m}=\mathrm{hf} / \mathrm{c}^{2}$.
  \item For everything to add up properly, we need $\ldots \frac{1}{0}$ which is a neutron.
  \item Same as above.
  \item Uranium split by a neutron is called fission.
  \item Merging together two elements (He usually being one of them) is called fusion.
  \item The mass number has changed by 28 , the atomic number has changed by 10 . 7 alpha particles $\left({ }^{4} \mathrm{He}_{2}\right) * 7$ equates to a loss of 28 for atomic mass and 14 for atomic number. If only the alpha particles were emitted, 4 protons would be missing. Those protons must have come from the conversion of neutrons into protons which would happen with the release of 4 beta particles.
  \item To balance the nuclear reaction, the sum of the values across the "top" and across the "bottom" must match... That is, we have $4+9=12+A \rightarrow A=1$ and $2+4=6+Z \rightarrow Z=0$. This gives us a particle with 1 nucleon, but 0 protons. This is a neutron.
  \item Conservation of charge is required. Eliminating an electron by itself violates this. However, when and electron ( - ) meets a positron ( + ) the matter and antimatter can annihilate to produce energy and the + and - charges can neutralize to conserve charge .. just as a side note.
  \item This is the similar to problem 12. Beta particles do not change the atomic mass number since there is simply a conversion between nucleons, so the only way to reduce the mass number is by emitting alpha particles. The mass number goes down by 32 and each alpha particle reduces it by 4 so 8 alpha particles are needed. 8 alpha particles by themselves would also reduce the atomic $\#$ by 16 , but it only ends up reduced by 10 so there are 6 protons needed. These 6 protons come from the beta decay where 6 neutrons turn into protons and release 6 beta particles.
  \item For everything to add up properly, we need $\ldots \frac{1}{1}$ which is a proton.
  \item First in the alpha decay, the atomic mass goes down by 4 and the atomic number goes down by 2 leaving ${ }^{214} \mathrm{X}_{82} \ldots$ then in the two beta decays, a neutron turns into a proton each time increasing the atomic number by two leaving... ${ }^{214} \mathrm{X}_{84}$.
  \item Simply make sure everything adds up to get the missing piece.
  \item $\quad{ }^{214} \mathrm{~Pb}_{82} \rightarrow \mathrm{X}+{ }^{0} \mathrm{e}_{-1} \quad \ldots \quad$ For everything to add up, we need ${ }^{214} \mathrm{X}_{83}$
  \item In a nuclear reaction, the total mass before must be larger than the total mass after since some of the mass will be 'missing' afterwards (mass defect) in the form of released energy.
  \item In this reaction, two light elements are fusing together and producing a heavier element. This is fusion.
  \item The reaction is as follows ${ }^{1} \mathrm{p}_{1}+{ }^{14} \mathrm{~N}_{7} \rightarrow{ }^{11} \mathrm{C}_{6}+\mathrm{X} \ldots$ to make it all add up X must be an alpha.
  \item We start with 2 neutrons and 1 proton. In beta decay with the emission of an electron, the process involves a neutron turning into a proton. The resulting nucleus would have 1 neutron and 2 protons. An atomic number of 2 is defined as He . It is ${ }^{3} \mathrm{He}_{2}$ which is an isotope of ${ }^{4} \mathrm{He}_{2}$.
  \item A beta particle, like all matter, can exhibit wave properties. Since the particle is so small, it can more readily show these wave properties than normal size matter.
  \item Using $\mathrm{E}=\mathrm{mc}^{2}$ with twice the mass since two particles are destroyed $=\left(2^{*} 9.1 \times 10^{-31}\right)\left(3 \times 10^{8}\right)^{2}=1.64 \times 10^{-13} \mathrm{~J} \quad \ldots \quad 2.63 \times 10^{-13} \mathrm{~J} *\left(1 \mathrm{eV} / 1.6 \times 10^{-19} \mathrm{~J}\right)=1.02 \times 10^{6} \mathrm{eV}$. This is the total energy released, and since there are two photons we split it in half.
  \item To conserve momentum, the photons must move in opposite directions.
  \item For everything to add up properly, 3 neutrons are needed.
  \item I. is Not True, for the following reason:
\end{enumerate}

In fission, and U-235 nucleus is broken into fragments that make smaller elements + neutrons + energy. The fragments created are not always the same and there is a statistical probability of which fragments can be created. The reaction provided in this problem is the most probable but other elements can be formed such as the following U-235 fission reaction: $\mathrm{U}-235+\mathrm{n} \rightarrow \mathrm{Zr}-94+\mathrm{Te}-139+3 \mathrm{n}+$ energy. There are actually many combinations of fragments that can be released. Small amounts of mass are missing as released energy but adding the whole numbers of the reaction will always balance the equation for a given reaction.\\
II. is TRUE. As explained above, as small amount of the mass will be missing in the form of energy after the reaction completes. This is necessary to produce the energy from the reaction.\\
III. is Not True. Again as explained in the first paragraph. There will be a small amount of mass missing but adding the whole numbers before and after will always result in the same numbers of particles for a fission reaction.\\
26. $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm{g}}=\mathrm{Gmm} / \mathrm{r}^{2} \ldots \mathrm{~F}_{\mathrm{e}}=\mathrm{kqq} / \mathrm{r}^{2} \ldots$ The electric and gravitational forces are inverse squared as shown from the equations here. Nuclear is not. This is fact and we don't know why. It was one of Einstein's last puzzles and he considered it a great failure of his to not solve this. It is called grand unification theory that attempts to combine all of the four fundamental forces into one unified force. It is a hot topic in modern physics that is as of yet unsolved.\\
27. Some reactions conserve all of these, others do not. Clearly the numbers of protons is not conserved as evidenced by beta decay. The "number" of nuclei is more often conserved but in some reactions such as annihilation the nuclei are disintegrated and converted into energy. This agrees with the law of conservation of matter and energy, but when looking at the total numbers of particles before and the total numbers of particles after, you would say that number is not conserved. Charge is a fundamental conservation law and it always conserved. Even in the annihilation example, the net charge before was zero and is zero after.\\
28. $\quad{ }^{214} \mathrm{~Pb}_{82} \rightarrow{ }^{0} \mathrm{~B}_{-1}+\gamma+$ ? ... For everything to add up, the missing product is ${ }^{214} \mathrm{X}_{83}$\\
29. This is a mass defect question. The energy released in the reaction is equal the equivalence of the missing mass comparing the products and reactants.\\
30. For everything to add up we need a helium nucleus (alpha particle).\\
31. These are all true statements about binding energy.

b) $p=h / \lambda \quad \ldots c=f \lambda \quad \ldots \quad p=h f / c \quad \ldots \quad p=3.73 \times 10^{-23} \mathrm{~kg}-\mathrm{m} / \mathrm{s}$\\
c) From energy conservation, the total energy before must equal the energy after

$$
\begin{gathered}
E_{\text {photon(i) }}=E_{\text {photon(f) }}+E_{\text {electron }} \ldots h_{i}=h_{f}+K_{e} \ldots\left(6.63 \times 10^{-34}\right)\left(1.69 \times 10^{19}\right)=\left(6.63 \times 10^{-34}\right)\left(1.64 \times 10^{19}\right)+K_{e} \\
K_{e}=3.31 \times 10^{-16} \mathrm{~J}
\end{gathered}
$$

d) The energy of the recoiled electron can help us find its momentum. Using $\mathrm{K}=1 / 2 \mathrm{mv}^{2}$

$$
\begin{aligned}
& 3.31 \times 10^{-16} \mathrm{~J}=1 / 29.11 \times 10^{-31} \mathrm{v}^{2} \ldots \mathrm{v}=2.7 \times 10^{7} \mathrm{~m} / \mathrm{s} \\
& \mathrm{p}=\left(9.11 \times 10^{-31}\right)\left(2.7 \times 10^{7}\right)=2.46 \times 10^{-23} \mathrm{~kg}-\mathrm{m} / \mathrm{s}
\end{aligned}
$$

e) $\mathrm{p}=\mathrm{h} / \lambda \quad \ldots 2.46 \times 10^{-23}=\left(6.6 \times 10^{-34}\right) / \lambda \ldots \lambda=2.7 \times 10^{-11} \mathrm{~m}$

1995B4.\\
This question is a slightly different energy level question. Rather than absorbing light photons of energy to excite electrons in the atom, this atom captures a free electron. This electron is captured from outside the atom and enters from the highly excited $\mathrm{E}=0$ state, and will have the full ionization energy of 13.6 eV . It will then undergo transitions to lower energy level states giving off its energy by emission of light photons from the atom as described in the problem.\\
a) $\mathrm{E}=\mathrm{hc} / \lambda \ldots 10.2=1240 / \lambda \ldots \lambda=122 \mathrm{~nm}$\\
b) i. There were two total energy emissions described in the problem. The first is unknown and the second is 10.2 eV which must have been a due to a drop to the ground state from the -3.4 eV state based on the energy level diagram provided. Since the second drop went from $-3.4 \mathrm{eV} \rightarrow-13.6 \mathrm{eV} \ldots$ the first drop must have brought the electron to the -3.4 eV state and we know it started out at $\mathrm{E}=0$ since it was initially captured from outside the atom. This means the first photon energy emitted must have been 3.4 eV to get it into that state and make the second drop described. $\ldots \mathrm{E}_{\mathrm{a}}=3.4 \mathrm{eV}$\\
ii. $E=h f \quad \ldots \quad 3.4 \mathrm{eV}=\left(4.14 \times 10^{-15}\right) \mathrm{f} \quad \ldots \quad \mathrm{f}=8.2 \times 10^{14} \mathrm{~Hz}$.\\
c)\\
\includegraphics[max width=\textwidth, center]{2025_07_25_f7c6487536cde8c1d115g-378}\\
d) i. When more than the ionization energy is provided to an electron in a level, the electron will escape the atom and the excess energy above the ionization energy will be in the form of $\mathrm{KE} .15 \mathrm{eV}-13.6 \mathrm{eV}=1.4 \mathrm{eV}$.\\
ii. Using the energy of the electron determine its speed. $\mathrm{K}=1 / 2 \mathrm{mv}^{2} \ldots$\\
$\left(1.4 \mathrm{eV}^{*} 1.6 \times 10^{-19} \mathrm{~J} / \mathrm{eV}\right)=1 / 2\left(9.11 \times 10^{-31}\right) \mathrm{v}^{2} \ldots \quad \mathrm{v}=7 \times 10^{5} \mathrm{~m} / \mathrm{s}$\\
Then, $\mathrm{p}=\mathrm{h} / \lambda \ldots \mathrm{mv}=\mathrm{h} / \lambda \ldots\left(9.11 \times 10^{-31}\right)\left(7 \times 10^{5}\right)=\left(6.63 \times 10^{-34}\right) / \lambda \ldots \lambda=1 \times 10^{-9} \mathrm{~m}$

\section*{1998B7.}
a) Diffraction grating: Since the screen distance is 1 m and the first order line is at 0.428 m , the angle is not small and the small angle approximation cannot be used. Instead we find the angle with $\tan \theta$ and use $\mathrm{m} \lambda=\mathrm{d} \sin \theta$. First find $\mathrm{d} . \mathrm{d}=600$ lines $/ \mathrm{mm}=1 / 600 \mathrm{~mm} /$ line $=0.00167 \mathrm{~mm} /$ line $=1.67 \times 10^{-6} \mathrm{~m} /$ line .

$$
\begin{array}{rlllll}
\tan \theta=\mathrm{o} / \mathrm{a} \quad \ldots \quad \tan \theta=0.428 / 1 \quad \ldots \quad \theta=23^{\circ} \quad \ldots \text { Then, } \mathrm{m} \lambda=\mathrm{d} \sin \theta \quad \ldots(1) \lambda & =\left(1.67 \times 10^{-6}\right) \sin 23^{\circ} \\
\lambda & =6.57 \times 10^{-7} \mathrm{~m}=657 \mathrm{~nm}
\end{array}
$$

b) First find the energy of the red light emission. $\mathrm{E}=\mathrm{hc} / \lambda \ldots \mathrm{E}=1240 / 657=1.89 \mathrm{eV}$ emitted

In the problem, the $\mathrm{n}=2$ level energy state is given by $\mathrm{E}_{\mathrm{n}}=-13.6 / \mathrm{n}^{2}=\mathrm{E}_{2}=-13.6 / 2^{2}=-3.4 \mathrm{eV}$\\
Since the red light was an emission of 1.89 eV , this is the energy that was removed from the initial level to end up in the -3.4 eV level, $\rightarrow$ it must have originated in a higher energy level (less negative) which can be found by adding back that emitted energy $-3.4+1.89=-1.51$.\\
Plugging this back into the provided equation, $\mathrm{E}_{\mathrm{n}}=-13.6 / \mathrm{n}^{2}$

$$
-1.51=-13.6 / \mathrm{n}^{2} \quad \ldots \mathrm{n}^{2}=9, \quad \text { so } \mathrm{n}=3 \text { is the original level. }
$$

c) Referring to the calculation of d from part a .. $\mathrm{d}=1 / 800 \mathrm{~mm}$ / line which is a smaller d value. Less d means $\sin \theta$ must increase so the angle is larger and the location of the line would be further out.
